  Current: The order itself is civil: no conviction, no criminal record. Breaching it is the criminal offence. But an order can still touch employment involving security licences or working-with-children checks, which is why conditions deserve negotiation rather than resignation.
  Proposed: No. The order itself is civil, so it brings no conviction and no criminal record, although breaching it is a criminal offence. An order can still affect work that depends on a security licence or a working with children check, which is why its conditions are worth negotiating rather than simply accepting.
- [medium] voice: Someone in danger reads this section first, and it is a run of fragments ('In immediate danger', 'Otherwise:') with an odd idiom. It gives no confidential support line, although the separation checklist on the same site already names 1800RESPECT. The proposal keeps every step and reuses the checklist's published sentence word for word. The next sentence, on safety planning, stays as it is.
  Current: In immediate danger, call 000. Nothing in this article comes before that. Otherwise: document incidents with dates, keep messages and photographs, tell your GP, and get advice early. Bring us the picture and we will advise whether a police referral or a private application better suits, prepare your evidence, and appear with you.
  Proposed: If you are in immediate danger, call 000 before anything else in this article. Otherwise, write down each incident with its date, keep messages and photographs, tell your GP, and get advice early. Tell us what has happened and we will advise whether to go through the police or make a private application, prepare your evidence and appear with you at court. For confidential family violence counselling and support at any time, the national line 1800RESPECT, on 1800 737 732, can help you work out what to do next, including whether an intervention order is appropriate.
- [medium] voice: A three part slogan that repeats the lead. 'For protection and respondent alike' is ungrammatical, because 'protection' is not a person. Pair this with the heading change in the headings finding.
  Current: Intervention orders exist to make people safe quickly, and they do. But every condition has consequences that outlast the court date, for protection and respondent alike. Whichever side of an application you stand on, the pattern is identical: act in days, not weeks; put evidence before emotion; and get advice before the first mention, when the widest range of outcomes is still open.
  Proposed: Intervention orders exist to make people safe quickly, and they do, but every condition has consequences that last well beyond the court date, for the protected person and the respondent alike. Whichever side of an application you are on, the advice is the same: act within days, keep a record of what has happened, and take advice before the first mention, while the widest range of outcomes is still open.
- [medium] legal-query: faq.html says the Victorian changes requiring police and courts to consider whether the wrong person has been named as the respondent start no later than 10 November 2026, which is tomorrow. Nothing on this page is wrong today. But the respondent section, where a wrongly named person will land, says nothing about the change and does not link the FAQ answer that covers it. Once the accuracy routine confirms commencement in the authorised Family Violence Protection Act and updates the two FAQ answers, as the content backlog already asks, add a sentence on what the change requires. The link can go in now.
  Current: Take advice before the first mention, not after.
  Proposed: Take advice before the first mention, not after. If you believe you have been wrongly named as the respondent, our answer on that question explains what to do first. [Link 'our answer on that question' to /faq#q-what-if-i-have-been-wrongly-named-as-the-respondent-on-an-in. The sentence on the new requirement follows once commencement is confirmed.]

## insight-parenting-arrangements.html
Scores: voice 6, clarity 7, persuasion 7, answer first 8

Accurate and well linked. It is the only page of the six that names both published parenting fees and links the fees page, and it has a strong urgent call prompt after the section on children being kept or taken. Its voice is older than the August articles: a bullet list of verbless fragments, label colons and short imperatives read like clipped notes. The closing callout also promises help today, which goes further than the firm's confirmed reply within one business day.

- [medium] firm-facts: 'We can help you today' reads as a same day promise for every parenting matter. That goes beyond the three confirmed service promises (a free first call, a reply within one business day and after hours contact for urgent matters), and an enquiry sent on a Friday night would break it. The approved rail card wording does the same persuasive work.
  Current: Worried about time with your children, or need arrangements made enforceable? We can help you today. Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry.
  Proposed: Worried about time with your children, or need arrangements made enforceable? Your first call is free, and you speak with a lawyer. Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry.
- [medium] voice: The section answers its question with three bullets of verbless fragments, the most staccato passage on these six pages. 'Resolves half the arguments' is an invented figure. The content is a comparison that reads better as three short paragraphs. The proposal keeps every legal statement, including the rule on the most recent plan and decision on the papers.
  Current: Informal agreement. Flexible and free, but unenforceable. Fine for amicable ex-partners, fragile everywhere else. | Parenting plan. A written, signed, dated agreement covering living arrangements, time, changeovers, holidays, communication. Not enforceable as an order, but still useful: courts must consider the most recent plan where doing so is in the child's best interests, and the discipline of writing one resolves half the arguments before they start. | Consent orders. Your agreement, approved by the court and enforceable like any judgment. A hearing is not normally needed. The application is usually decided on the papers, although the court can ask for more information or list the matter if it has concerns. For most separated parents who want certainty, this is the destination.
  Proposed: There are three ways to do it. An informal agreement is flexible and costs nothing, but it cannot be enforced, so it suits former partners who get on well and is fragile everywhere else. | A parenting plan is a written agreement, signed and dated, covering where the children live, their time with each parent, changeovers, holidays and communication. It cannot be enforced as an order can, but it is still useful, because a court must consider the most recent plan where doing so is in the child's best interests, and writing one down settles many arguments before they start. | Consent orders are your agreement approved by the court, and they are enforceable like any other court order. A hearing is not normally needed, because the application is usually decided on the papers, although the court can ask for more information or list the matter if it has concerns. For most separated parents who want certainty, consent orders are where the agreement should end up.
- [medium] voice: The section opens with a fragment and then runs as labelled notes, so the answer reads like a slide rather than an explanation. The link sentence that follows, to the grandparents guide, stays.
  Current: Three recurring flashpoints. Relocation: moving a child far enough to disrupt their time with the other parent generally requires agreement or a court order. Moving first and litigating later starts you behind. New partners: courts care about the child's experience, not the parent's private life, unless a new relationship creates risk. Grandparents and other significant people can themselves seek orders to spend time with a child; the same best-interests lens applies.
  Proposed: These three issues come up again and again. Moving a child far enough to disrupt their time with the other parent generally needs agreement or a court order, and a parent who moves first and litigates later starts behind. With a new partner, a court's concern is the child's experience rather than the parent's private life, unless the new relationship creates a risk. Grandparents and other people significant to a child can themselves apply for orders to spend time with the child, and the same test of the child's best interests applies.
- [low] voice: Four short lines that either clip or need a second read: a five word slogan, a curt imperative in an FAQ answer, 'if safety is immediate', which says the opposite of what is meant, and a staged 'honest and simple' set-up before the point. The FAQ change must be mirrored in the FAQPage schema.
  Current: Mediation is not a hoop. Many parenting matters are resolved there in whole or in part, faster and at a fraction of the cost of litigation. | There are proper remedies for breached orders; use them. | If safety is immediate, call 000 first, then call us. | What remains is honest and simple: arrangements are built around this child's best interests, on these facts.
  Proposed: Mediation is worth taking seriously rather than treating as a box to tick, because many parenting matters are resolved there in whole or in part, faster and at a fraction of the cost of litigation. | If parenting orders are being breached, the answer is to use the remedies the law provides for that, not to stop paying. | If anyone is in immediate danger, call 000 first, then call us. | Arrangements are now built around this child's best interests, on these facts.
- [low] persuasion: 'Comparatively inexpensive' is vague when the page has a published figure. FAQ answers are the passages most often lifted into search results, so the fixed fee belongs here too. Mirror the change in the FAQPage schema.
  Current: Convert the agreement into consent orders. It is a paper application, comparatively inexpensive, and it turns goodwill into enforceable certainty while everyone is still on good terms.
  Proposed: Convert the agreement into consent orders, which turns goodwill into enforceable certainty while everyone is still on good terms. It is a paper application, and for agreed parenting arrangements our fixed fee is $3,025 including GST.
- [low] voice: The heading is a stock phrase the checker flags. 'The law is now built to reward exactly that' is a sweeping claim about the law that the article does not support. Keep the id. The paragraph's last sentence, 'Whatever stage you are at...', stays.
  Current: The bottom line | Children do best when arrangements are settled early, precisely and with as little combat as the facts allow, and the law is now built to reward exactly that. Put safety first, use mediation properly, convert agreement into consent orders, and save the courtroom for the matters that need a judge.
  Proposed: Heading: What should parents do first? | Text: Children do best when arrangements are settled early and precisely, with as little conflict as the facts allow. Put safety first, use mediation properly, turn any agreement into consent orders, and keep the courtroom for the matters that need a judge.

## insight-property-after-separation.html
Scores: voice 6, clarity 6, persuasion 7, answer first 7

A strong, answer first guide. The lead gives the four steps and both deadlines, the call prompt follows the time limits, and the consent orders fee is named and linked. It also carries the one legal inconsistency that matters on these pages: 'the tax and duty concessions are unavailable', in the myths and the FAQ, which the 29 Sep 2026 stamp duty correction in its own consent orders section contradicts. Beyond that: a gendered 'her redundancy', two vague valuation phrases, a fragment list in step three, rhetorical closing lines, label headings, and no link to the newer agreed settlement guide.

- [high] legal-query: On 29 Sep 2026 the body sentence on stamp duty was corrected against section 44(1) of the Duties Act 2000 (Vic), which requires no court order. A transfer under an informal deal is now described as harder to bring within the exemption, not excluded from it. The myth paragraph and the FAQ answer, which is also in the FAQPage schema, still say the duty concession is unavailable, so the page contradicts itself. The accuracy ledger notes that this page's full re-verification is still due. insight-agreed-property-settlement.html words the same point correctly. This is a statement of law, so its substance is not rewritten here.
  Current: Until formalised by consent orders or a binding financial agreement, nothing is final: the claim survives, the tax and duty concessions are unavailable, and future windfalls remain in play. | FAQ: In practical terms, yes: until an agreement is formalised by consent orders or a binding financial agreement, nothing is final. The claim survives, the tax and duty concessions are unavailable, and future windfalls remain in play.
  Proposed: For Spencer and the accuracy routine: align both sentences and the schema with the corrected consent orders paragraph and with the verified wording in insight-agreed-property-settlement.html. That wording says capital gains tax rollover relief needs a court order, a binding financial agreement or an arbitration award, and that the Victorian duty exemption depends on the Commissioner being satisfied the transfer was made because the relationship broke down, with orders or an agreement the clearest way to show it.
- [medium] clarity: 'Her redundancy' assumes the reader is a man whose former partner is a woman, which jars for every other reader, including same sex couples. The last sentence uses a participle phrase as its subject and makes an absolute claim.
  Current: But the deeper reason to settle promptly is that the pool is valued at settlement, not separation: your post-separation savings, her redundancy and the market's movement all stay in play while nothing is finalised. Separated for years without formalising anything is the riskiest financial position in family law.
  Proposed: But the deeper reason to settle promptly is that the pool is valued at settlement, not separation: your savings since separation, a redundancy payment your former partner receives and movements in the market all stay in play while nothing is finalised. Being separated for years with nothing formalised is one of the riskiest financial positions in family law.
- [medium] legal-query: 'Trusts and their realities' and 'valued at its real figure' cannot be understood on first reading. The second phrase is repeated in the superannuation FAQ answer and its schema. A reader cannot tell whether the point is that a trust someone controls may be counted, or that some super interests are valued by a set method rather than at the balance on the statement. Both are statements about the law, so their substance is not rewritten here.
  Current: ...the home, other real estate, bank accounts, shares, businesses, trusts and their realities, vehicles, tools, cryptocurrency, and every liability, from the mortgage to the credit cards to the tax debt. Superannuation counts, valued at its real figure.
  Proposed: For Spencer: say in plain words what each phrase means, read against the Act and the superannuation valuation rules, or drop the qualifiers, leaving 'trusts' and 'Superannuation counts too'.
- [medium] voice: The second sentence is a verbless list held together by semicolons, so it reads as notes. The same factors flow as one sentence with no change to what the court considers.
  Current: Contributions look backward; this step looks forward. Age and health; income and realistic earning capacity; who has the primary care of children, and the housing that care requires; access to resources such as family trusts or a new partner's support.
  Proposed: Contributions look back over the relationship, and this step looks forward, at each person's age and health, their income and realistic earning capacity, who has the primary care of the children and the housing that care requires, and their access to other resources, such as a family trust or a new partner's support.
- [medium] voice: After a stock heading the checker flags, the section uses an 'it is not X and not Y' set-up and two aphorisms ('futures unpriced', 'the fear leaves the process'). That is rhetorical framing, not the concrete advice the rest of the article gives. Keep the service page link on 'Property settlement' and keep the id.
  Current: The bottom line | Property settlement is not a coin-flip and not a war: it is a structured assessment you can prepare for. Know the pool, evidence your contributions, be honest about future needs on both sides, and formalise whatever you agree. The couples who fare worst are not the ones who fight; they are the ones who drift, leaving claims open and futures unpriced. Get the picture early, and most of the fear leaves the process.
  Proposed: Heading: What should you do first? | Text: Property settlement is a structured assessment you can prepare for. Know what is in the pool, gather the evidence of what each of you contributed, be honest about what each of you will need, and formalise whatever you agree. The people who fare worst are usually not the ones who argue but the ones who let things drift, leaving the claim open while the assets and debts keep changing. A clear picture early takes much of the fear out of the process.
- [low] headings: The step headings are labels, so a reader scanning the contents list or a search result sees no question being answered. The numbering can stay while each heading becomes the question it answers. Keep the ids and update the In this article list.
  Current: First: everything goes in the pool | Second: contributions of money and everything else | Third: future needs | Fourth: is the result just and equitable? | Three persistent myths, corrected
  Proposed: Step one: what goes into the property pool? | Step two: how are contributions weighed? | Step three: how do future needs change the split? | Step four: is the result just and equitable? | What do people most often get wrong about property settlement?
- [low] persuasion: 'Start at our family law page' is a weak close to the section where a reader who has already agreed is deciding whether to call. The page never links insight-agreed-property-settlement.html, the hire guide written for exactly that reader, which links back here.
  Current: Where a matter must be litigated, we prepare it thoroughly and fight it firmly, but the destination for most families is a signed, sealed settlement and a clean start. Start at our family law page.
  Proposed: Where a matter must be litigated, we prepare it thoroughly and fight it firmly, but the destination for most families is a signed, sealed settlement and a clean start. If you have already agreed, our guide for couples in that position explains what a lawyer does with the agreement you have reached, and our family law page explains how we work. [Link 'our guide for couples in that position' to /insight-agreed-property-settlement.]
- [low] persuasion: Two stock idioms, 'close the loop' and 'the next chapter', in a sentence that could name the published prices for exactly the documents it recommends. Both prices match fees.html. Keep the existing links on 'will' and 'super nominations'.
  Current: And once the settlement is done, close the loop: update your will, your powers of attorney and your super nominations, so the next chapter starts with documents that match it.
  Proposed: Once the settlement is done, update your will, your powers of attorney and your super nominations, so that your documents match your new circumstances. A single will is a fixed fee of $495, and an estate plan for one person, with a will, an enduring power of attorney and an appointment of a medical treatment decision maker, is $1,089, both including GST.
- [low] voice: A cliché in the myths section, and a clumsy double 'and' in the FAQ answer, which is also in the FAQPage schema.
  Current: Conduct moves the needle only through its economic consequences | FAQ: No. That is a myth, and it is not the law, and never has been.
  Proposed: Conduct affects the outcome only through its economic consequences | No. An automatic equal split is not the law and never has been.

## insight-property-reforms-2025.html
Scores: voice 5, clarity 7, persuasion 6, answer first 7

Useful as the update for readers holding pre-2025 advice, with a well aimed call prompt after the time limits. But it is the most rhetorical of the six: 'the arithmetic of good sense', 'the statute now speaks your language', 'a statutory home' and 'futures narrowed by violence' read as speechwriting, not Spencer's plain register. Its most important section opens with a fragment, it makes one unverifiable claim about mediation, it uses a dated phrase that will look stale in 2027, it names no fee, and it repeats much of the property guide.

- [medium] answer-first: The answer to the page's most important question opens with a three word fragment instead of the answer. The rule should come first and its significance after it.
  Current: The most significant change. Courts must now consider the effect of family violence on each person's ability to contribute and on the current and future circumstances of the person subjected to it, recognising that violence can make a party's contributions harder to make and their financial future weaker.
  Proposed: Courts must now consider the effect of family violence on each person's ability to contribute and on the current and future circumstances of the person subjected to it, which makes this the most significant change in the reforms. It recognises that violence can make a party's contributions harder to make and their financial future weaker.
- [medium] voice: A filler fragment opens the section and an aphorism closes it. 'Not the arithmetic' also sits oddly with the page's own point that family violence can now move the percentages.
  Current: Perspective matters. There is still no automatic 50/50. ... The reforms changed the reasoning, not the arithmetic of good sense.
  Proposed: There is still no automatic 50/50 split. ... The reforms changed how the court reasons, and left these basics as they were.
- [medium] stale-wording: 'Speaks your language' is a cliché. 'Courts through 2025 and 2026' ties the sentence to two years, so it will read as stale from January 2027 even if nobody touches the page.
  Current: If you carried the parenting and the mortgage while your former partner ran up debts, the statute now speaks your language. ... Courts through 2025 and 2026 are still working the new provisions through case by case, which makes current advice more valuable, not less.
  Proposed: If you carried the parenting and the mortgage while your former partner ran up debts, the Act now speaks to your situation directly. ... The courts are still working out how the new provisions apply, case by case, which makes current advice more valuable, not less.
- [low] unsupported-claim: 'It has already reshaped how these conversations run in mediation' is a claim about practice that nobody can verify. 'Outsized emotional significance' is the phrase the checker queried; the spelling flag is a false positive, but the phrase is overwritten all the same.
  Current: It is a small change with outsized emotional significance, and it has already reshaped how these conversations run in mediation.
  Proposed: It is a small change, but it matters a great deal to many families.
- [medium] voice: A stock heading, then the most overwritten paragraph on these six pages. The reader who most needs this page, someone holding old advice, gets imagery instead of a plain statement of what the Act now requires, and the free first call is not mentioned. Keep the id.
  Current: The bottom line | The 2025 reforms did not change who you were in the relationship; they changed how completely the law can see it. Contributions made under coercion, futures narrowed by violence, assets burned in spite: these now have a statutory home. If your settlement was negotiated on pre-2025 assumptions, or your former partner is quoting old law at you, an hour of current advice is the cheapest correction available.
  Proposed: Heading: What should you do if your advice predates the reforms? | Text: The 2025 reforms did not change what happened in your relationship, but they changed how fully the law takes it into account. The effect of family violence on what a person could contribute and on their future, and the deliberate or reckless waste of assets, are now matters the Act requires the court to consider. If your settlement was negotiated on pre-2025 assumptions, or your former partner is quoting the old law at you, current advice is the cheapest correction available, and your first call is free.
- [low] persuasion: The page links the property settlement service page but never names the published consent orders fee, which a reader who has already agreed needs to see.
  Current: We will tell you where you stand under the current law, what disclosure to seek, and the sensible path to a binding settlement, with costs set out in writing first.
  Proposed: We will tell you where you stand under the current law, what disclosure to seek, and the sensible path to a binding settlement, with costs set out in writing first. If you have already agreed, consent orders for an agreed property settlement are a fixed fee of $3,025 including GST, and our fees page sets out what that covers. [Link 'our fees page' to /fees#family-law.]
- [low] legal-query: Before the reforms, the approach was a line of Family Court decisions applying the Family Law Act, not a common law doctrine. Those decisions still inform how the new provisions are applied, so 'no longer depend' may overstate the change. This is a statement about the law, so it is not rewritten here.
  Current: Survivors no longer depend on a difficult common-law doctrine to have these realities counted.
  Proposed: For Spencer: confirm the characterisation. If he agrees, a neutral form would be 'Survivors no longer have to rely on case law alone to have these effects counted.'
- [low] voice: An informal register ('kids', 'a roof over'), a glib line in an FAQ answer that is also in the schema, and two sentences that add commentary rather than information.
  Current: Three further threads were drawn into the statute. ... which in practice strengthens the position of the primary carer seeking to keep a roof over the kids. | FAQ: With evidence, it moves percentages, not just sympathy. | The elevation signals how central honesty is to the new scheme.
  Proposed: The reforms wrote three further matters into the Act. ... which in practice strengthens the position of a primary carer who wants to keep a home for the children. | With evidence, it can change the percentages. | Delete 'The elevation signals how central honesty is to the new scheme.'

## insight-spousal-maintenance-victoria.html
Scores: voice 7, clarity 7, persuasion 5, answer first 7

Measured and flowing, second only to the grandparents article in matching Spencer's register, with clear distinctions from property settlement and child support. It is let down by structure and conversion. The mid article call prompt sits two sections after the deadlines rather than straight after them. The opening section repeats the lead, the test section starts with what the test is not, and one sentence on death and wills does not say whose will or why. The body never links the property guide, the property settlement page or the fees page, although it says most claims run alongside a settlement.

- [medium] call-prompt: The site rule puts the mid article prompt straight after the section that states a time limit. Here the deadlines are in 'Who can apply for spousal maintenance?', but the prompt sits two sections later, after 'What does the court weigh'. By then a reader close to a deadline has already read past the moment of urgency.
  Current: Not sure whether you could claim spousal maintenance, or whether a claim might be made against you? Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry.
  Proposed: Move the callout to directly after 'Who can apply for spousal maintenance?' and name the deadline: Separated or divorced, and unsure whether the twelve month or two year deadline is already running? Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry.
- [medium] persuasion: The page says maintenance usually runs alongside a property settlement, but the body never links the property guide, the property settlement service page or the fees page, which lists consent orders and binding financial agreements. The property guide appears only in the Related reading list at the foot. The close links the top of the hub rather than its child support and spousal maintenance section.
  Current: Spencer Alexander Lawyers advises both applicants and respondents on spousal maintenance claims across Melbourne and Victoria, usually alongside a property settlement or a binding financial agreement rather than as a standalone matter. ... Learn more about our family law practice.
  Proposed: Keep the first two sentences and link 'property settlement' to /service-property-settlement, then add: Our fees page lists the fixed fees for consent orders and binding financial agreements, and anything outside them is estimated in writing before substantive work begins. Our guide to how property is divided after separation explains the settlement that usually runs alongside a maintenance claim. [Link the guide to /insight-property-after-separation and point 'family law practice' at /family-law#svc-child-support-and-spousal-maintenance.]
- [medium] answer-first: The section answers 'What test does the court apply?' by first saying what the test is not. The two questions end in full stops, so on a first read they look like statements.
  Current: There is no formula and no calculator, unlike child support. The court asks two connected questions. First, can the applicant meet their own reasonable needs from their own income, earning capacity and assets. Second, does the other party have the capacity to meet those needs, after allowing for their own reasonable needs and financial commitments. Maintenance is only available where the answer to the first question is no and the answer to the second is yes.
  Proposed: The court asks two connected questions. First, can the applicant meet their own reasonable needs from their own income, earning capacity and assets? Second, does the other party have the capacity to meet those needs, after allowing for their own reasonable needs and financial commitments? Maintenance is only available where the answer to the first question is no and the answer to the second is yes. Unlike child support, there is no formula and no calculator.
- [medium] legal-query: The sentence does not say whose will it means or how a will helps. A recipient's own will does nothing about the payer's death, and the payer's will is outside the recipient's control, so the reader cannot act on the advice. This is a statement about the law, so it is not rewritten here.
  Current: An existing maintenance order also stops if either party dies, and it cannot generally be pursued against the paying party's estate afterwards, which is a good reason to also have a will and, where appropriate, a binding financial agreement that anticipates that risk.
  Proposed: For Spencer: say whose document protects whom, for example a lump sum or a provision in the payer's will or agreement. Also consider whether to mention that a former spouse may in some circumstances be able to make a family provision claim in Victoria, once that is read in the Administration and Probate Act 1958 (Vic).
- [low] voice: The first sentence repeats the lead almost word for word, and 'which handles family law matters for Victorians' is padding. A reader who has just read the lead wants the next fact.
  Current: Spousal maintenance is financial support that one party to a marriage or de facto relationship pays to the other after separation, ordered under the Family Law Act 1975 (Cth), the same federal legislation that governs divorce, property settlement and parenting matters across Australia. In Victoria, an application is made to the Federal Circuit and Family Court of Australia, which handles family law matters for Victorians and whose website has the forms and guidance for a maintenance application.
  Proposed: Spousal maintenance is ordered under the Family Law Act 1975 (Cth), the same law that governs divorce, property settlement and parenting arrangements across Australia. In Victoria an application is made to the Federal Circuit and Family Court of Australia, whose website has the forms and guidance.
- [low] clarity: Consent orders are court orders, so 'the same force as a court order' is circular and leaves the reader wondering what the difference is. The intended meaning is that the agreement becomes an order without a contested hearing.
  Current: Consent orders reached as part of a broader property settlement can also record an agreed maintenance arrangement, giving it the same force as a court order without a contested hearing.
  Proposed: Consent orders made as part of a broader property settlement can also record an agreed maintenance arrangement, turning it into an order of the court without a contested hearing.
- [low] persuasion: 'One conversation clarifies where you stand' overpromises for a discretionary claim that the article itself says can go either way, and it misses the firm's strongest true offer, the free first call.
  Current: Whether you might be entitled to spousal maintenance, or a claim has been raised against you, one conversation clarifies where you stand. Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry.
  Proposed: Whether you might be entitled to spousal maintenance or a claim has been raised against you, your first call is free, and you speak with a lawyer. Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry.
- [low] clarity: A 72 word first sentence in an FAQ answer, the passage most likely to be quoted on its own. Splitting it keeps every condition. Mirror the change in the FAQPage schema.
  Current: Yes, on largely the same needs and capacity test that applies to married couples, including same sex de facto couples, provided the relationship first qualifies under the Family Law Act, which generally means it lasted at least two years, there is a child of the relationship, it was registered under a State or Territory relationships register, or one partner made substantial contributions and serious injustice would result if no order were made. Once that threshold is met, the main practical difference is the time limit: generally two years from separation, rather than the twelve months that applies after divorce.
  Proposed: Yes, on largely the same needs and capacity test that applies to married couples, including same sex de facto couples. The relationship must first qualify under the Family Law Act, which generally means it lasted at least two years, there is a child of the relationship, it was registered under a State or Territory relationships register, or one partner made substantial contributions and serious injustice would result if no order were made. Once that threshold is met, the main practical difference is the time limit: generally two years from separation, rather than the twelve months that applies after divorce.

## insight-guardianship-administration-orders.html
Scores: voice 6, clarity 6, persuasion 6, answer first 7

Careful on the law, with a lead that answers the title in its first sentence and a well aimed call prompt after the first steps section, but the middle reads as a statute summary rather than advice: a 71 word sentence of VCAT's conditions, an undefined "EPOA", two bare "Read our guide" sentences, and a closing section that never names the $495 fixed fee for the one document that avoids this process. One sentence says families are left with no lawful decision-maker, which is wider than what the site's advance care directives guide says about medical decisions.

- [medium] legal-query: Wider than the law as the site states it elsewhere. insight-advance-care-directives-victoria.html says that if nobody is appointed, 'Victorian law still produces a decision maker' for medical treatment, from a statutory order of priority, so a family reading this page may think it needs VCAT for medical decisions too. The closing clause also turns the point into a saying. Not rewriting the law: for Spencer to confirm. The guardian bullet and the 'what families should do first' list both mention health care and would need to stay consistent with his answer.
  Current: Families who did not get to this step in time are left with no lawful decision-maker, and that gap is exactly what guardianship and administration orders are designed to fill.
  Proposed: Families who did not get to this step in time have no one with authority over the person's money, property or living arrangements, and that is what a guardianship or administration order provides. Medical treatment decisions are different, because the law identifies a medical treatment decision maker even where nobody was appointed, as our guide to advance care directives explains.
- [medium] clarity: At 71 words, four statutory conditions strung together with 'that' need a second reading. Split into two sentences with the conditions and their wording unchanged.
  Current: VCAT will only make an order if it is satisfied, broadly, that the person has a disability that affects their decision-making, that because of that disability they do not have decision-making capacity for the matters in question, that they are in need of a guardian or administrator because the matters cannot be dealt with by informal or less restrictive means, and that the order will promote their personal and social wellbeing.
  Proposed: VCAT will only make an order if it is satisfied, broadly, that the person has a disability that affects their decision-making and that, because of that disability, they do not have decision-making capacity for the matters in question. It must also be satisfied that they are in need of a guardian or administrator, because those matters cannot be dealt with by informal or less restrictive means, and that the order will promote their personal and social wellbeing.
- [medium] clarity: The first sentence announces a 'significant' difference without saying what it is, which the next sentences then do. 'EPOA' appears once on the page and is never defined, so a worried reader has to guess what it means.
  Current: The practical difference between an enduring power of attorney signed in advance and a VCAT application made after capacity is lost is significant. An EPOA is a private document, prepared and signed on your own timetable, naming the person you trust.
  Proposed: An enduring power of attorney is a private document, prepared and signed on your own timetable, naming the person you trust.
- [medium] fees-and-links: This is where a reader decides to act, yet the page never names the published fixed fee for the one document that avoids VCAT: $495 including GST on fees.html. The paragraph also ends with a bare link sentence that does not say why to follow it, and 'which avoids this process altogether' is a tail commenting on the sentence. Link the price to /fees#fee-enduring-power-of-attorney and the document to /service-wills-and-estate-planning.
  Current: If capacity has not yet been lost, for example following an early dementia diagnosis, there may still be a window to put an enduring power of attorney and other estate planning documents in place, which avoids this process altogether. Read our guide to advance care directives in Victoria.
  Proposed: If capacity has not yet been lost, for example following an early dementia diagnosis, there may still be time to put an enduring power of attorney and other estate planning documents in place and avoid this process altogether. An enduring power of attorney is a fixed fee of $495 including GST, and our guide to advance care directives explains the separate document for medical decisions.
- [low] voice: A bare link sentence tacked onto a point about guardians and administrators. The misuse guide is about attorneys, so the reader is not told why they would go there.
  Current: Both roles carry real personal responsibility, in much the same way an executor is personally answerable for how they handle an estate. Read our guide to misuse of a power of attorney in Victoria.
  Proposed: Both roles carry real personal responsibility, in much the same way an executor is personally answerable for how they handle an estate. Where an attorney rather than an administrator has misused their position, our guide to misuse of a power of attorney explains what families can do.
- [low] voice: A metaphor and a dramatic tail where a plain statement would carry more weight.
  Current: Bills, care decisions and time-sensitive matters can sit in limbo while an application is prepared and heard, at exactly the moment a family can least afford the delay.
  Proposed: Bills, care decisions and other urgent matters can be left waiting while an application is prepared and heard, often when the family most needs them resolved.
- [low] answer-first: Two statement headings among question headings, and the second is vague. Questions match how a worried reader searches. Each H2 id and its contents list entry change with the heading.
  Current: What a guardian or administrator can and cannot do
The cost of not planning ahead
  Proposed: What can a guardian or administrator do, and what can they not do?
What does it cost to have no enduring power of attorney?
- [low] clarity: The flat opening 'There is no end date' is contradicted by the exception that follows, since urgent orders do end, and the second sentence runs to 60 words. The body puts it in the right order. The FAQPage schema text must change with the visible answer, which the gate checks.
  Current: There is no end date. Apart from urgent orders, an order continues until VCAT revokes it, but VCAT must reassess it within 12 months of making it and then at least once every three years unless it orders otherwise, and can continue, vary, replace or revoke it as the person's circumstances change, including ending it altogether if capacity is regained.
  Proposed: Apart from urgent orders, an order has no end date and continues until VCAT revokes it. VCAT must reassess it within 12 months of making it and then at least once every three years unless it orders otherwise, and can continue, vary, replace or revoke it as the person's circumstances change, including ending it altogether if capacity is regained.

## insight-making-a-valid-will.html
Scores: voice 6, clarity 7, persuasion 7, answer first 6

Clear and correct on the formal requirements, with prices quoted exactly as fees.html sets them and a strong mid-article prompt aimed at people who have married, separated or divorced since signing. It loses marks on voice and structure: the lead ends on a signpost, the mistakes list leans on clever lines ('Precision is kindness'), the advance care directives link is a non sequitur, two answers open with a cliché rather than the answer, one line claims years of experience for the firm, and one factual claim about capacity disputes is wider than the evidence.

- [medium] answer-first: The lead's last sentence is a signpost rather than a fact, and 'until it is too late' is a cliché that hides the concrete point: the mistake is found after death and needs a court application to fix. The proposed lead is 67 words and every fact in it is in the body.
  Current: The formal requirements are strict, and mistakes rarely surface until it is too late. Here is what makes a will valid, what it can and cannot control, and the errors we see causing disputes.
  Proposed: The formal requirements are strict, and a mistake in signing or witnessing usually comes to light only after death, when putting it right takes a slow, expensive and uncertain application to the Supreme Court.
- [medium] voice: The advance care directives link is a non sequitur after a paragraph about assets that pass outside a will. 'That is why' comments on the sentence before, and 'pull in the same direction' is a metaphor. The link belongs in the cost paragraph, where the estate plan includes an appointment of a medical treatment decision maker.
  Current: A good estate plan lines all of these up so they pull in the same direction. That is why a proper will appointment starts with a conversation about what you own and how it is held, not with a form. Read our guide to advance care directives in Victoria.
  Proposed: A good estate plan deals with all of these together, which is why a will appointment with us starts with a conversation about what you own and how it is held, not with a form.
- [medium] voice: Two clever lines in a row, the second turning the point into a saying, and neither tells the reader what actually goes wrong with vague wording.
  Current: Ambiguous wording. "I leave everything to my family" reads warmly and resolves nothing. Precision is kindness.
  Proposed: Ambiguous wording. A gift of "everything to my family" leaves your executor to work out who your family is and in what shares, and if the family cannot agree, a court may have to decide what the words mean.
- [medium] legal-query: A factual claim wider than the evidence. Most Victorian estate disputes are family provision claims, which the site's own contesting a will guide treats as the main route, not challenges to capacity, and 'battleground' is a metaphor. For Spencer to confirm. The suggested wording narrows the claim to challenges to validity and changes no statement of law.
  Current: Capacity is the single most common battleground in will disputes, particularly where a will is made late in life or after a diagnosis of dementia.
  Proposed: Capacity is one of the most common grounds on which a will's validity is challenged, particularly where a will is made late in life or after a diagnosis of dementia.
- [medium] firm-facts: An experience claim made for the firm. CLAUDE.md says experience claims never attach to the firm, which is not old enough for 'years'. Dropping the claim keeps the plural firm voice and loses nothing, and 'again and again' is padding.
  Current: After years of acting in estate disputes, we see the same handful of errors again and again:
  Proposed: The same handful of errors cause most of the will disputes we see:
- [low] answer-first: The heading asks whether kits are valid, and the answer, yes, is not the first word. The short second sentence breaks the flow of the reason, and 'the cost lands on' gives a cost a human verb.
  Current: You can legally write your own will in Victoria, and a kit will that is properly signed and witnessed can be valid. The difficulty is that kits cannot ask follow-up questions. They do not notice the blended family, the asset that will not pass under the will, the beneficiary on a disability pension whose inheritance needs protecting, or the wording that means two different things. When a cheap will fails, the cost lands on your estate and your family, usually many times the price of having it drafted properly in the first place.
  Proposed: Yes, a will kit or online will can be valid in Victoria if it is properly signed and witnessed. The difficulty is that a kit cannot ask follow-up questions, so it will not notice the blended family, the asset that will not pass under the will, the beneficiary on a disability pension whose inheritance needs protecting, or the wording that means two different things. When a cheap will fails, your estate and your family bear the cost, which is usually many times the price of having the will drafted properly.
- [low] answer-first: The heading asks how often, and the answer arrives in the third sentence after a cliché. The last sentence makes an unsupported claim and adds a comment tail.
  Current: A will is not a set-and-forget document. Review it after marriage, separation or divorce, the birth of children or grandchildren, the death of an executor or beneficiary, buying or selling significant assets, or starting a business. As a rule of thumb, read it every three to five years. Most reviews end with no changes needed, which is itself useful certainty.
  Proposed: Read your will every three to five years, and review it straight away after marriage, separation or divorce, the birth of a child or grandchild, the death of an executor or beneficiary, buying or selling significant assets, or starting a business.
- [low] clarity: 'Your share of the house' contradicts, on a first read, the bullet two lines later saying that a home owned as joint tenants passes outside the will.
  Current: A will controls the assets you own personally: your share of the house, money, vehicles, shares, personal effects.
  Proposed: A will controls the assets you own in your own name, such as a house in your sole name or your share of one you own as tenants in common, money, vehicles, shares and personal effects.

## insight-power-of-attorney-misuse.html
Scores: voice 7, clarity 8, persuasion 7, answer first 7

The strongest of the seven. Question headings are answered in their first words ('It can be.', 'Often, yes'), the warning signs are concrete and the first steps section is practical. The gaps are structural: the lead leaves out the six month deadline the body states, the mid-article prompt sits two sections before that deadline, the page names no fixed fee, one link points at a service page that does not cover the topic, and four aphoristic closing lines read as a habit.

- [high] answer-first: The site's rule is that a lead names any deadline the body states, and this body states one that decides whether money can be recovered at all: a compensation application within six months of the first death unless the time is extended. The lead omits it. The proposed lead is 69 words and drops only 'require them to account for their dealings', which the body keeps.
  Current: If someone is misusing a power of attorney in Victoria, you can act. The Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal can review the attorney's conduct, require them to account for their dealings, revoke the appointment and order compensation, and dishonest misuse can also be a criminal matter. The sooner concerns are raised, the more of the principal's money and property can be protected.
  Proposed: If someone is misusing a power of attorney in Victoria, you can act. The Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal can review the attorney's conduct, revoke the appointment and order compensation, and dishonest misuse can also be a crime. Once the principal or the attorney has died, a compensation application must be made within six months of the first death unless the tribunal or the Supreme Court extends the time.
- [medium] call-prompt: The rule puts the mid-article prompt straight after the section that states a time limit. This one sits after the capacity section, and 'Can the money be recovered?', which states the six month limit, has no prompt. Keep this one and add the wording below after the recovery section, or move it there.
  Current: Worried about how an attorney is using a family member's money or property? Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry.
  Proposed: Has a parent died, and do you believe their attorney took money from them? An application for compensation must be made within six months of the first death, the parent's or the attorney's, unless the time is extended. Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry.
- [medium] voice: 'Matters' twice in four words, and the last sentence is one of four aphoristic closing lines on the page. The others are 'The duties do not bend for good intentions', 'Doing nothing is the one option that reliably makes things worse' and 'the hardest money to see again'. The voice rules allow one such line in an article.
  Current: Recovery in practice depends on what the attorney still holds: money that has been spent, or moved beyond reach, is harder to recover than money sitting in an account or represented by an asset that can be traced. That is the practical reason speed matters more in these matters than in most.
  Proposed: Recovery in practice depends on what the attorney still holds. Money that has been spent or moved beyond reach is harder to recover than money still sitting in an account or represented by an asset that can be traced, so acting quickly matters more in these cases than in most.
- [medium] fees-and-links: The page names no price anywhere, and this is where a family wanting to replace the attorney decides to act. fees.html lists an enduring power of attorney at $495 including GST. Link the price to /fees#fee-enduring-power-of-attorney and the document to /service-wills-and-estate-planning.
  Current: A principal who still has decision-making capacity remains in charge. They can revoke the enduring power of attorney, provided the formal requirements for revocation are met, appoint someone else instead, and tell their bank and the former attorney in writing that the authority has ended.
  Proposed: A principal who still has decision-making capacity remains in charge. They can revoke the enduring power of attorney, provided the formal requirements for revocation are met, appoint someone else instead, and tell their bank and the former attorney in writing that the authority has ended. A new enduring power of attorney is a fixed fee of $495 including GST, with advice on choosing the attorney and a signing appointment.
- [low] voice: A staged opening, the law given a personality ('The law's answer') and a reference back to 'the duties above'.
  Current: The reason misuse matters so much is the word enduring. The appointment keeps operating after the principal loses decision-making capacity, which is precisely when the principal can no longer watch what is being done in their name. The law's answer to that vulnerability is the set of duties above, backed by a tribunal with real powers to intervene.
  Proposed: An enduring power keeps operating after the principal loses decision-making capacity, which is exactly when they can no longer see what is being done in their name, so the attorney's duties are strict and VCAT has real power to intervene.
- [low] voice: A saying where the plain point is stronger.
  Current: Where money is still moving, get advice promptly about seeking urgent orders, because what is spent before anyone intervenes is the hardest money to see again.
  Proposed: Where money is still moving, get advice promptly about seeking urgent orders, because money spent before anyone intervenes is the hardest to recover.
- [low] fees-and-links: This phrase links to /service-contesting-a-will, whose sections cover family provision claims, challenges to validity, defending an estate and protecting a beneficiary's share. It says nothing about recovering money from an attorney, so the click disappoints. Remove the link from this phrase, or point it at /wills-and-estates until a page covers these claims. The words stay as they are.
  Current: the estate may be able to pursue what was taken

## insight-probate-victoria.html
Scores: voice 6, clarity 7, persuasion 7, answer first 8

Answer-first and well signposted, with the right prices, a call prompt straight after the six month time limit and a link to the probate service page. The most important issue is legal: the page still says professional fees are ordinarily paid from the estate, the statement withdrawn from the probate service page pending Spencer's view on rule 9.01. The rest is voice: a comma splice, bare link sentences, a fraudulent calumny link in a list about caveats, 'hands on the ground', and an over-promise about what one conversation delivers.

- [high] legal-query: DECISIONS.md, 28 Sep 2026, 'Waiting on Spencer', records that rule 9.01 of the Supreme Court (Administration and Probate) Rules 2023 sets the professional charges for a grant that may be paid out of an estate by its Appendix scale, so service-probate.html stopped saying costs are ordinarily paid from the estate until Spencer gives his view. This article still says it, straight after the $1,815 and $2,178 fixed fees, and again in the FAQ 'Who pays the cost of obtaining probate?' and its FAQPage schema. Not rewriting the law: for Spencer. If he wants the article in step with the service page meanwhile, the service page's own sentence serves here, and the FAQ answer needs his view before it is reworded.
  Current: Both are ordinarily paid from the estate, not from the executor's own pocket.
  Proposed: Before any work begins, we confirm in writing how these costs will be paid.
- [medium] voice: A comma splice joins two complete sentences, and the opening announces the point instead of making it. The FAQ answer already writes the same clause correctly with 'and'.
  Current: There are two costs to distinguish. The first is the court fee for filing the application, which in Victoria is scaled to the gross value of the estate: modest estates pay little or nothing, large estates pay substantially more.
  Proposed: There are two costs. The first is the court fee for filing the application, which in Victoria is scaled to the gross value of the estate, so modest estates pay little or nothing and large estates pay substantially more.
- [medium] clarity: The bare link sentence splits the reason from the consequence it explains, and 'See the time limits in contesting a will in Victoria' is not a sentence anyone would say.
  Current: Executors are generally advised not to distribute earlier than six months from the grant, because that is the window in which an eligible person may bring a family provision claim against the estate. See the time limits in contesting a will in Victoria. Distributing early can expose the executor personally.
  Proposed: Executors are generally advised not to distribute earlier than six months from the grant, because that is the period in which an eligible person may bring a family provision claim against the estate, and an executor who distributes early can be personally exposed. Our guide to contesting a will in Victoria sets out the time limits.
- [medium] fees-and-links: A reader worried about a caveat is sent, by a bare link sentence, to fraudulent calumny, one of the least common grounds of challenge, rather than to the guide that explains the two kinds of challenge.
  Current: Someone signals a challenge. A caveat can be lodged to halt a grant while validity is examined; family provision claims run on their own six-month clock. Read our guide to fraudulent calumny in Victoria.
  Proposed: Someone signals a challenge. A caveat can be lodged to halt a grant while the will's validity is examined, and family provision claims run on their own six-month clock. Our guide to contesting a will explains the two kinds of challenge, and our guide to fraudulent calumny covers one of the less familiar grounds.
- [medium] firm-facts: Promises more than a first conversation can deliver and reads as a new service promise, where the site allows only three. A written estimate follows the conversation rather than coming out of it.
  Current: Then take advice: one conversation will map the entire administration, tell you whether probate is needed, and give you a written estimate of the costs.
  Proposed: Then take advice. One conversation is usually enough to tell you whether probate is needed and what the administration will involve, and we confirm our fee in writing before any substantive work begins.
- [low] voice: 'Your hands on the ground' and 'in the right time zone' are cute where plain words serve, and 'from afar' is vaguer than the heading's interstate or overseas.
  Current: If you are administering a Victorian estate from afar, we can act as your hands on the ground: receiving documents, dealing with the court and the institutions, and keeping the administration moving in the right time zone.
  Proposed: If you are administering a Victorian estate from interstate or overseas, we can do the local work for you, receiving documents, dealing with the court and the institutions, and keeping the administration moving.
- [low] fees-and-links: The link goes to the top of /fees rather than to the two prices quoted, which have anchors; the trustee company article already links #fee-probate-application. 'How we help' also carries padding, 'We act with the discretion these matters call for', which can go.
  Current: as our fixed fees page sets out
  Proposed: Link the two prices to /fees#fee-probate-application and /fees#fee-letters-of-administration, and in How we help read: We set out our fees in writing before any substantive work begins.
