What if you have more than one super fund?
How do you check your nomination?
- [medium] voice: Overwritten: 'routinely astonished', 'carefully', 'simply' and 'does not speak to' all strain for effect.
  Current: Families are routinely astonished to learn that the will they carefully prepared simply does not speak to this asset.
  Proposed: Many families learn only after a death that the will does not decide where the super goes.
- [medium] voice: 'Real money' is casual, and the last sentence is a saying built on a metaphor.
  Current: On a large balance that difference is real money, and it can often be managed with planning: the split between spouse and children, the use of the estate, even the timing of contributions all affect the outcome. This is where superannuation advice and estate planning need to sit in the same room.
  Proposed: On a large balance the difference is substantial, and planning can often reduce it, because the split between spouse and children, the use of the estate and even the timing of contributions all affect the outcome. Your super and your estate plan should therefore be reviewed together, with your financial adviser where you have one.
- [medium] clarity: 'SMSF' is never spelled out on the page, and 'littered with' overstates case law the page does not cite.
  Current: In an SMSF the trustee deciding your death benefit may be exactly the family member who stands to gain from the decision. Australian case law is littered with disputes where a surviving trustee paid the benefit to themselves despite the deceased's clear wishes, because the nomination had lapsed or was defective.
  Proposed: In a self-managed super fund, or SMSF, the trustee deciding your death benefit may be exactly the family member who stands to gain from the decision. There have been a number of reported cases in which a surviving trustee paid the benefit to themselves despite the deceased's clear wishes, because the nomination had lapsed or was defective.
- [medium] fees-and-links: The estate plan has a published price, $1,089 including GST for one person and $1,980 for a couple, yet the page says fees are quoted in writing and sends the reader to the hub rather than to the estate planning service page or the phone. Link the prices to /fees#fee-estate-plan-for-one-person and the estate plan to /service-wills-and-estate-planning.
  Current: We do this as part of every estate plan, and quote fixed fees in writing before we start. Our wills and estates page is the place to begin.
  Proposed: We do this as part of every estate plan, and an estate plan with a will, an enduring power of attorney and an appointment of a medical treatment decision maker is a fixed fee of $1,089 including GST for one person, or $1,980 for a couple.
- [medium] legal-query: The Sources line cites sections 1053 and 1056 of the Corporations Act, which govern these complaints and their time limits, but the page never states a period, and the 3 Nov 2026 verification did not record reading those two sections. If Spencer is satisfied of the exact periods after reading the authorised text, naming them would give the lead, this section and the call prompt a concrete deadline; if not, the citation should be checked. No wording proposed until the provisions are read.
  Current: Time limits apply at each stage and they are short.
- [low] style: The house style writes percentages in figures. The same sentence appears in the FAQ answer and its FAQPage schema, which must change together.
  Current: The taxed element is taxed at up to fifteen per cent and any untaxed element, which often arises where the benefit includes insurance proceeds, at up to thirty per cent.
  Proposed: The taxed element is taxed at up to 15 per cent and any untaxed element, which often arises where the benefit includes insurance proceeds, at up to 30 per cent.

## insight-testamentary-trusts.html
Scores: voice 5, clarity 6, persuasion 7, answer first 5

Useful and honest, with the right prices, a good mid-article prompt and a welcome 'not everyone needs one', but the page is built on metaphor ('a shield, not a fortress' twice, 'better armour', 'imprisonment', 'horizon') and cryptic headings. The tax section opens with a staged line, the cost question is answered only at the end of the page, 'we say so' appears three times, and the May 2026 minimum tax paragraph needs checking for currency.

- [medium] voice: A metaphor in the middle of a list has to be decoded and breaks the grammar of the series. The lead is also exactly 70 words, the gate's limit, so any addition fails the build; the proposed sentence brings it to 69.
  Current: Properly drafted, it generally keeps the money outside a bankrupt beneficiary's personal estate, is a shield, not a fortress, on relationship breakdown, and lets children under 18 receive income from inherited assets taxed at ordinary adult rates.
  Proposed: Properly drafted, it generally keeps the money outside a bankrupt beneficiary's personal estate, gives partial protection if a relationship breaks down, and lets children under 18 receive income from inherited assets taxed at ordinary adult rates.
- [medium] answer-first: The heading asks how a testamentary trust reduces tax, and the first sentence is a staged opening; the answer arrives in the third.
  Current: Here is the feature that surprises people. Minors who receive distributions from an ordinary family trust are taxed at penalty rates above a tiny threshold. But income from a testamentary trust is excepted: children under 18 are taxed at ordinary adult rates, with the tax-free threshold.
  Proposed: Income a testamentary trust distributes to children under 18 is taxed at ordinary adult rates, with the tax-free threshold, whereas minors who receive distributions from an ordinary family trust are taxed at penalty rates above a small threshold.
- [medium] fees-and-links: The heading asks what a testamentary trust costs, and the published prices, from $1,650 including GST for one person and from $3,025 for a couple, appear only in the closing section. Link the prices to /fees#fee-will-with-a-testamentary-trust-for-one-person; the service page link on this sentence stays.
  Current: A will containing testamentary trusts costs more to prepare than a simple will, because the drafting is more complex, and a trust that is activated has modest ongoing costs: a tax file number, annual returns, trustee record-keeping.
  Proposed: A will with a testamentary trust is a fixed fee from $1,650 including GST for one person and from $3,025 for a couple, more than a simple will because the drafting is more complex, and a trust that is activated after your death has modest ongoing costs: a tax file number, annual returns and trustee record-keeping.
- [medium] legal-query: A dated statement about a proposal, followed by 'The Government has since said' and 'not yet law', last checked when the page was modified on 29 Sep 2026. Whether the measure is still a proposal, and whether the exclusion for testamentary trusts still stands, should be checked before the page is next published, with the currency line moving if anything changes. No rewrite proposed.
  Current: In May 2026 the Australian Government announced a proposed minimum tax on the income of discretionary trusts, to start from 1 July 2028.
- [medium] voice: 'Nuance' is a stock word, and the shield and fortress image appears for the second time on the page.
  Current: Trust assets are treated with more nuance: the family courts can still take a trust into account, particularly where the beneficiary controls it, so a trust is a shield rather than a fortress.
  Proposed: Trust assets are treated differently, but the family courts can still take a trust into account, particularly where the beneficiary controls it, so the protection is partial.
- [low] legal-query: On the page's own account, in the relationship breakdown bullet and the FAQ, a beneficiary's control can weaken the protection in family law, so 'still enjoy its protections' reads as a promise the rest of the page qualifies. For Spencer to confirm; the suggested wording restates only what the page already says.
  Current: Your primary beneficiary can usually control the trust, benefit from it, and still enjoy its protections.
  Proposed: Your primary beneficiary can usually control the trust and benefit from it, although control can weaken its protection in a family law property dispute.
- [low] answer-first: Cryptic or clever headings: 'Who should say no' does not say to what. 'Three questions clients always ask' claims 'always' and differs from the 'Common questions' every other article uses. Each H2 id and its contents list entry change with the heading.
  Current: Protection from life's reversals
Blended families
Who should say no
Getting the trustee right
How a trust runs, year to year
Three questions clients always ask
  Proposed: How does a testamentary trust protect an inheritance?
How can a testamentary trust help a blended family?
Who does not need a testamentary trust?
Who should be the trustee?
How does a testamentary trust run each year?
Common questions
- [low] voice: One of several images the reader must decode: 'better armour' here, plus 'a steady hand between the money and the risk', 'designed for flexibility, not imprisonment', 'that horizon comfortably covers grandchildren', 'A trust is only as good as its trustee' and 'thirty years rather than three'. Plainer FAQ lines, whose FAQPage schema must change with them: 'If they control the trust as trustee, generally yes, because most testamentary trusts are designed to be flexible.' 'The protection is real, but it is stronger where control is shared or held by an independent trustee.' 'Trusts must vest within the legal maximum period, which in Victoria is long enough to cover grandchildren.'
  Current: The structure only feels complicated in the abstract; run properly, it feels like a family investment account with better armour.
  Proposed: Run properly, it works much like a family investment account that also carries the trust's protections.

## insight-trustee-company-or-lawyer-will.html
Scores: voice 7, clarity 6, persuasion 7, answer first 6

The newest page and the closest to Spencer's register: fair to trustee companies, concrete about fees and linked to the fee anchors. It is let down by three dense paragraphs on executor commission written closer to the statute than to the reader, a run of eight paragraphs of identical shape, a lead and payment section that circle before answering, a call prompt placed before the section it refers to, and two legal points for Spencer: the 'paid from the estate' statement under review for rule 9.01, and the 1 November 2017 qualification that the executor duties guide carries and this page omits.

- [high] legal-query: The statement withdrawn from service-probate.html pending Spencer's view on rule 9.01 (DECISIONS.md, 28 Sep 2026) is made here, directly before the $1,815 probate fixed fee, and in the lead ('the estate pays for legal work only as it is actually done') and the meta description ('how each is paid from the estate'). This page was published on 12 Oct, after the service page was changed. Not rewriting the law: for Spencer. If he wants it in step with the service page meanwhile, the wording below serves, and the lead finding covers the lead.
  Current: None of this affects legal costs. A family executor who engages a lawyer pays the lawyer from the estate.
  Proposed: These rules govern the executor's own pay, not legal costs. A family executor who engages a lawyer agrees the lawyer's fees, and we confirm in writing how they will be paid before any work begins.
- [medium] legal-query: insight-executor-duties.html limits this rule to a will signed on or after 1 November 2017, and the 27 Oct 2026 verification recorded that section 106(2) confines sections 65B to 65D to wills executed from that date. This page states the consent rule, the election to charge fees and the duty to notify beneficiaries without the date, and its section on wills that already name a trustee company speaks to readers with older wills. For Spencer to confirm against the Act.
  Current: A clause in a will paying the executor only works if the will-maker gave written informed consent to it before signing the will.
  Proposed: For a will signed on or after 1 November 2017, a clause in the will paying the executor only works if the will-maker gave written informed consent to it before signing.
- [medium] answer-first: The title asks 'should', and the lead never gives the article's view, which the body states plainly: 'most estates are best handled by that person with legal help'. 'The real choice is' is a staged opening, and the last clause carries the statement under review in the first finding. The proposed lead is 67 words.
  Current: Either a trustee company or a lawyer can prepare a valid will in Victoria. The real choice is who will administer your estate and how they will be paid for it. A trustee company commonly expects to be named as your executor and is paid from the estate. With a lawyer, you choose your own executor, and the estate pays for legal work only as it is actually done.
  Proposed: Either a trustee company or a lawyer can prepare a valid will in Victoria, so the choice that matters is who will administer your estate and how they are paid. A trustee company commonly expects to be named as your executor and is paid from the estate. With a lawyer, you choose your own executor, which for most families with someone they trust is the better fit.
- [medium] answer-first: The heading asks how a trustee company is paid, and the section opens with its duty to publish a fee schedule; the answer, paid from the estate and commonly by reference to its value, arrives in the second paragraph. Add the sentence below as the section's first sentence, and start the second paragraph at 'A house and two bank accounts'.
  Current: A licensed trustee company must keep an up to date schedule of the fees it generally charges available to the public on its website, and free of charge at its offices during opening hours.
  Proposed: A trustee company is paid from the estate, and its fees for administering an estate are commonly calculated by reference to the estate's value, so the cost moves with what you own rather than with how much work the estate needs.
- [medium] clarity: 'Those beneficiaries' points back two paragraphs, and the last sentence stacks four qualifications that need a second reading. Subject to the second finding, which may add the 1 November 2017 date here too.
  Current: An executor other than a trustee company who wants to be paid must also tell each of those beneficiaries, as soon as reasonably practicable, the basis of the payment, how it is calculated, its estimated value and their right to have it reviewed by the court. An executor who does not do so is not entitled to commission or fees from the estate. Separately, the Supreme Court can reduce, or order repayment of, commission or fees it finds excessive, on the application of anyone interested in the estate, such as a beneficiary, or of a creditor, or on its own motion, although that power does not apply to State Trustees.
  Proposed: An executor other than a trustee company who wants to be paid must also tell each beneficiary whose share of the residue would be reduced, as soon as reasonably practicable, the basis of the payment, how it is calculated, its estimated value and their right to have it reviewed by the court. An executor who does not do so is not entitled to commission or fees from the estate. Separately, the Supreme Court can reduce commission or fees it finds excessive, or order them repaid. It can act on the application of a beneficiary or anyone else interested in the estate, or of a creditor, or on its own motion, although the power does not apply to State Trustees.
- [medium] call-prompt: The prompt sits after the payment sections, two sections before 'What if your will already names a trustee company?', so it answers a question the reader has not reached. With no time limit in the article, the rule puts the prompt where the reader realises they need help: move this one to follow that section, or retarget it to where it sits with the wording below.
  Current: If your will already names a trustee company and you are not sure what that will mean for your family, we can read it with you. Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry.
  Proposed: Weighing a trustee company's fees against a family executor with legal help? We can work through what each would mean for your estate. Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry.
- [medium] fees-and-links: 'Anything outside those descriptions' and 'anything else' overlap, so the reader cannot tell which work is priced on the fees page; the site's standard wording is 'estimated in writing'. No part of this page links the wills and estate planning service page, and this section is the place for it.
  Current: Testamentary trust wills and anything outside those descriptions are set out on our fixed fees page, and anything else is confirmed in writing before substantive work begins.
  Proposed: Testamentary trust wills and other estate planning work are priced on our fixed fees page, and anything the page does not cover is estimated in writing before substantive work begins.
- [low] voice: Turns the point into a saying, and the paragraph's last sentence already says what a lawyer is engaged for.
  Current: The will itself then has to be drafted around the facts, and that is where the real work lies.
  Proposed: The will itself then has to be drafted around your family's circumstances.

## insight-advance-care-directives-victoria.html
Scores: voice 7, clarity 7, persuasion 6, answer first 7

A sound, practical page that applies the law to real family situations and names the $495 appointment fee. It falls short in four places. The lead defines the document instead of answering who decides. One section opens with a 'Both' that refers to nothing. The closing paragraph and the final call suggest an advance care directive is part of the $1,089 estate plan, which fees.html and the estate planning service page contradict. A handful of phrases are stiff or repeated. The law was verified on 6 Oct 2026, and none of the proposals below changes it.

- [high] consistency: The page says a directive is normally prepared as part of a complete estate plan, and the final call says the will, the power of attorney and the directive are put in place in one conversation. fees.html prices the estate plan for one person at $1,089 and lists what it covers: a will, an enduring power of attorney and an appointment of a medical treatment decision maker. The wills and estate planning service page says an advance care directive is not on the fees page and is estimated in writing. A reader who calls expecting a directive inside the $1,089 plan will be disappointed. Link each price to its own anchor, /fees#fee-estate-plan-for-one-person and /fees#fee-appointment-of-a-medical-treatment-decision-maker.
  Current: How we help: "Spencer Alexander Lawyers prepares advance care directives and medical treatment decision maker appointments for clients across Melbourne and Victoria, normally as part of a complete estate plan alongside a will and an enduring power of attorney. We work through what you want in plain language, draft instructions that will still make sense to a treating team years later, make sure they are executed correctly, and brief the person you appoint on the role. An appointment of a medical treatment decision maker is a fixed fee of $495 per person, including GST, as our fixed fees page sets out." Closing call: "A will, an enduring power of attorney and an advance care directive belong together. One conversation puts all three in place."
  Proposed: How we help: "We prepare advance care directives and appointments of medical treatment decision makers for clients across Melbourne and Victoria, usually alongside a will and an enduring power of attorney. We work through what you want in plain language, draft instructions that will still make sense to a treating team years later, make sure the documents are signed and witnessed correctly, and brief the person you appoint on the role. An estate plan for one person, with a will, an enduring power of attorney and an appointment of a medical treatment decision maker, is a fixed fee of $1,089 including GST. An appointment on its own is $495 per person including GST, and an advance care directive is estimated in writing before substantive work begins, as our fixed fees page sets out." Closing call: "We can prepare your will, enduring power of attorney and appointment of a medical treatment decision maker together, for a fixed fee of $1,089 including GST for one person. Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry."
- [medium] answer-first: The H1 asks who decides if you cannot, but the first sentence defines the document. The answer only arrives in the second sentence, as a clipped imperative, 'Appoint nobody and...', and it leaves out the person you appointed, who comes first. The last sentence only points ahead. The proposed lead is 63 words, and every fact in it is already in the body: 'Can your family override what you have written?' says an instructional directive operates as your own consent or refusal.
  Current: An advance care directive is a formal written document recording the medical treatment you consent to or refuse, and the values you want considered, if you lose decision making capacity. Appoint nobody and Victorian law still produces a medical treatment decision maker: a VCAT-appointed guardian with that power, then the first available person on a list headed by your spouse or domestic partner. We explain what happens when relatives disagree.
  Proposed: If you lose decision making capacity, any instructional directive you have made operates as your own consent or refusal, and other decisions fall to your medical treatment decision maker. That decision maker is the person you appointed or, if you appointed nobody, a VCAT-appointed guardian with that power, then the first available person on a list headed by your spouse or domestic partner.
- [medium] clarity: 'Both' refers to nothing. The heading asks about one document, and the paragraph opens as though two had just been named, so the reader has to work out that the second is the appointment of a medical treatment decision maker. 'One' in the heading is also vague when a search result quotes the heading on its own. Update the contents list to match.
  Current: Heading: "How do you make one in Victoria?" First sentence: "Both are formal legal documents and the signing requirements are strict."
  Proposed: Heading: "How do you make an advance care directive in Victoria?" First sentence: "An advance care directive and an appointment of a medical treatment decision maker are both formal legal documents, and the signing requirements for each are strict."
- [medium] accuracy: 'Removes all of that' overstates what an appointment achieves. The same paragraph says two sentences later that a dispute can still end up before VCAT.
  Current: Appointing someone yourself removes all of that. You choose the person, and you tell them beforehand what you want, so they are not guessing under pressure.
  Proposed: Appointing someone yourself avoids most of that, because you choose the person and tell them beforehand what you want, so they are not guessing under pressure.
- [low] voice: 'Tolerably' is stiff. 'A conventional family' reads as a judgement on the separated and blended families the list goes on to describe, and those are the readers most likely to need this page.
  Current: That default works tolerably for a conventional family. It works badly in the situations we see most often:
  Proposed: That order works well enough in many families, but it works badly in the situations we see most often:
- [low] voice: Two phrases repeat. 'Which is precisely why' appears twice, and 'fail at the only moment it matters' appears here and again in the answer on whether you need a lawyer. 'Get the formalities wrong and...' is the same clipped imperative as 'Appoint nobody and...' in the lead. If the FAQ answer changes, the FAQPage schema must change in the same commit.
  Current: "Get the formalities wrong and the document may fail at the only moment it matters. That is the main reason to have these prepared alongside your will and your enduring power of attorney rather than downloaded and signed at the kitchen table." Also "which is precisely why the document has to be findable rather than filed away" and "which is precisely why the document has to exist beforehand".
  Proposed: "If the formalities are wrong, the document may fail at the only moment it matters, and that is the main reason to have it prepared alongside your will and enduring power of attorney rather than downloaded and signed at the kitchen table." Then "so the document has to be easy to find, not filed away" and "so the document has to be in place beforehand".
- [low] voice: The first passage opens with a sentence that only clears its throat, then runs four short sentences in a row and uses a colon to stage a reveal. The second inflates the point with 'matters enormously' instead of saying how the two kinds of statement differ.
  Current: "It helps to see where it sits among the other documents. A will speaks only after you die. An enduring power of attorney deals with money, property and personal matters while you are alive. An advance care directive deals with one thing: medical treatment. Only the directive tells a treating team what you want done when you cannot tell them yourself." And "Victoria recognises two kinds of statement inside an advance care directive, and the difference matters enormously in practice."
  Proposed: "A will speaks only after you die, and an enduring power of attorney deals with money, property and personal matters while you are alive. An advance care directive deals only with medical treatment, and it is the one document that tells a treating team what you want done when you cannot tell them yourself." And "An advance care directive can contain two kinds of statement, and they work very differently when a decision has to be made."
- [low] call-prompt: This is a verbless advertising question rather than a sentence. It also misses the chance to name the fixed fee at the moment a reader is deciding whether to call.
  Current: Want your medical wishes recorded properly, and the right person appointed to speak for you? Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry.
  Proposed: If you want your medical wishes recorded properly and the right person appointed to speak for you, call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry. An appointment of a medical treatment decision maker is a fixed fee of $495 including GST.

## insight-contesting-a-will.html
Scores: voice 5, clarity 6, persuasion 6, answer first 7

Legally careful and well organised around the six month limit, and the mid article call sits where it should, straight after the deadline. It is also the most rhetorical page in this group. A worried claimant has to reread jargon and figures of speech such as 'caveat-level question', 'claimant-adjacent beneficiary', 'have a free swing' and 'the same clock is your ally'. The closing section is headed with the stock phrase 'The bottom line', and the page says nothing about how the firm charges for a dispute. It was verified against the Act on 3 Nov 2026, and none of the proposals changes a statement of law.

- [high] clarity: 'Caveat-level' means nothing to a client. Caveat is also a term of art in probate: lodging a caveat stops a grant, and it is not how a family provision claim is made, so a reader may take it as the step to take. All the sentence means is: find out the date of the grant, because the six months run from it.
  Current: If you suspect you have a claim, the sensible first step this week is a caveat-level question: has probate been granted, and when?
  Proposed: If you think you may have a claim, find out this week whether probate has been granted and on what date, because the six months run from that date.
- [medium] voice: 'Costs follow reality' says nothing. 'Have a free swing' is slang that turns a point about costs into a saying. The FAQ answer on costs already has the plain version.
  Current: Costs follow reality: a successful claimant's reasonable costs are often paid from the estate, but an unsuccessful or overreaching claimant can be ordered to pay costs personally. The era of "the estate always pays, so have a free swing" is gone.
  Proposed: On costs, a successful claimant's reasonable costs are often paid from the estate, but an unsuccessful or overreaching claimant can be ordered to pay costs personally, so it is no longer safe to assume the estate always pays.
- [medium] clarity: 'A claimant-adjacent beneficiary' and 'an interest worth keeping' need rereading, and it is not clear what they mean. 'But risk shrinks with structure' is clipped, and the semicolon list runs four ideas into one sentence. The proposal assumes the point is to give a likely claimant a real stake in the estate. Confirm that meaning with Spencer before using it, because it is advice.
  Current: No drafting removes Part IV: you cannot contract your family out of the Act. But risk shrinks with structure: honest provision where a moral duty plainly exists; documented reasons for unequal treatment; strategic use of non-estate assets such as superannuation and joint ownership; and, where conflict is foreseeable, testamentary trusts that give a claimant-adjacent beneficiary an interest worth keeping. Read our guide to making a valid will, then have the awkward conversation with us instead of leaving it to the Supreme Court.
  Proposed: No will can remove the right to bring a Part IV claim, because you cannot contract your family out of the Act. Careful planning does reduce the risk, through honest provision where a moral duty plainly exists, a written record of your reasons for treating people unequally, and deliberate use of assets that pass outside the estate, such as superannuation and jointly owned property. Where conflict is foreseeable, a testamentary trust can give a likely claimant a real interest in the estate. Read our guide to making a valid will, then talk it through with us rather than leaving it to the Supreme Court.
- [medium] voice: 'The bottom line' is a stock phrase the checker flags. The paragraph is built from sayings ('wills sometimes fail the people they had a duty to hold', 'grief and fairness do not always arrive together', 'the same clock is your ally') rather than telling each kind of reader what to do. Change the heading's id and the contents list along with the heading.
  Current: Heading "The bottom line", then: "Family provision claims exist because wills sometimes fail the people they had a duty to hold, and because grief and fairness do not always arrive together. If you have been left out or left short, the six-month clock makes this week the right time to ask where you stand. If you are defending an estate, the same clock is your ally. Either way, most of these disputes end in a mediated settlement, and the quality of preparation decides its terms."
  Proposed: Heading "What should you do now?", then: "If you have been left out of a will or left short, the six months from the grant are the deadline that matters, so find out now whether probate has been granted and when. If you are an executor defending an estate, the same six months tell you when it is generally safe to distribute, provided you have had no notice of a claim. Either way, most of these disputes end in a mediated settlement, and how well you have prepared shapes its terms." Keep the existing last sentence pointing to the Supreme Court website.
- [medium] persuasion: After 'can I claim?', a claimant's next question is what it will cost, and the page never answers it. The will disputes service page and fees.html both say a dispute has no fixed fee and is charged on a written estimate, set out in a costs agreement before substantive work begins. Add that at the end of 'Defending an estate', linking /fees#when-a-fixed-fee-does-not-fit.
  Current: We act for executors as well as claimants, and the judgment calls are mirror images. Our executor's guide covers the wider role.
  Proposed: Add after those sentences: "A will dispute has no fixed fee, so once we understand what you need we set out a written fee estimate in a costs agreement before any substantive work begins. Your first call is free, and you speak with a lawyer."
- [medium] legal-query: These timing figures, and the word 'compulsory', are not in the sections the ledger verified, and the page gives no source for them. The will disputes service page says only that the court can refer the dispute to mediation at any stage. Check them against the court's practice note on family provision claims, or generalise them. The substance is not rewritten here.
  Current: "Victorian procedure pushes Part IV claims to mediation early, and most resolve there, commonly within six to twelve months of filing, against two years or more for the rare matter that runs to trial." And "a compulsory mediation within months, where most matters end".
  Proposed: If the figures cannot be sourced: "Victorian procedure pushes Part IV claims to mediation early, and most resolve there, long before any trial."
- [low] clarity: At 71 words, the answer that search and answer engines are most likely to quote needs rereading. Split it into the main categories and the dependency rule. The FAQPage schema must change in the same commit.
  Current: Victoria confines Part IV family provision claims to defined categories of eligible person, including a spouse or domestic partner at the date of death, children and stepchildren of any age, a former spouse or partner who could have brought family law proceedings but had not finalised them, and, if they were wholly or partly dependent on the deceased, a registered caring partner, a grandchild or a member of the deceased's household. Friends, siblings and carers outside those categories cannot claim, however deserving.
  Proposed: Only an eligible person can make a family provision claim in Victoria. Eligible people include a spouse or domestic partner at the date of death, children and stepchildren of any age, and a former spouse or partner who could have brought family law proceedings but had not finalised them. A registered caring partner, a grandchild or a member of the deceased's household is also eligible, but only if they were wholly or partly dependent on the deceased. Friends, siblings and carers outside those categories cannot claim, however deserving.
- [low] voice: A 'not this, but that' saying, used in the body and repeated word for word in the FAQ answer.
  Current: Extensions are the exception, not a plan.
  Proposed: An extension is the exception, so do not plan on one.
- [low] voice: 'Files' is lawyers' shorthand. 'Converting grievance into strategy' is a slogan, and the last sentence is a rhetorical three part close.
  Current: Emotion runs high on both sides of these files, and part of our role is converting grievance into strategy. The claimants who do best arrive early, document their circumstances honestly, and let the merits, not the anger, set the pace.
  Proposed: Emotions run high on both sides of a will dispute, and part of our work is turning a sense of grievance into a clear plan. Claimants do best when they come to us early, set out their circumstances honestly and let the merits of the claim, rather than anger, set the pace.
- [low] clarity: The final clause has to be read twice before its meaning comes through.
  Current: Eligibility is a firm threshold: friends, siblings and carers outside the categories cannot claim however deserving, which is itself a reason careful wills consider who can.
  Proposed: Eligibility is a firm threshold, so friends, siblings and carers outside the categories cannot claim, however deserving. A careful will therefore starts by working out who could claim.

## insight-dying-without-a-will.html
Scores: voice 4, clarity 6, persuasion 5, answer first 6

The intestacy rules are stated clearly and correctly, the blended family split is explained well, and both fixed fees are named. Even so, this is the furthest of the seven from Spencer's voice. It has clever headings, a saying to close almost every section, verbless fragments and a slash. It also makes two promises that go too far: that a will avoids 'every consequence above', and that it takes 'an hour'. A worried surviving partner is told the right to take the home is 'time-bound' but not what the time is. Part IA was read in version 130 of the Act for this audit, and the proposals change no statement of law.

- [high] accuracy: A will does not avoid every consequence the article lists. Superannuation and life insurance still pass under their own rules, and a family provision claim can still be brought against a will, as the contesting article explains. 'An hour of your time' also understates what the fixed fee covers, which is an advice meeting and a separate signing appointment; the making a valid will article says one meeting and a short signing appointment. The heading 'The one-hour fix' and the closing line 'Few hours in adult life return more per minute' carry the same claim.
  Current: Heading "The one-hour fix", then: "Every consequence above is avoided by a valid will: an hour of your time, a fixed fee, and signing supervised so the formal requirements are properly met. A single will is $495 including GST. Read how to make a valid will in Victoria, then book the hour." Closing call: "The fix for intestacy costs an hour of your time: a properly drafted will, at a fixed fee quoted first."
  Proposed: Heading "How do you avoid intestacy?", then: "A valid will replaces the intestacy formula with your own decisions. We prepare it for a fixed fee and supervise the signing so the formal requirements are met, and a single will is $495 including GST, with wills for a couple $880. Read how to make a valid will in Victoria, then call us to arrange an appointment." Closing call: "A properly drafted will avoids intestacy, and a single will is a fixed fee of $495 including GST. Call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry."
- [high] answer-first: Six of the eleven headings are clever phrases rather than the plain statements or questions Spencer uses, and none of them could be quoted on its own as an answer. The question headings already on this page show the better pattern. Update the contents list along with them.
  Current: "Intestacy: the will Parliament wrote for you", "Blended families: where the formula bites", "Multiple partners, no partner, no family", "Common myths, quickly retired", "The one-hour fix", "The bottom line"
  Proposed: "What does it mean to die intestate?", "Who inherits if you leave a partner and a child from another relationship?", "Who inherits if there is more than one partner, or no partner?", "Common misunderstandings about intestacy", "How do you avoid intestacy?", and either fold the closing section into the one before it or head it "What can a will do that intestacy cannot?"
- [medium] voice: Almost every section ends on a saying, which the voice guide names as a sign of machine writing. In each case the sentence before has already made the point, so most of these can simply go.
  Current: "For many young families this approximates what a will would have said, which lulls people into thinking intestacy is harmless. The illusion ends at the next scenario." "Either way: conflict is built into the arithmetic, between the two groups of people you loved most." "Intestacy does not avoid disputes; it invites them." "Intestacy is not a neutral default: it is a distribution scheme with opinions, and they may not be yours. It cannot protect a vulnerable child, provide for a friend, steer a business, or keep peace in a blended family. Everything it does badly, a one-hour will does well. Few hours in adult life return more per minute."
  Proposed: First passage: "For many young families that is close to what a will would have said, which leads people to think intestacy is harmless, but the position changes once there is a child from another relationship." Delete "Either way: conflict is built into the arithmetic..." and "Intestacy does not avoid disputes; it invites them." Closing passage: "The intestacy formula cannot protect a vulnerable child, provide for a friend, deal sensibly with a family business or reduce the risk of conflict in a blended family. A will can do each of those things, and a single will is a fixed fee of $495 including GST."
- [medium] voice: This is one long paragraph of bold quotations and fragment answers. It contains a slash and a gendered 'she', and two quips: 'Intestacy law does not check birthdays' and 'which should bother you almost as much'. The legal test inside it is right and stays word for word.
  Current: "Everything automatically goes to my spouse anyway." Only in the simplest family shape. "We're not married, so she gets nothing / everything." Neither is automatic. ... Even then, proving the partnership, under grief, is its own ordeal. "I'm young." Intestacy law does not check birthdays. "The government takes it all." Almost never, but the government does decide who gets it, which should bother you almost as much.
  Proposed: Many people assume that everything goes to their spouse anyway. That is true only in the simplest family shape, where you leave one partner and every child is also your partner's child. Unmarried couples often assume either that the surviving partner gets nothing or that they get everything, and neither is automatic. An unmarried partner inherits like a spouse only if the relationship was formally registered, or the two of you were living together as a couple when you died and had done so continuously for at least two years, or were living together as a couple and have a child together who was under 18 at the time of death. A partner who meets none of those tests inherits nothing under the formula, and even one who does may have to prove the relationship while grieving. Young people often assume they do not need a will yet, but the formula applies whatever your age. And the government almost never takes the estate, although the law, not you, decides who does.
- [medium] legal-query: The reader most at risk is told the right is 'time-bound' but not what the time limit is. Sections 70O to 70R of the Administration and Probate Act 1958, version 130, were read for this audit. They give a partner in the blended family case a right to elect to acquire property from the estate, which must be exercised within three months of the grant if the partner is the administrator. Otherwise it must be exercised within three months of the written notice the administrator must give within 30 days of the grant, unless the court extends the time. Stating this would also give the mid article call a real deadline to follow. The candidate below is for Spencer to check before use; the Sources line already cites Part IA.
  Current: Partners should take advice before agreeing to anything about the home, because the statutory right to elect to take it is valuable and time-bound.
  Proposed: Candidate for Spencer's check: "Partners should take advice before agreeing to anything about the home. The right to elect to acquire estate property such as the home generally has to be exercised within three months of the grant of letters of administration if the partner is the administrator, or otherwise within three months of the written notice the administrator must give the partner within 30 days of the grant, unless the court extends the time."
- [medium] voice: 'Perhaps you are reading this...' and 'Take heart:' are staged openings, 'the path is well worn' is a cliché, and 'where marriage and birth certificates do real work' has to be read twice.
  Current: Perhaps you are reading this after a death, not before one. Take heart: intestate estates are administered every week, and the path is well worn. Establish who is entitled to apply for letters of administration, gather proof of the family tree, where marriage and birth certificates do real work, and do not distribute anything until entitlements are certain and the family provision window has passed.
  Proposed: If you are reading this after a death, it may help to know that intestate estates are administered every week and the steps are well settled. Establish who is entitled to apply for letters of administration, gather proof of the family tree, including marriage and birth certificates, and do not distribute anything until entitlements are certain and the family provision period has passed.
- [low] legal-query: The lead and the FAQ say someone must apply for letters of administration. The probate service page says not every estate needs a grant, and that a bank or other holder may release money or personal property up to a set threshold without one; the probate article has a whole section on whether a grant is needed. The lead is the sentence answer engines quote, so the two should agree. Spencer to confirm; the candidate only softens 'must'.
  Current: Lead: "Someone must also apply to the Supreme Court for letters of administration." Body and FAQ: "With no executor appointed, someone, usually the partner or a child, must apply to the Supreme Court for letters of administration."
  Proposed: Candidate: "Someone will usually need to apply to the Supreme Court for letters of administration."
- [low] voice: Fragments led by bold words and strung together with two colons each ('the formula pays cash outright, ready or not', 'all just assets in the pool'), where a few plain sentences would read better. 'See super death benefits' also sits awkwardly in the middle of a sentence.
  Current: "Structure: no testamentary trusts, no tax-efficient design for minors, no protection for a vulnerable beneficiary: the formula pays cash outright, ready or not." And "Sentiment: the ring, the letters, the business you built with one child: all just assets in the pool."
  Proposed: Intestacy cannot do several things a will does. It cannot appoint a guardian for children under 18, so that question is left to family agreement or, failing that, to the family law courts. It cannot set up a testamentary trust, with the protection and tax treatment one can offer, or protect a vulnerable beneficiary, because the formula gives each person their share outright. It cannot leave a particular item, such as a ring, letters or a business you built with one child, to a person of your choosing, because everything goes into the pool the formula divides. Superannuation and life insurance often pass outside the estate under their own rules, although a benefit paid to the estate joins the pool and follows the formula.
- [low] voice: A question asked only so it can be answered, followed by a sentence whose first half has no verb.
  Current: No partner? The estate passes in fixed order: first to children equally, with a deceased child's share passing to their own children, then to parents, siblings, grandparents, aunts and uncles, and cousins. No relatives inside the categories at all, and the estate passes to the Crown, the State of Victoria.
  Proposed: If you leave no partner, the estate passes in a fixed order: first to children equally, with a deceased child's share passing to their own children, then to parents, siblings, grandparents, aunts and uncles, and cousins. If you leave no relative within those categories, the estate passes to the Crown, which means the State of Victoria.
- [low] voice: A colon sets up a reveal, followed by three adjectives where one plain sentence would do, and 'utterly indifferent' is overwritten. Part IA was inserted by Act No. 41 of 2017, so 'rewritten in 2017' is exact.
  Current: The formula was modernised in 2017 to reflect contemporary families, and it is more generous to partners than the old law, but it remains a formula: rigid, impersonal, and utterly indifferent to promises, estrangements, need or the mortgage your stepdaughter shares with you.
  Proposed: Victoria's intestacy rules were rewritten in 2017 and are more generous to partners than the old law, but they remain a fixed formula. It takes no account of promises you made, a family member you were estranged from, anyone's need, or the mortgage your stepdaughter shares with you.
- [low] fees: CLAUDE.md requires the $2,178 price always to be quoted as being for where there is no will, and the link should go to the fee's own anchor, /fees#fee-letters-of-administration. The first sentence says nothing specific.
  Current: One early conversation prevents most of the expensive mistakes. Where the estate is straightforward, our fixed fee for obtaining letters of administration is $2,178 including GST, as our fixed fees page sets out.
  Proposed: Where the estate is straightforward, our fixed fee for letters of administration where there is no will is $2,178 including GST, as our fixed fees page sets out. Taking advice before anything is paid out or agreed avoids the most expensive mistakes.

## insight-enduring-powers-of-attorney.html
Scores: voice 6, clarity 7, persuasion 7, answer first 6

Accurate, practical and well linked, with both fixed fees stated exactly as fees.html states them. In places it slips into stock phrasing ('peace of mind', 'if life takes a turn', 'gaps between them are where problems grow'), and several headings are labels rather than the questions people actually ask. One sentence implies a speed promise the site does not make. The VCAT statistic Spencer was asked about on 27 Oct 2026 is still on the page without a source.

- [medium] service-promise: 'Quickly' reads as a promise of speed. The site makes only three service promises: the free first call, a reply within one business day, and after hours contact for urgent matters. CLAUDE.md says not to add a fourth.
  Current: If you are worried about how an attorney is using their powers over a family member, we can advise on a VCAT application quickly.
  Proposed: If you are worried about how an attorney is using their powers over a family member, we can advise you on an application to VCAT.
- [medium] legal-query: No source is given for 'thousands of administrators each year', and the second half is a counterfactual nobody can check. The accuracy run of 27 Oct 2026 raised it with Spencer, and it has not changed since. Delete the sentence unless a figure from a VCAT annual report is read and cited.
  Current: VCAT appoints thousands of administrators each year; most of those applications would have been unnecessary if an enduring power of attorney existed.
  Proposed: Delete the sentence.
- [medium] accuracy: 'Peace of mind' is a stock phrase, and 'a contingency, not a surrender' is a contrast device. 'Many clients describe...' is a claim about clients that nobody can check. 'You remain in charge' glosses over the body's warning that once the power has started, your attorney can act without asking you. The proposal reuses the body's own verified sentence. The FAQPage schema must change in the same commit.
  Current: Yes. Appointing an attorney takes nothing away from you while you have capacity. You remain in charge; the document is a contingency, not a surrender. Many clients describe signing one as buying peace of mind for the price of a signature.
  Proposed: Yes. While you have capacity you can still make your own decisions and revoke the appointment. Once the power has started, though, your attorney can also act without asking you, so choose the start date carefully.
- [medium] consistency: 'Gaps between them are where problems grow' is a saying, and 'a different regime' is stiff. 'These documents complete a Victorian estate plan' suggests the advance care directive is part of the estate plan, but fees.html's $1,089 plan is a will, an enduring power of attorney and an appointment of a medical treatment decision maker.
  Current: Medical treatment is governed by a different regime. Under the Medical Treatment Planning and Decisions Act 2016 (Vic), you can appoint a medical treatment decision maker to consent to or refuse treatment if you cannot, and you can make an advance care directive recording your values or binding instructions about particular treatments. Together with the enduring power of attorney and your will, these documents complete a Victorian estate plan. We prepare them as a set, because gaps between them are where problems grow.
  Proposed: Medical treatment is governed by a different Act, the Medical Treatment Planning and Decisions Act 2016 (Vic). Under it you can appoint a medical treatment decision maker to consent to or refuse treatment if you cannot, and you can make an advance care directive recording your values or binding instructions about particular treatments. A will, an enduring power of attorney and an appointment of a medical treatment decision maker together make up the estate plan we prepare, and an advance care directive can be added where you want your own instructions recorded. We prepare them together so that no decision falls between them.
- [low] answer-first: The H1 promises to say who needs one, and the lead never addresses it. The strongest answer in the body is what happens without one: VCAT may appoint someone you would not have chosen. 'If that happens without one' sends the reader back to find what 'that' means, and the last clause only points ahead. The proposal is 65 words, and every fact in it is in the body.
  Current: An enduring power of attorney lets you appoint someone you trust to make financial and personal decisions for you, and continues to operate if you lose capacity. If that happens without one, no one, not even your spouse, automatically has the right to deal with your assets, and your family must apply to VCAT for an administrator or guardian. Medical decisions need a separate appointment, which we prepare alongside it.
  Proposed: An enduring power of attorney lets you appoint someone you trust to make your financial and personal decisions, and it keeps operating if you lose capacity. Without one, no one, not even your spouse, automatically has the right to deal with your assets, and your family must apply to VCAT for an administrator or guardian, who may not be the person you would have chosen.
- [low] answer-first: Five headings are labels where readers would ask questions, and 'Answers to two common questions' and 'A practical package' break the pattern used on the rest of the site. Update the contents list along with them.
  Current: "Medical decisions are separate", "Safeguards against misuse", "Keeping it current", "Supportive attorneys: help without handover", "Answers to two common questions", "A practical package"
  Proposed: "Does an enduring power of attorney cover medical decisions?", "What protects you if an attorney misuses the power?", "How do you change or revoke an enduring power of attorney?", "What is a supportive attorney?", "Common questions", "What does an enduring power of attorney cost?"
- [low] voice: The first sentence ends in a saying after a colon. The second uses the stock phrase 'if life takes a turn'.
  Current: "Tell your bank, your accountant and your family where the originals are held: a perfect document no one can find helps nobody." And "Preparing them as a set costs less than untangling the gaps later, and it means the people you trust can step in without delay if life takes a turn."
  Proposed: "Tell your bank, your accountant and your family where the originals are held, because a document nobody can find cannot be used." And "Preparing them together costs less than sorting out the gaps later, and the people you trust can act without delay if you lose capacity."
- [low] clarity: The page never spells out what VCAT stands for. The section on what happens without a power of attorney covers exactly the subject of the guardianship article, yet links that article only from the related reading list, not from the body.
  Current: Your family must apply to VCAT for the appointment of an administrator for financial matters or a guardian for personal ones.
  Proposed: Your family must apply to the Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal, known as VCAT, for the appointment of an administrator for financial matters or a guardian for personal ones. Our guide to guardianship and administration orders explains how that process works.

## insight-executor-duties.html
Scores: voice 6, clarity 6, persuasion 7, answer first 7

A useful working checklist. It names both probate fees exactly and links the probate service page and the free executor checklist. The commission section is the weak point: two sentences of 77 and 63 words that a lay executor will not follow the first time. A few lines promise outcomes. The answer on how long an executor has calls the executor's year a yardstick, which the beneficiary article of 2 Nov 2026 explains it is not.

- [high] clarity: Two sentences of 77 and 63 words carry five separate rules, and a lay executor will not follow them the first time. The proposal splits them so each sentence carries one rule; the law is unchanged, word for word.
  Current: The will may leave the executor a gift or authorise payment; otherwise, an executor may seek commission for their "pains and trouble": either with the fully informed consent of every beneficiary whose share the payment would reduce, which is only possible where all of them are adults with capacity, or by order of the court, which weighs the size of the estate, the work done and the care shown, and cannot allow more than 5 per cent. For a will signed on or after 1 November 2017, a clause in the will authorising payment counts only if the will maker gave written informed consent to it before signing. Under such a will, an executor who seeks payment on any basis must tell every beneficiary whose share it would reduce, as soon as reasonably practicable, the basis of the payment, how it is calculated, its estimated amount and their right to ask the court to review it, and an executor who does not do so is not entitled to commission or fees.
  Proposed: The will may leave the executor a gift or authorise payment. If it does neither, an executor may seek commission for their "pains and trouble" in one of two ways. The first is the fully informed consent of every beneficiary whose share the payment would reduce, which is only possible where all of them are adults with capacity. The second is an order of the court, which weighs the size of the estate, the work done and the care shown, and cannot allow more than 5 per cent. For a will signed on or after 1 November 2017, a clause in the will authorising payment counts only if the will maker gave written informed consent to it before signing. Under such a will, an executor who seeks payment on any basis must tell every beneficiary whose share it would reduce, as soon as reasonably practicable, the basis of the payment, how it is calculated, its estimated amount and their right to ask the court to review it. An executor who does not do so is not entitled to commission or fees.
- [medium] service-promise: This promises an outcome, which AGENTS.md rules out.
  Current: We work alongside the estate's accountant so nothing is missed and nothing unnecessary is paid.
  Proposed: We work alongside the estate's accountant on the final return, any estate returns and the timing of sales.
- [medium] consistency: The heading does not say how long for what. The answer calls the executor's year a yardstick, which is the reading the newer article on executors who will not distribute, verified on 2 Nov 2026, describes as a misunderstanding: section 49 protects the executor from being made to distribute within a year and sets no date for finishing. The last sentence is a saying. The proposal uses that article's verified wording. The FAQPage schema must change in the same commit; the body's mention of the customary executor's year can stay.
  Current: Heading "How long do I have?", then: "There is no fixed statutory deadline for completing an administration, but the customary yardstick is the executor's year, and beneficiaries can seek intervention where an estate drifts without reason. Steady progress, documented and communicated, is the standard the law expects."
  Proposed: Heading "How long does an executor have to finish the administration?", then: "There is no fixed deadline. Section 49 of the Act means an executor cannot be made to distribute within a year of the death, but it sets no date by which the work must be finished. The law expects an estate to be administered with reasonable diligence, and beneficiaries can seek the court's intervention where an estate drifts without reason."
- [medium] legal-query: The sentence opens with a staged colon, and 'required notices' overstates the law. The notice that protects an executor after the grant, which the probate service page calls the statutory notice inviting claims, is a protection rather than a requirement. Spencer to confirm; the candidate uses the service page's wording.
  Current: The practical rule: publish the required notices, wait out the period, resolve any claims, and only then distribute.
  Proposed: Candidate: "In practice, that means publishing the statutory notice inviting claims, waiting out the period, resolving any claims and only then distributing."
- [low] legal-query: 'Careful timing avoids' states as a rule that the tax can be avoided by choosing when to sell. Timing can affect the tax, sometimes a great deal, but it does not always avoid it. Spencer to settle the wording; it is not rewritten here.
  Current: Selling estate assets can trigger capital gains consequences that careful timing avoids.
- [low] consistency: The free first call has fixed wording across the site, 'Your first call is free, and you speak with a lawyer'. 'A first conversation that costs nothing' drifts toward the free consultation the site does not offer.
  Current: Start with our wills and estates page or call for a first conversation that costs nothing.
  Proposed: Start with our wills and estates page, or call us on (03) 9125 8355. Your first call is free, and you speak with a lawyer.
- [low] voice: 'Blessing' is a figure of speech for the court's permission, and the page never explains 'intermeddling'.
  Current: What you cannot easily do is start the job and then abandon it: renouncing after "intermeddling" needs the court's blessing.
  Proposed: What you cannot easily do is start the job and then abandon it. Once you have dealt with the estate, which the law calls intermeddling, you need the court's permission to renounce.
- [low] voice: Two short sentences where one connected sentence reads better. The second passage uses a 'from this through to that' construction and ends with a fragment after a colon, and 'almost always avoidable' overstates.
  Current: "In practice that means every significant step needs both. That works well while they agree." And "If deadlock threatens the administration, options range from one executor renouncing through to a court application: expensive, and almost always avoidable with early advice."
  Proposed: "In practice every significant step needs both of them, which works well while they agree." And "If deadlock threatens the administration, the options include one executor renouncing and, at the far end, an application to the court, which is expensive and can usually be avoided with early advice."

## insight-executor-not-distributing-estate.html

The best page of the seven and the model for the others. It answers each question in its first words, applies each section of the Act to the beneficiary's position and reads calmly. Link /fees#when-a-fixed-fee-does-not-fit.

Many executors provide the will early, and once probate is granted it becomes part of the court's record.
- [low] clarity: After 'those answers', 'It' has no clear subject, and the reader has to work out that it means the written request.

## insight-fraudulent-calumny-victoria.html
Scores: voice 6, clarity 7, persuasion 6, answer first 5

A clear account of a narrow doctrine, with a useful comparison to undue influence. Its main problems are about certainty. The first sentence of the lead states as settled Victorian law something the body says has only been allowed to go to trial. The 2026 decision is described as fact although nobody at the firm has read the judgment. The FAQ also states the test more narrowly than the body does. The mid article call interrupts the case discussion, a few staged openings remain, and the page is the only one of the seven to fail the style checker, on the brackets in the case citation.

- [high] legal-query: The body says the 2026 decision only refused to strike the claim out, that the trial is still to come, and that the doctrine was 'previously largely untested in Australia'. Yet the first sentence of the lead, which is the sentence search and answer engines quote, states as settled Victorian law that a lie can set aside a will, and the body repeats it without qualification. Spencer to settle the wording. The candidate uses only what the body already says and is 70 words.
  Current: Lead: "A deliberate lie can be grounds to set aside a will in Victoria." Body: "the resulting will, or the affected part of it, can be set aside."
  Proposed: Candidate lead: "Fraudulent calumny is a deliberate lie told to a will maker about another person that causes the will maker to leave that person out or give them less. It has long been a ground for setting aside a will in England, and in 2026 the Supreme Court of Victoria allowed such a claim to go to trial. Challenges are usually raised by caveat before probate is granted, so act quickly."
- [high] legal-query: AGENTS.md says to cite only what has been read. The ledger records that Re the Estate of Iovenitti was confirmed through the caveators' solicitors' published commentary and that the judgment itself could not be read. AustLII still returned a 403 challenge from this environment on 9 Nov 2026. The page nonetheless states the Court's findings as fact. Either Spencer reads the judgment, or the description is credited to the commentary until he does.
  Current: The Court refused, finding that the caveators had established a prima facie case worthy of investigation on all the grounds pleaded, including fraudulent calumny.
  Proposed: Until the judgment is read: "According to published commentary on the decision, the Court refused the application, finding that the caveators had established a prima facie case worthy of investigation on all the grounds pleaded, including fraudulent calumny."
- [medium] style-checker: This is the only hard failure across the seven pages. The brackets are part of the case citation, but CLAUDE.md exempts only the Vic and Cth of Act titles and the phone number. Either name the case and its year in words, or Spencer approves a checker exemption for case citations of this kind.
  Current: In Re the Estate of Iovenitti [2026] VSC 106, caveators opposing a grant of probate challenged the deceased's final will on several grounds, including fraudulent calumny.

  Proposed: A doctrine that lets a will be challenged where someone told the will maker something false and damaging about another person, knowing it was false or not caring whether it was true, causing the will maker to exclude that person or leave them less than they otherwise would have.

  Current: Has an Australian court accepted a fraudulent calumny claim? Legal commentary has described it as the first reported Victorian case to treat the doctrine as a viable ground to challenge a will. The underlying claim itself is still to be decided at trial.
  Proposed: Has a Victorian court allowed a fraudulent calumny claim to go to trial? Yes. The next sentence states that exception accurately, but 'the Act treats it as one' attributes to the statute a last resort character that comes from how courts approach winding up a solvent company. For Spencer to decide whether the clause stays, is qualified, or the sentence ends at 'Winding up is a last resort.' No rewrite of the substance is proposed.
  Current: Winding up is a last resort, and the Act treats it as one.

The proposal replaces the whole paragraph and splits its 65 word second sentence.
  Current: Spencer Alexander Lawyers acts for business owners across Victoria in disputes with a co-owner, whether you want to leave the company or keep it.
  Proposed: Spencer Alexander Lawyers acts for business owners across Victoria in disputes with a co-owner. Whether you want to leave the company or keep it, we start by reading the constitution, any shareholder agreement and the company's records. We then tell you plainly where you stand and what you can and cannot do without the other owner, and work towards the exit that keeps the most value, whether that is a negotiated buyout, a sale, mediation or, where it is warranted, an application to the court. A dispute is charged on a written estimate, set out in a costs agreement before any substantive work begins, and we revisit it with you at each stage. Learn more about our commercial law practice.
