## insight-business-structures.html
Scores: voice 4, clarity 6, persuasion 5, answer first 5

Accurate and well organised, but written in the clipped register of the early articles: verbless fragments, colon reveals such as 'The obligations are real:' and 'The constraints:', semicolon chains, 'not X; it is Y' turns and closing sayings. Three question headings are answered only in the last line of their paragraph. One fee sentence contradicts fees.html, recorded for Spencer on 20 Oct 2026 and still live; the closing FAQ hands the structure decision to the advisers rather than the client; the accountant point is made four times; and the social image alt text describes a different photograph. The trust paragraph would gain from the same rewrite pass.

- [high] fees: Contradicts fees.html, which says anything outside a published fixed fee 'gets a written fee estimate first', the commercial hub, which says transactions run on a written estimate, and the rail card on this same page. DECISIONS.md recorded it for Spencer on 20 Oct 2026 and it is still live. The paragraph also sells shareholder agreements without the price fees.html publishes.
  Current: Other defined work is quoted as a fixed fee before we start, and our commercial law page describes it.
  Proposed: A shareholders agreement is from $2,420 including GST, and any other work is estimated in writing before substantive work begins. Our commercial law page describes the full range of what we do.
- [medium] answer-first: The heading asks when sole trading makes sense, and the answer arrives only in the last line, as a verbless fragment. The paragraph is built from clipped clauses, a colon reveal and the colloquial 'and away you go'.
  Current: Trading as a sole trader means the business is simply you: an ABN, perhaps a registered business name, and away you go. Setup is near-instant and costs almost nothing; reporting is your personal tax return. The price is unlimited personal liability: every business debt and every claim is yours, reaching your house and savings. Profits are taxed at your marginal rate, with no ability to share income or retain profits at lower rates. Sensible for low-risk services testing an idea; uncomfortable the day you hire staff, sign a lease or supply anything that can fail expensively.
  Proposed: Trading as a sole trader makes sense for a low risk service business that is still testing an idea, and it becomes uncomfortable the day you hire staff, sign a lease or supply anything that could fail expensively. The business is simply you, trading under an ABN and perhaps a registered business name, so it costs almost nothing to set up and its income goes on your personal tax return. The cost of that simplicity is unlimited personal liability, because every business debt and every claim against the business is yours and can reach your house and your savings. Profits are taxed at your marginal rate, and you cannot share that income or keep profits in the business at a lower rate.
- [medium] answer-first: The heading asks for the risks; the first two sentences define a partnership and the risk comes third, behind a colon reveal. 'Not optional paperwork; it is the difference between a disagreement and a catastrophe' is the 'not X, it is Y' turn and an overstatement the voice standard rules out.
  Current: A partnership arises when people carry on business in common with a view to profit, in Victoria under the Partnership Act 1958. It is cheap and flexible, and profits flow straight to the partners' returns. The structural flaw is joint liability: each partner can bind the firm, and each is personally exposed to the partnership's debts, including those created by the other partner. A written partnership agreement covering decisions, money, disputes and exits is not optional paperwork; it is the difference between a disagreement and a catastrophe. Many groups that begin as partnerships mature into companies or trusts once real value exists.
  Proposed: The main risk of a partnership is joint liability. Each partner can bind the firm, and each is personally exposed to the partnership's debts, including debts created by the other partner. A partnership arises when people carry on business in common with a view to profit, in Victoria under the Partnership Act 1958, and it is cheap and flexible, with profits flowing straight to the partners' own tax returns. A written partnership agreement covering decisions, money, disputes and exits is essential, because it sets out in advance what happens when partners disagree or one of them wants to leave. Many groups that begin as partnerships become companies or trusts once the business has real value.
- [medium] clarity: 'Those duties, care and diligence, good faith and no insolvent trading, carry personal consequences' reads as a list of five things and needs rereading. Two colon reveals, the figure of speech 'quietly re-connects', and 'now needs', which dates a 2021 change.
  Current: The obligations are real: registration and annual fees to ASIC, statutory records, and directors' duties. Those duties, care and diligence, good faith and no insolvent trading, carry personal consequences. Every director now needs a director identification number, and must generally apply for one before appointment. And limited liability has practical limits: landlords and banks routinely demand personal guarantees from small-company directors, which quietly re-connects the company's risk to your home unless negotiated.
  Proposed: A company also brings obligations: registration and annual fees to ASIC, statutory records and directors' duties. Those duties include care and diligence, good faith and the duty to prevent insolvent trading, and a breach carries personal consequences for the director. Every director needs a director identification number and must generally apply for one before being appointed. Limited liability also has practical limits, because landlords and banks routinely require personal guarantees from the directors of small companies, and unless its terms are negotiated, a guarantee puts your home within reach of the landlord or the bank if the company does not pay.
- [medium] answer-first: The third question is a fragment and its answer opens with one. Its last sentence makes the structure 'a joint decision for your lawyer and accountant', when the decision is the client's, made on their advice. The first answer uses the 'not X; it is Y' turn and the second closes on a saying. The FAQPage schema in the head must change with the visible questions and answers.
  Current: Trust or company for my family business? / Usually a question of who takes the profits and how stable they are. Flexible family distributions favour a trust; retained earnings, outside investors and employee share plans favour a company. It is a joint decision for your lawyer and accountant. / Often, yes: that is a rational sequence for low-risk ventures, and restructure pathways exist. The mistake is not starting simple; it is staying simple after hiring staff, signing a lease or taking real commercial risk. / A company narrows risk; conduct and guarantees decide the rest.
  Proposed: Should my family business use a trust or a company? / It usually depends on who takes the profits and how stable those profits are. Flexible distributions among family members favour a trust, while retained earnings, outside investors and employee share plans favour a company. The decision is yours, and it is best made on advice from your lawyer and your accountant together. / Often, yes. Starting as a sole trader is sensible for a low risk venture, and the business can move into a company or trust later. The mistake is staying a sole trader after you hire staff, sign a lease or take on real commercial risk. / A company limits your exposure, but it does not protect you from the consequences of your own conduct as a director or from a guarantee you have signed.
- [medium] persuasion: The heading asks 'Is there one right structure?' and the answer never says no; it closes on a metaphor. In the closing prompt, 'We set structures' is not idiomatic, and the one published price for this work is missing where the reader decides.
  Current: There is no universally right structure, only the right structure for this business, this family and this risk, reviewed as each of those changes. Choose deliberately at the start, revisit at every growth milestone, and treat the modest cost of restructuring as what it is: insurance priced far below the risks it retires. / Starting out, or outgrowing the structure you started with? We set structures alongside your accountant.
  Proposed: No. The right structure is the one that fits this business, the family behind it and the risks it carries, and it should be reviewed whenever any of those changes. Choose it deliberately at the start, look at it again each time the business grows, and weigh the modest cost of restructuring against the personal exposure it removes. / Starting out, or outgrowing the structure you started with? We set up and restructure businesses alongside your accountant, and setting up a company is a fixed fee of $1,210 including GST and the ASIC registration fee.
- [low] answer-first: A stock signpost that ends the lead, which is also the speakable text, on nothing. The lead answers the question without it.
  Current: Here is how they compare, and how we help.
  Proposed: Delete the sentence, so the lead ends on 'and a layer of asset protection.' at 61 words.
- [low] voice: A semicolon list after a colon, two colon reveals and a saying. 'Shielding the humans' is flippant and overstates the protection, since section 197 of the Corporations Act makes the directors of a corporate trustee liable for trust debts in some cases; the rewrite describes the arrangement without the claim. The accountant point is made three more times on the page, so the last sentence can go.
  Current: Mature structures are usually combinations: a trading company owned by a family trust; premises or intellectual property held in a separate entity and licensed to the trader; a corporate trustee with a nominal-asset company shielding the humans. The design principle is simple: separate the assets from the risk. The execution is where legal and tax advice must be in the same room. We work alongside your accountant so the legal structure and the tax position pull in the same direction.
  Proposed: As a business grows, its structure usually becomes a combination of entities. Common examples are a trading company owned by a family trust, premises or intellectual property held in a separate entity and licensed to the trading business, and a company with no significant assets of its own acting as trustee in place of individuals. The principle is to keep valuable assets away from the entity that carries the trading risk, and getting that right needs legal and tax advice given together.
- [low] clarity: A turnover cannot be predated, so the last sentence needs rereading, and the first is a colon reveal.
  Current: The worst path is drift: carrying a grown business's risks on a start-up's structure because changing felt like admin. If the last structural conversation predates your current turnover, it is due.
  Proposed: The real risk is drift, where a grown business keeps carrying its risks on the structure it started with because changing it felt like paperwork. If nobody has reviewed your structure since the business reached its current size, a review is due.
- [low] factual: The social image alt text in the head describes a different photograph. The image, 1499744349893, is the workshop wall that the in-page alt text describes correctly; I viewed it to confirm.
  Current: og:image:alt content="Two founders working side by side at laptops in a bright office"
  Proposed: Hand tools on the walls and shelves of a small workshop
- [low] legal-query: The page states tax rules: the company rate against the top personal rates, the penal rate on undistributed trust income, rollover concessions on a genuine restructure, and director penalty notices for some tax debts, as well as director liability for unpaid employee entitlements. None of the Acts behind those statements is named in the Sources line, and the 20 Oct 2026 verification read only the two Acts listed. For Spencer: name and read them, or generalise those sentences. No rewrite of the substance proposed.
  Current: Sources: Partnership Act 1958 (Vic), sections 5, 9 and 13; Corporations Act 2001 (Cth), sections 119, 124, 180, 181, 516, 588G, 1272B and 1272C.

## insight-buying-a-business-victoria.html

It sets out information about the business, including its financial performance.

For Spencer to settle one wording for both pages after reading section 52 in the authorised version. No rewrite of the substance proposed.

Split the paragraph into three, keep every statement of law as it stands, and link the newer guide.
  Current: Two disciplines make terms enforceable: they must be accepted before the work, on the quote or credit application and not on the invoice that follows, and they must match how you actually trade. For most small businesses there is a third, fairness:
  Proposed: Your terms and conditions govern every sale: when payment falls due, interest and recovery costs on late accounts, who owns goods until payment, a retention of title clause registered on the PPSR to survive a customer's insolvency, limits on your liability, warranty scope consistent with the Australian Consumer Law, and how disputes are handled. Most payment disputes are won or lost on them. [new paragraph] Two things make terms enforceable. They must be accepted before the work, on the quote or credit application and not on the invoice that follows, and they must match how you actually trade. For most small businesses they must also be fair: under the Australian Consumer Law, an unfair term in a standard form contract is void where the customer is a consumer or either party is a small business, meaning one employing fewer than 100 people or with turnover under $10 million in its last income year. Since 9 November 2023, proposing or relying on such a term can itself attract a penalty, so clauses limiting liability or charging interest and recovery costs need drafting with that test in mind. Our guide to whether you can use a template for your terms and conditions explains those rules in more detail. [new paragraph] When a contract does go wrong, the Victorian Small Business Commission offers low cost dispute resolution, with guidance for small businesses on its website. See our companion guide to recovering unpaid debts.

Co-ownership without a written constitution is the leading cause of the disputes that end businesses.

The most common commercial dispute we see is between co-owners who never agreed these things in writing, and our guide to falling out with a business partner explains what happens then. A shareholders agreement is from $2,420 including GST. / Yes. If your company has more than one shareholder, a shareholder agreement is the most important document you can sign, because it settles how decisions are made, how a deadlock is broken, how dividends are paid, and how an owner exits and at what value. A personal guarantee puts your personal assets, including your home, behind the company's debts, and it often lasts longer than you expect, so have it reviewed before you sign.

## insight-debt-recovery-victoria.html
Scores: voice 4, clarity 6, persuasion 6, answer first 6

The legal skeleton is right and the mid article prompt sits after the 21 day limit, addressing both creditor and served company. The prose is clipped and slogan driven: 'toolkit', 'intelligence-driven', 'ninety cents fast rather than one hundred cents never', 'systems, not moods'. The statutory demand section uses the Act's word 'compound' where the hub says 'compromise', and leaves out the filing and service step that a company just served most needs. The $495 letter of demand appears only near the end, one sentence treats a guarantee as security, two answers do not answer their headings first, and the first call paragraph comes close to a fourth service promise.

- [medium] clarity: 'Compound' is the Act's word, but most readers will take it for compound interest. The commercial hub and faq.html say 'reach a compromise to the creditor's reasonable satisfaction', which matches section 459E(2)(c). The last sentence is a stock signpost in the speakable lead. The same 'compound' appears in the statutory demand section and should change with it.
  Current: Where the debt is $4,000 or more and not genuinely disputed, that demand gives the company 21 days to pay, secure or compound it or apply to set the demand aside. We explain each step and its honest economics.
  Proposed: Where the debt is $4,000 or more and not genuinely disputed, that demand gives the company 21 days to pay the debt, secure it or reach a compromise to the creditor's reasonable satisfaction, or apply to set the demand aside. This leaves the lead at 69 words. / In the statutory demand section: Served under the Corporations Act, it gives the company 21 days to pay the debt, secure it or reach a compromise to the creditor's reasonable satisfaction, or apply to set the demand aside.
- [medium] persuasion: 'A remarkable share' is a claim nobody can source, and the paragraph is clipped. The $495 fixed fee, the most persuasive fact for a reader at this step, appears only near the end of the article; the closing prompt could carry it too. The opening borrows the hub's published wording.
  Current: A lawyer's letter of demand resolves a remarkable share of debts without proceedings. It states the debt, the basis, the deadline and the consequences, and it signals, cheaply, that you are organised and serious. Tone matters: firm, precise, professional. So does accuracy, because an inflated or aggressive demand hands the debtor a grievance. Where the debtor has a genuine dispute, the letter often surfaces it early, which is itself valuable: better to learn the defence now than after filing fees. / Owed money and tired of excuses? We will tell you candidly whether the debt is worth pursuing, then pursue it.
  Proposed: A letter of demand from a lawyer often resolves a debt without proceedings, or opens the way to a sensible payment plan. It states the debt, its basis, the deadline and what will follow if it is not paid, and it shows the debtor, at little cost, that you are organised and serious. It needs to be firm, precise and accurate, because an inflated or aggressive demand hands the debtor a grievance. Where the debtor has a genuine dispute, the letter often brings it out early, and it is far better to learn of the defence now than after you have paid the court's filing fees. For an undisputed debt, our letter of demand is a fixed fee of $495 including GST. / Owed money and tired of excuses? We will tell you plainly whether the debt is worth pursuing, and a letter of demand for an undisputed debt is a fixed fee of $495 including GST.
- [medium] legal-query: The callout below speaks to a company that has just been served, but the article omits section 459G(2) and (3), read today in compilation 149: the application counts only if, within the 21 days, the supporting affidavit is filed and copies are served on the creditor. The commercial hub and faq.html both say so, and add that the deadline cannot be extended; that point rests on case law rather than the Act's text, so whether to include it is for Spencer under the legislation-only Sources rule. Proposed addition after this sentence, statutory content only, to be checked by the article routine before publishing.
  Current: Served under the Corporations Act, it gives the company 21 days to pay, secure or compound the debt, or apply to set the demand aside.
  Proposed: An application to set the demand aside can only be made within those 21 days, and only if, within that time, the supporting affidavit is filed with the court and copies of the application and the affidavit are served on the creditor.
- [medium] legal-query: A registered retention of title can give a supplier a security interest that holds in the customer's liquidation. A director's guarantee is not security over the company's property; it gives a personal claim against the guarantor, pursued outside the liquidation. The sentence treats both as 'secured recovery'. For Spencer to restate, probably by describing the two separately. No rewrite of the substance proposed.
  Current: having your retention of title registered on the PPSR and your account supported by guarantees is the difference between secured recovery and an unsecured dividend.
- [medium] firm-facts: This promises what the free first call delivers, a recommendation and a costed plan. That goes beyond the three service promises and beyond how fees.html describes the first call, which 'tells you whether your matter fits a fixed fee, and what it will cost if it does not', with the fee confirmed in writing afterwards.
  Current: With it in front of us, the first conversation ends with a recommendation and a costed plan rather than a list of things to go and find.
  Proposed: With that record in front of us, the first call can turn straight to what happens next rather than what is missing.
- [medium] answer-first: The heading asks how quickly to act, and the answer, the timetable, is the last clause. Before it come a slogan, 'leverage', which the checker flags, 'sporadic bursts of fury' and 'director-level'. Periods of days mix words and figures, against the page's own '21 days'.
  Current: Speed is the most underrated collection tool. Recovery prospects decay with every month an invoice ages: the debtor's position worsens, other creditors move first, and your leverage as a continuing supplier evaporates once the relationship ends. A disciplined ladder recovers more than sporadic bursts of fury ever will: reminder at seven days, director-level call at fourteen, lawyer's demand at twenty-one, proceedings or statutory demand on a fixed date you honour.
  Proposed: Act as soon as an invoice is overdue, and follow a fixed timetable: a reminder at 7 days, a call to a director at 14, a lawyer's letter of demand at 21, and proceedings or a statutory demand on a date you set and keep. Recovery prospects fall with every month an invoice ages, because the debtor's position worsens, other creditors move first, and your bargaining power as a continuing supplier disappears once the relationship ends. A timetable you keep to will recover more than occasional bursts of chasing.
- [medium] answer-first: The question is 'Do I wait?' and the answer never says no; it opens with jargon and closes on a saying. The FAQPage schema must change with it.
  Current: Convert promises into instruments: a signed payment plan with default consequences, security, or post-dated commitments. A debtor who resists documenting a promise has told you its value.
  Proposed: No, not on a promise alone. Ask for a signed payment plan that says what happens if an instalment is missed, for security, or for payments committed to fixed dates, and if the debtor will not put the promise in writing, treat that refusal as your answer.
- [low] voice: 'Grounding a creditor's petition' and 'deserve cold assessment' are stiff, and the paragraph closes on a slogan. The slogan also ignores that a payment pressed out of a debtor who then becomes bankrupt can be recovered by the trustee as a preference, which Spencer may want to weigh before any wording prefers pressure to process. The rewrite keeps the law and drops the slogan.
  Current: Against individual debtors, a final judgment of $10,000 or more supports a bankruptcy notice; non-compliance within the time allowed is an act of bankruptcy grounding a creditor's petition. The threat is potent, because bankruptcy reaches homes and livelihoods, but the economics deserve cold assessment: in bankruptcy you may share with every other creditor. Pressure that produces payment beats process that produces a dividend.
  Proposed: Against an individual, a final judgment of $10,000 or more supports a bankruptcy notice, and if the debtor does not comply within the time the notice allows, that failure is an act of bankruptcy on which a creditor's petition can be based. The threat is a serious one, because bankruptcy reaches homes and livelihoods, but weigh the economics carefully, because once the debtor is bankrupt you may have to share what is recovered with every other creditor.
- [low] legal-query: The FAQ says the costs scale keeps modest claims economic, and the economics section says scales return only part of what you spend, so a reader cannot tell whether the scale helps or hurts them. For Spencer to say which he means. No rewrite of the substance proposed.
  Current: Scaled costs in the Magistrates' Court keep modest claims economic, and interest and recovery-cost clauses in good terms of trade shift much of the burden to the debtor. / Court scales return only part of actual costs; a judgment-proof debtor converts victory into expense.
- [low] clarity: The heading asks how to enforce a judgment, and the first sentence does not say. 'Toolkit', 'intelligence-driven' and 'garnishee timed to invoice cycles' are jargon, and the closing line needs rereading.
  Current: A judgment is a legal fact; enforcement converts it into cash. The Victorian toolkit includes: / Enforcement choices are intelligence-driven: a debtor with employment suits attachment; a company with a trading account suits garnishee timed to invoice cycles.
  Proposed: A judgment does not pay itself, and if the debtor still does not pay, you have to enforce it. In Victoria the main ways of doing so are: / The right step depends on what you know about the debtor's assets. An attachment of earnings suits a debtor who is employed, and an attachment of debts suits a company with money moving through its trading account, timed for when its own customers usually pay it.
- [low] voice: Slogans and metaphors where plain statements belong: 'converts victory into expense', 'ninety cents fast rather than one hundred cents never', 'prevention compounds', 'tomorrow's bad-debt book', 'systems, not moods', 'ladder' and 'rung', 'a process, not a crisis'. The closing heading asks how, and the answer is an aphorism.
  Current: Court scales return only part of actual costs; a judgment-proof debtor converts victory into expense. Sometimes the commercial answer is a negotiated instalment plan with security, or settling at ninety cents fast rather than one hundred cents never. And prevention compounds: credit checks on new customers, deposits, retention of title registered on the PPSR, and guarantees for company customers shrink tomorrow's bad-debt book. / Debts are recovered by systems, not moods: tight terms at the front, a fixed escalation ladder in the middle, and honest cost-benefit decisions at every rung. Build the system once and the next unpaid invoice becomes a process, not a crisis.
  Proposed: A costs order on the court's scale returns only part of what you actually spend, and a judgment against a debtor with nothing to take costs you money rather than recovering it. Sometimes the better commercial answer is an instalment plan backed by security, or accepting 90 cents in the dollar now rather than pursuing the full amount and recovering nothing. Prevention matters as much: credit checks on new customers, deposits, retention of title registered on the PPSR and guarantees from the directors of company customers all reduce the debts you will be chasing next year. / Reliable recovery comes from terms of trade agreed before the work, a fixed timetable for chasing every overdue account, and a decision at each step about whether the next one is worth its cost. Once that is in place, the next unpaid invoice is handled as routine rather than as an emergency.

## insight-directors-duties-victoria.html
Scores: voice 7, clarity 7, persuasion 6, answer first 8

Accurate and well organised. Each question heading is answered in its first sentence, a mid article prompt comes straight after the insolvent trading section, and the register is plain, connected and close to Spencer's exemplar. The faults are specific. The lead promises four duties and lists three. One FAQ answer reads as though a liquidator or shareholder can seek disqualification. One cross reference sends readers to a guide that never mentions guarantees. There are two 'is where it begins' sayings, a safe harbour passage that repeats the safe harbour guide in two sentences of more than 50 words, and a generic How we help. check_style.py: PASS with no notes.

- [medium] clarity: The lead promises four duties but lists three items, because the last item folds two duties into one, so a reader counting has to go back. This is the speakable lead that answer engines quote. The proposed sentence names the four duties as the body's own list labels them, uses the Act's term 'general duties' and keeps the lead at exactly 70 words, the gate's cap.
  Current: Every company director in Victoria owes four core statutory duties: care and diligence, good faith in the company's best interests and for a proper purpose, and no improper use of their position or company information.
  Proposed: Every company director in Victoria owes four general duties: care and diligence, good faith in the company's best interests and for a proper purpose, proper use of their position, and proper use of company information.
- [medium] legal-query: Read naturally, this FAQ answer says a company, a liquidator or a shareholder can pursue disqualification. Only the regulator can seek that, and the body already says so correctly. The proposed wording keeps the legal substance: section 1317J(1) of the Corporations Act, read in the authorised compilation during this audit, lets ASIC apply for a compensation order, so the regulator stays in the compensation list. Spencer should confirm the wording. The FAQPage schema must change with the visible answer.
  Current: The company, a liquidator, sometimes a shareholder, or the regulator can pursue compensation or disqualification from managing companies.
  Proposed: The company, a liquidator, sometimes a shareholder and the regulator can pursue the director for compensation, and the regulator can seek an order disqualifying the director from managing companies.
- [medium] accuracy: This cross reference promises something the reader will not find. The buying a business guide never mentions personal guarantees; the word guarantee does not appear anywhere on that page. The retail leases guide does answer how far a guarantee of a lease reaches, so link that guide instead, or cut the sentence.
  Current: Our guide to buying a business in Victoria touches on the personal guarantees that often extend a director's exposure beyond the duties themselves.
  Proposed: A personal guarantee often extends a director's exposure beyond the duties themselves, and our guide to retail leases in Victoria explains how far a director's guarantee of a lease usually reaches.
- [medium] overlap: The safe harbour passage is two sentences of more than 50 words each, and it repeats the safe harbour guide. That includes the clause on the director's burden to prove safe harbour with records, which the guide also carries in its FAQ. The insolvent trading definition just above shares its operative wording with the guide word for word. Keep the rule and its conditions on this page and let the dedicated guide carry the rest. Every legal statement that stays is unchanged.
  Current: It is generally not available while the company is behind on paying employee entitlements, including superannuation, or on lodging the returns and other documents the tax law requires, and it is for the director to point to evidence that the safe harbour applies, so records of the advice taken and the steps followed matter. It rewards directors who confront a problem early and get proper advice, rather than directors who look away and hope trading conditions improve, and our guide to safe harbour and small business restructuring explains what that protection asks of a director and the options open to a smaller company.
  Proposed: It is generally not available while the company is behind on paying employee entitlements, including superannuation, or on lodging the returns and other documents the tax law requires. It rewards the director who acts early and takes proper advice, and our guide to safe harbour and small business restructuring explains what else it asks of a director, including the records that prove it applies, and the options open to a smaller company.
- [low] voice: Two lines turn the point into a saying ('is where the exposure begins', 'is where disputes tend to start'). Spencer's voice allows that move at most once in an article. The first also needs a second reading, because the 'rather than' clause sits between the subject and the verb. Keep the existing disputes link on the words 'a dispute'.
  Current: Continuing to trade, order stock or accept customer deposits after the warning signs appear, rather than at the point of formal insolvency, is usually where the exposure begins. ... A related party transaction handled transparently rarely causes trouble. The same transaction pushed through quietly is where disputes, and sometimes claims for breach of duty, tend to start.
  Proposed: The exposure usually begins when a company keeps trading, ordering stock or taking customer deposits after the warning signs appear, not when a formal insolvency process starts. ... A related party transaction handled transparently rarely causes trouble, but the same transaction pushed through quietly often leads to a dispute, and sometimes to a claim for breach of duty.

  Current: It is common for one set of facts, a related party deal pushed through while the company was already in financial difficulty, for example, to raise more than one of these consequences at the same time.
  Proposed: One set of facts often raises more than one of these consequences at the same time, for example a related party deal pushed through while the company was already in financial difficulty.
- [low] persuasion: Generic copy. It uses a 'from ... to' sweep and abstract phrases ('sound governance', 'where the real exposure sits') that would fit any firm's page. Say what the firm does for a director, in the order a worried director needs it, in the firm's plural voice.
  Current: Spencer Alexander Lawyers advises directors and companies across Melbourne and Victoria on their statutory duties, from setting up sound governance and related party processes at the start, to responding when a company is trading in difficult conditions or a director faces a claim over past conduct. We help directors understand where the real exposure sits, take the steps that protect both the company and its directors personally, and act quickly where insolvency risk is emerging.

We set up the conflict and related party processes a company needs at the start, advise directors whose company is trading in difficult conditions, and act for a director who faces a claim over past conduct. Where insolvency is a risk, we tell you where your personal exposure lies, take the steps that protect the company and its directors, and act quickly.

  Current: Options to renew are the tenant's asset: diarise the exercise window religiously, because a missed option date can end a profitable business's tenure, and landlords must give notice of the last date; if that notice comes late, the last date moves to three months after it is given, but it is safer not to rely on the landlord's notice.

It is the cheapest insurance in commercial law. Details at our commercial law page, and see the contracts every Victorian business should have for the rest of your paperwork.

  Proposed: Retail lease disputes in Victoria go first to the Victorian Small Business Commission, whose low-cost mediation resolves many disputes without litigation, and VCAT hears what mediation cannot settle.

  Current: Victorian retail tenants enjoy some of the strongest leasing protections in the country, but every one of them works better asserted before signature than litigated after. Read the disclosure statement like the financial document it is, negotiate the exit clauses while you have leverage, and never let an option date pass unwatched. ... Read the disclosure statement as closely as you would a set of accounts, negotiate the exit clauses while you still have bargaining power, and never let an option date pass unwatched. ... Read the repair clause against the outgoings schedule before signing, because together they show which repair costs the landlord can pass on to you. ... The statement must be prepared to accounting standards and come with a registered company auditor's report, unless the outgoings are limited to GST, water and council rates, insurance and other prescribed items with proof of payment. 'Audited-style statements' undersells that, and a tenant cannot tell what it means.

## insight-safe-harbour-small-business-restructuring.html
Scores: voice 7, clarity 7, persuasion 6, answer first 8

Strong, flowing and close to the exemplar, with an answer first lead, question headings, a clear comparison of the two paths and two call prompts. The biggest gap is the statutory demand. A director reading this page may well be holding one, yet the page says only that ignoring it 'has serious consequences', links to the creditor's guide and leaves out the 21 day deadline that the hub and faq.html state. The other problems are smaller: a 69 word sentence, two staged openings, a 236 word paragraph, one comparison with no source and a tangled How we help. check_style.py: PASS, with one note for the 69 word sentence.

- [high] persuasion: A director with a statutory demand is one of the readers this page is most likely to have, and the 21 day deadline is the most urgent fact they face and the strongest reason to call. The page names no deadline and links only to the creditor's guide. The proposed wording is the commercial hub's own copy, from its urgent strip and its debt recovery answer, so it is already on the site; read it once more against the Act before publishing. Link 'what to do when you receive a statutory demand' to /faq#q-i-have-received-a-statutory-demand-what-should-i-do. Once a time limit is stated, the house rule puts a call prompt straight after this section, so move the existing prompt here or add a second one.
  Current: A statutory demand deserves particular respect, because ignoring one has serious consequences for the company; our guide to debt recovery in Victoria explains how those demands work from the creditor's side.
  Proposed: A statutory demand needs action the day it arrives. A company served with one has 21 days to pay, secure or compromise the debt, or to apply to set the demand aside, and an application can only be made within those 21 days. If the company does neither, a court hearing a winding up application can presume it is insolvent. Our answer on what to do when you receive a statutory demand sets out the first steps, and our guide to debt recovery in Victoria explains how those demands work from the creditor's side.
- [medium] clarity: The section opens with a fragment and then runs a 69 word sentence, which the checker flags, listing five statutory markers after a colon. Splitting it keeps every marker as written and lets the reader take them in on one reading.
  Current: More than good intentions. In judging whether a course of action was reasonably likely to lead to a better outcome, the law points to practical markers: whether the director kept properly informed of the company's financial position, kept proper books and records, took steps to prevent misconduct by officers and employees, obtained advice from an appropriately qualified adviser, and was developing or implementing a plan to restructure the company and improve its position.
  Proposed: It requires more than good intentions. In judging whether a course of action was reasonably likely to lead to a better outcome, the law points to practical markers. They include whether the director kept properly informed of the company's financial position, kept proper books and records and took steps to prevent misconduct by officers and employees. They also include whether the director obtained advice from an appropriately qualified adviser and was developing or implementing a plan to restructure the company and improve its position.
- [low] voice: Two staged openings build up to the point, one with a colon and one with a 'what sets it apart is' frame, where Spencer would state the point directly.
  Current: The practical point is blunt: once you have real grounds to suspect the company may be insolvent, doing nothing is the one response the law punishes most reliably. ... What sets it apart from voluntary administration is control. The directors keep running the business day to day while the plan is prepared and voted on, instead of handing the company over to an external administrator.
  Proposed: Once you have real grounds to suspect the company may be insolvent, doing nothing is the response the law punishes most reliably. ... Unlike a voluntary administration, small business restructuring leaves the directors running the business day to day while the plan is prepared and voted on, instead of handing the company over to an external administrator.
- [low] structure: The answer to 'What is the safe harbour?' is one 236 word block. It covers why the protection exists, what it covers and the four ways it ends or is lost, which is hard to take in on a phone.
  Current: Nothing is filed and nothing is made public: safe harbour is not a formal insolvency process but a shield around a genuine rescue attempt. It begins when the work on the plan begins, and it ends if the director does not take the course of action within a reasonable period
  Proposed: No wording change: start a new paragraph at 'It begins when the work on the plan begins', so that what the safe harbour covers and when it ends are read as two ideas.
- [low] accuracy: 'More companies than the liabilities cap does' is a comparison with no source. The point stands without it, and the colon reveal becomes a plain 'because'.
  Current: The entitlements and lodgement conditions trip up more companies than the liabilities cap does: a company that has drifted behind on superannuation or activity statements often needs to bring that position current before its plan can be proposed.
  Proposed: The entitlements and lodgement conditions catch many companies out, because a company that has drifted behind on superannuation or activity statements often needs to bring that position current before its plan can be proposed.
- [low] clarity: The first sentence nests a conditional inside a list of four things and needs rereading. The closing line ('options, not obligations ... is what keeps the options open') turns the point into a saying.
  Current: We help you understand your exposure, put safe harbour protection on a proper footing with the records to prove it, work alongside your accountant and, where a formal process is the right answer, with a restructuring practitioner, and respond to creditor pressure, including statutory demands. The first conversation is usually about options, not obligations, and having it early is what keeps the options open.
  Proposed: We help you understand your exposure and put safe harbour protection on a proper footing, with the records to prove it. We work alongside your accountant and, where a formal process is the right answer, with a restructuring practitioner, and we respond to creditor pressure, including statutory demands. The first conversation is usually about the options open to the company, and the earlier it happens, the more of them there are.

## insight-shareholder-agreements.html
Scores: voice 4, clarity 6, persuasion 5, answer first 6

The weakest page for voice. It persuades through aphorism, antithesis and superlative ('brutally fair', 'convert wars into arithmetic', 'the single highest-return legal document in small business', 'The first is cheapest', 'That answer is information'). It also leans on jargon ('cap table', 'paper the shareholder loans', 'TPD', 'buy/sell') where Spencer would give plain specifics. The lead announces what the page will cover instead of finishing the answer. The price matches fees.html, but the page leaves out the scope that tells a reader whether it applies: up to three shareholders who have agreed the commercial terms. The fee link also lands on the commercial section, not the shareholder agreement row. The law was re-verified on 6 Oct 2026, so this should be a rewrite in Spencer's voice that keeps every legal sentence. check_style.py: PASS, with one note on 'expert', which refers to a valuer, not the firm.

- [medium] answer-first: The lead ends by announcing what the page will cover, a signpost Spencer's voice avoids, instead of finishing the answer with the point the page argues. 'Your partnership' uses a legal term loosely on a page about companies, when the site's own structures guide treats a partnership as a different structure. 'Framework' is a stock word. The proposed lead is 68 words, and its last sentence comes from the page's own FAQ.
  Current: Without one, the company is governed by its constitution and the Corporations Act, rules written for companies in general, not your partnership. Here is what we put in a good agreement, why it is best signed early, and what happens without it. ... A company without a shareholder agreement runs on its constitution and the Corporations Act, a framework built for general cases, not your partnership.
  Proposed: Without one, the company is governed by its constitution and the Corporations Act, rules written for companies in general rather than for yours. It is cheapest and fairest to negotiate while goodwill is high, which is why it is best signed early. ... A company without a shareholder agreement runs on its constitution and the Corporations Act, which were written for companies in general rather than for yours.
- [medium] voice: Clipped sentences and aphorisms ('brutally fair', 'the wrong one becomes a weapon', 'Choosing it deliberately is the point') do the persuading that a concrete explanation would do better. The semicolon list after a colon needs rereading. The proposed version keeps every mechanism and explains plainly why a shotgun clause can be unfair.
  Current: Equal partners need a deadlock mechanism. Escalation to mediation is the gentle first step. Beyond it sit the classic devices: a shotgun clause, where one names a price per share and the other must buy or sell at it, brutally fair and fair only between parties of equal financial strength; auction-style processes; or an agreed independent valuation with one side exiting. The right mechanism depends on the shareholders' realities; the wrong one becomes a weapon. Choosing it deliberately is the point.
  Proposed: Equal shareholders need an agreed way to break a deadlock, and mediation is the sensible first step. If mediation fails, the agreement can provide for a shotgun clause, under which one shareholder names a price per share and the other must either buy or sell at that price, for an auction between the shareholders, or for an independent valuation with one side leaving. A shotgun clause is fair only between shareholders of similar financial strength, because one who cannot afford to buy is forced to sell, so the right mechanism depends on who the shareholders are and what each of them could fund.
- [medium] voice: The chiasmus ('convert wars into arithmetic ... convert arithmetic into wars') is the clever phrasing Spencer's rules rule out. It makes the reader decode a slogan to reach a plain point.
  Current: Almost every exit turns into a number. Agreements that specify the method, such as an independent expert, an earnings multiple or defined treatment of debts and surplus assets, convert wars into arithmetic. Agreements that say nothing convert arithmetic into wars, with duelling valuers and legal fees consuming the very value in dispute.
  Proposed: Almost every exit comes down to a price. An agreement that fixes the method, such as a valuation by an independent expert, an earnings multiple or a set treatment of debts and surplus assets, turns the price into a calculation. An agreement that says nothing leaves the price to be argued, often with a valuer on each side and legal fees eating into the very value in dispute.
- [medium] persuasion: Two claims no one can verify ('sink more partnerships than fraud ever does', 'the single highest-return legal document in small business') and an antithesis built for effect ('allies ... adversaries'). The site is meant to persuade through what the firm does and what things cost, never through superlatives. 'Sixty' should be 60 under the house number style.
  Current: Fights about effort and reward sink more partnerships than fraud ever does. ... When one founder works sixty hours and the other has quietly retired to the beach, the agreement, not resentment, should already contain the answer. ... Every co-owned company will one day face a disagreement, an exit or a death. The only choice is whether the rules for that day are written while the owners are allies or discovered while they are adversaries. A tailored agreement, signed early, is the single highest-return legal document in small business.
  Proposed: Disagreements about effort and reward cause many disputes between co-owners. ... If one founder works 60 hours a week while the other has stepped back, the agreement should already say how each of them is paid. ... Every co-owned company eventually faces a disagreement, an exit or a death. An agreement signed early settles the rules for that day while the owners are still on good terms, and it costs a fraction of the dispute it is written to prevent.
- [medium] fees-and-links: The price matches fees.html, but a reader cannot tell whether it applies to them, because the scope that decides it is missing: up to three shareholders who have agreed the commercial terms. The link lands on /fees#commercial-law instead of the row; link '$2,420 including GST' to /fees#fee-shareholders-agreement. 'Tailored' and 'modest' are the firm describing itself, 'routinely ... many multiples' cannot be verified, and 'The first is cheapest' is clipped.
  Current: A tailored shareholder agreement costs a modest fee, confirmed in writing before we start: ours is from $2,420 including GST, and our fees page sets out what it covers. A shareholder dispute without one routinely costs each side many multiples of that, before counting the management time, the poisoned workplace and the customers who drift away from the noise. We have drafted these agreements at every stage: incorporation, first investor, first crisis. The first is cheapest.
  Proposed: Our fee for a shareholder agreement is from $2,420 including GST, confirmed in writing before we start. That is for one private company with up to three shareholders who have agreed the commercial terms, with one round of changes, and more shareholders, investors or employee shares cost more. A shareholder dispute without an agreement can cost each side many times that, before counting the management time lost and the customers who drift away while the owners argue. We draft these agreements at incorporation, when a first investor comes in and in the middle of a first disagreement, and the earlier it is done, the less it costs.
- [medium] clarity: Several problems sit close together. 'Cap table', 'paper the shareholder loans' and 'TPD' are jargon a worried owner may not know. 'Buy/sell' uses a slash, and 'fixed fee, quoted first' is a clipped fragment. One link is labelled 'super death benefits'. A bare 'Read our guide' sentence ends the decision map paragraph without connecting to it. Two smaller fixes in the same sections: replace 'The agreement pre-writes each ending' with 'The agreement sets out in advance what happens in each case', and 'drafted in cascading, defensible steps' with 'drafted as a series of alternative periods and areas'. The second describes the drafting without claiming how a court will treat it.
  Current: Bring us your cap table and an honest account of how the partners work together. We will draft the agreement, align the company constitution, and paper the shareholder loans while we are at it: fixed fee, quoted first. ... Death and TPD: buy/sell arrangements, often insurance-funded, so a deceased partner's estate is paid out rather than staying on as a shareholder alongside you, a clause that quietly protects both families. See also super death benefits for the adjacent planning. ... Read our guide to directors' duties in Victoria.
  Proposed: Bring us the company's share register and an honest account of how the owners work together. We will draft the agreement, bring the company's constitution into line with it and document any shareholder loans at the same time, for a fixed fee confirmed in writing before we start. ... Death and total and permanent disability: buy-sell arrangements, often funded by insurance, so that a deceased shareholder's estate is paid out rather than staying on as a shareholder alongside you, which protects both families. Our guide to superannuation death benefits covers the related planning. ... Our guide to directors' duties in Victoria explains what the law expects of the directors who make those day to day decisions.
- [low] voice: 'That answer is information' needs rereading, and 'invest further value' is vague. 'Businesses do it at every funding round and family succession' overstates. Both answers are mirrored in the FAQPage schema.
  Current: That answer is information. You cannot force a signature, but you can decline to invest further value on unwritten terms, and the refusal itself tells you how a future dispute would run. ... Yes, and businesses do it at every funding round and family succession.
  Proposed: You cannot force a co-owner to sign, but you can decline to put more money or work into the company on unwritten terms, and the refusal itself tells you a good deal about how a future dispute would run. ... Yes, and many businesses add one at a funding round or when the business passes to the next generation of a family.
- [low] legal-query: For Spencer, not reworded here. As written, this can be read as a rule of law. Between the shareholders who sign it, the agreement prevails over the constitution only because it says so. It cannot bind members who did not sign, and it cannot amend the constitution, which needs a special resolution. The usual drafting is a clause saying the agreement prevails between the parties and obliging them to amend the constitution to remove any conflict. Consider saying that the agreement usually provides that it prevails. The answer is also in the FAQPage schema.
  Current: They must be drafted to work together, with the agreement usually prevailing between the shareholders.

## insight-terms-and-conditions-template-or-lawyer.html
Scores: voice 8, clarity 7, persuasion 8, answer first 9

The best of the five and the model for the others. The lead answers the title question, the headings are questions, and the law is applied to how the reader actually sells. The fee comes with its scope and links to the exact fee row, and there are call prompts mid article and at the close. What remains is small. One sentence on limiting liability first reads as 'failures to repair'. The word consumer does two legal jobs on one page. PPSR is unexplained in the lead. There is one 'this is where' saying and an echoed 'covers ... covering' in the fee paragraph, and Spencer may want to add a point on when to register. check_style.py: PASS with no notes.

- [medium] clarity: 'Limit its liability for most failures to repair' first reads as liability for failing to repair. Only at 'replacement' does the reader see that the list is what liability is limited to. Section 64A, read in the authorised compilation during this audit, limits liability for a failure to comply with a guarantee, other than the title guarantees, to repair, replacement or supplying the services again, or their cost. The proposed wording changes the order, not the law, and splits the sentence in two.
  Current: Where goods or services are not of a kind ordinarily acquired for personal or household use, a supplier may limit its liability for most failures to repair, replacement or supplying the services again, or the cost of doing so, unless the customer shows that relying on the limit is not fair or reasonable.
  Proposed: Where goods or services are not of a kind ordinarily acquired for personal or household use, a supplier may limit its liability for most failures to comply with a guarantee. It may limit that liability to repairing or replacing the goods or supplying the services again, or to the cost of doing so, unless the customer shows that relying on the limit is not fair or reasonable.
- [medium] legal-query: For Spencer, not reworded here. Two sections earlier the page explains, correctly, that the consumer guarantees definition in section 3 covers many business customers. The unfair terms provisions use a narrower meaning. Section 23(3), read in the authorised compilation during this audit, limits a consumer contract to a supply to an individual whose acquisition is wholly or predominantly for personal, domestic or household use or consumption. A reader who carries the earlier meaning forward will think a business customer under the price threshold is protected by the consumer limb. For this audience the practical effect is small, because the small business limb usually applies, but the word does two legal jobs on one page. Spencer can decide how to mark the difference.
  Current: Where the customer is a consumer, or either party is a small business, an unfair term in a standard form contract is void.
- [low] legal-query: For Spencer to consider. The section says registration is needed but not when it must be made. Section 588FL of the Corporations Act, read in the authorised compilation during this audit, applies to a security interest that a company grants and perfects by registration alone. If that registration is made more than 20 business days after the security agreement came into force and within six months before the customer's liquidation, administration, deed of company arrangement or small business restructuring, the interest vests in the company. Section 62(2) of the Personal Property Securities Act gives a supplier priority over a lender's general security in stock only if a purchase money registration is in place by the time the customer takes possession. One sentence on timing would complete the section's answer to its own heading, and the Corporations Act would then join the Sources line.
  Current: To be enforceable against anyone other than the customer, it needs writing that the customer signed or accepted and that describes the goods, and once the goods have left your hands it generally needs a registration on the Personal Property Securities Register to be perfected.
- [low] clarity: The lead is the passage answer engines quote and the first thing a worried owner reads, and it leaves 'PPSR' unexplained. Spelling it out and dropping 'actually' keeps the lead at 70 words.
  Current: Most templates are not written for the way you actually trade, some contain clauses the Australian Consumer Law makes void, and a retention of title clause will rarely protect you in a customer's insolvency unless it is properly documented and registered on the PPSR.
  Proposed: Most templates are not written for the way you trade, some contain clauses the Australian Consumer Law makes void, and a retention of title clause will rarely protect you in a customer's insolvency unless it is properly documented and registered on the Personal Property Securities Register.
- [low] voice: A 'this is where' line that turns the point into a saying. The same pattern recurs across these pages.
  Current: This is where the gap between a template and properly set up terms costs the most, because it shows only when a customer fails while owing you money.
  Proposed: The gap between a template and properly set up terms costs most here, because it shows only when a customer fails while owing you money.
- [low] voice: 'It covers ... covering' repeats the verb, because the fees page's scope line was pasted in, and the reader hears the echo.
  Current: Our fixed fee for terms of trade is from $1,815 including GST. It covers one set of terms for a business selling goods or services, covering orders, payment and credit, delivery, retention of title and liability, drafted to work with the Australian Consumer Law, with one round of changes.
  Proposed: Our fixed fee for terms of trade is from $1,815 including GST. That is for one set of terms for a business selling goods or services, dealing with orders, payment and credit, delivery, retention of title and liability, drafted to work with the Australian Consumer Law, with one round of changes.

## faq.html
Scores: voice 7, clarity 7, persuasion 6, answer first 7

Most of the 53 answers open with a direct answer, and the law is stated with care. Two intervention order answers go out of date on 10 November 2026, which is tomorrow. The two price questions put the firm's own fixed fee last. Only one answer has a phone number that can be tapped, and the one other number in an answer cannot be tapped. No answer links a service page, and a few legal sentences of about 70 words need splitting. Any wording change must also be made in the FAQPage schema, with the Last reviewed date, dateModified, sitemap lastmod and llms.txt Last updated all moving together.

- [high] legal-query: This goes out of date tomorrow at the latest. On 9 Nov 2026 I read the as made Justice Legislation Amendment (Family Violence, Stalking and Other Matters) Act 2026, No. 1 of 2026, on legislation.vic.gov.au. Section 2(3) commences Division 3 of Part 2, the Stage 3 amendments on misidentification, minimum respondent age, default final order duration and continuing protection for children, on proclamation. Section 2(4) commences on 10 November 2026 anything not proclaimed by then. From then the question heading 'Are Victoria's intervention order laws changing?', the future tense and 'The protections available now are unchanged' are all wrong, and the FAQPage schema carries the same words to search results. I have not rewritten the substance.
  Current: Further changes, requiring police and the courts to look at the whole relationship when deciding whether the wrong person has been named as the respondent, and stopping an application being made against a child under 12, start on a day to be proclaimed and no later than 10 November 2026. The protections available now are unchanged, and an order can still be sought urgently.
  Proposed: For the accuracy routine: confirm the commencement date in the authorised version, then change the tense. If no earlier proclamation was made, the sentence would read: 'Further changes came into operation on 10 November 2026, so police and the courts must now look at the whole relationship when deciding whether the wrong person has been named as the respondent, and an application can no longer be made against a child under 12.' Delete 'The protections available now are unchanged' and keep 'An order can still be sought urgently.' Retitle the question 'How did Victoria's intervention order laws change in 2026?'.
- [high] legal-query: The commencement problem in the answer above applies here too. The misidentification provisions, including new sections 53AAA, 60CA and 76A and the safety notice amendments in Subdivision 3 of Division 3 of Part 2, are in force from 10 November 2026 at the latest. After that, 'will require' tells someone who has been misidentified that a protection they already have does not yet apply.
  Current: Victorian reforms passed in 2026 will require police deciding on a safety notice, and courts deciding whether to make an intervention order, to consider as far as practicable whether this has occurred, from a day to be proclaimed and no later than 10 November 2026.
  Proposed: After the date is confirmed, the sentence would read: 'Since 10 November 2026, police deciding on a safety notice, and courts deciding whether to make an intervention order, must consider as far as practicable whether this has occurred.' If Division 3 was proclaimed earlier, use the proclaimed date instead.
- [medium] answer-first: The question asks about price, but the firm's own price comes about 115 words in, after the staged opener 'Three things make up the cost'. The fee is not linked to fees.html#fee-probate-application or to service-probate. 'Covers publishing the notice' also leaves unclear whether the notice charge itself is included. fees.html says the fixed fee covers publishing the notice and paying its fee.
  Current: Three things make up the cost. There is the Supreme Court filing fee, which the court sets on a scale according to the gross value of the Victorian estate, so a modest estate pays far less than a large one and the smallest estates pay no filing fee at all. There is also a charge for publishing the notice of intention to apply. Then there are legal fees, which turn on how straightforward the estate is: one property, a couple of bank accounts and the original will in hand is a very different job from a missing will, an asset held overseas or a dispute about who should administer the estate. Court fees are reviewed each financial year, so ask for the current figures rather than relying on an older guide. Our fixed fee for a probate application in a straightforward estate is $1,815 including GST, which covers publishing the notice, and administering the estate after the grant is estimated in writing once its shape is known.
  Proposed: Our fixed fee for a probate application in a straightforward estate is $1,815 including GST, which includes publishing the notice of intention to apply and paying its charge, and our fees page sets out what counts as a straightforward estate. The Supreme Court filing fee is separate, and the court sets it on a scale according to the gross value of the Victorian estate, so a modest estate pays far less than a large one and the smallest estates pay no filing fee at all. Court fees are reviewed each financial year, so ask for the current figure rather than relying on an older guide. Where the estate is not straightforward, legal fees turn on what is involved: one property, a couple of bank accounts and the original will in hand is a very different job from a missing will, an asset held overseas or a dispute about who should administer the estate, and administering the estate after the grant is estimated in writing once its shape is known. Our guide to how probate works in Victoria sets out the steps. Link 'probate application' to /service-probate and 'fees page' to /fees#fee-probate-application.
- [medium] answer-first: Someone asking what a divorce costs reads three sentences about the court's fee before the firm's fixed fee, which is the most persuasive fact on the page. The divorce service page is not linked. fees.html also says the filing fee is paid directly to the court, which this answer leaves out.
  Current: The court charges a filing fee for a divorce application, and a reduced fee is available to concession card holders and people in financial hardship, although for a joint application both of you must qualify. Fees change from year to year, so check the current schedule of the Federal Circuit and Family Court of Australia. - [medium] answer-first: The question asks what safe harbour is, but the first sentence is about liability. The third sentence packs two sets of conditions into 58 words. The answer ends on a line that turns the point into a saying instead of a call prompt, though the reader may be a director facing insolvency.
  Current: A director can be personally liable for a debt the company incurs while it is insolvent, or that makes it insolvent, where there were reasonable grounds to suspect as much. Safe harbour is a protection available to a director who, once they suspect the company may become or be insolvent, starts developing a course of action reasonably likely to lead to a better outcome for the company than the immediate appointment of an administrator or liquidator, such as a genuine restructuring plan made with proper advice. It covers debts incurred in connection with that course of action or in the ordinary course of business, but only if the director acts on it within a reasonable time, and only for as long as the director keeps pursuing it, it remains reasonably likely to lead to that better outcome and no administrator or liquidator has been appointed. [...] It rewards directors who act early rather than those who keep trading and hope conditions improve.
  Proposed: Safe harbour protects a director from personal liability for insolvent trading, that is, for a debt the company incurs while it is insolvent, or that makes it insolvent, where there were reasonable grounds to suspect as much. It is available to a director who, once they suspect the company may become or be insolvent, starts developing a course of action reasonably likely to lead to a better outcome for the company than the immediate appointment of an administrator or liquidator, such as a genuine restructuring plan made with proper advice. It covers debts incurred in connection with that course of action or in the ordinary course of business, but only if the director acts on it within a reasonable time. It lasts only for as long as the director keeps pursuing it, it remains reasonably likely to lead to that better outcome and no administrator or liquidator has been appointed. Keep the employee entitlements and books sentence as it is, and replace the closing line with: 'If you suspect your company may be insolvent, call us on (03) 9125 8355 as soon as you do, because safe harbour depends on acting early.'
