## service-wills-and-estate-planning.html
Scores: voice 7, clarity 7, persuasion 8, answer first 8

Clear and well priced: it states the fixed fees plainly in the lead and the fee note and explains why each document matters. The second paragraph repeats the lead, one process step runs to 58 words and states a witnessing bar that may go beyond the Act, the medical decisions block slips into clipped phrasing, and the page has no answer to the question will makers search most, what a will costs.

- [medium] voice: The middle sentence repeats the lead's second sentence almost word for word, so the reader meets the same explanation twice on one screen, and the paragraph's one real deadline, capacity, invites no call, although the adult child of a parent whose memory is failing is the reader most at risk.
  Current: An estate plan is usually built on three documents. Your will decides who receives your estate when you die, and an enduring power of attorney and a medical treatment decision maker appointment decide who can act for you while you are alive. Each depends on your capacity when you sign, so act while you are well.
  Proposed: An estate plan is usually built on three documents: your will, an enduring power of attorney and a medical treatment decision maker appointment. Each depends on your capacity when you sign, so act while you are well. If a parent's memory is starting to fail, call us now, while they can still make these documents.
- [medium] legal-query: On 27 October 2026 the site accuracy routine read section 35(2)(c) of the Powers of Attorney Act 2014 and the prescribed certificate. They exclude an attorney under the power and say nothing of an alternative attorney, so the routine corrected the enduring powers article to say only that it is safest not to ask an alternative attorney to witness. 'Anyone you appoint' takes in an alternative attorney, so for a power of attorney this sentence may state a bar the Act does not contain, and the page's claims register records the witness requirements but not this bar. The accuracy routine should read section 35 and the witnessing provision of the Medical Treatment Planning and Decisions Act 2016 and settle this sentence; no change to its substance is proposed here.
  Current: the Acts bar some people from witnessing, including anyone you appoint and, for a power of attorney, a relative of yours or of your attorney.
- [medium] clarity: At 58 words it is the longest prose sentence on the eight pages, and it runs two separate rules together, who must witness and who cannot. The split below keeps the wording of both rules, subject to the legal query on 'anyone you appoint'.
  Current: A power of attorney or medical appointment needs two adult witnesses who sign in front of you and each other, one of them a doctor or a person authorised to witness affidavits, and the Acts bar some people from witnessing, including anyone you appoint and, for a power of attorney, a relative of yours or of your attorney.
  Proposed: A power of attorney or medical appointment needs two adult witnesses who sign in front of you and each other, and one of them must be a doctor or a person authorised to witness affidavits. The Acts also bar some people from witnessing, including anyone you appoint and, for a power of attorney, a relative of yours or of your attorney.
- [medium] persuasion: The first FAQ repeats the hub's identical question and the two service blocks just above it, while the question most will makers search, what a will costs, has no FAQ here, although the playbook allows the wills hub's cost answer on this page. The gate needs exactly five questions, so replace this one. Every figure matches fees.html.
  Current: What is the difference between a power of attorney and a medical treatment decision maker?
  Proposed: How much does a will cost? Our fixed fee for a single will is $495, and for wills for a couple $880. An estate plan for one person, with a will, an enduring power of attorney and a medical treatment decision maker appointment, is $1,089, and a will with a testamentary trust is from $1,650. Every price includes GST, and our fees page sets out what each covers.
- [low] voice: 'That is the role of' comments on the sentence before, 'Appoint nobody and the law looks to' is a clipped conditional that reads as a flourish, and alone among the four blocks it never says what we prepare.
  Current: A power of attorney made now does not cover medical treatment. That is the role of a medical treatment decision maker you appoint, who decides when you cannot. Appoint nobody and the law looks to any guardian VCAT has appointed with power over medical treatment, then to a list headed by your spouse or domestic partner. We also draft advance care directives.
  Proposed: A power of attorney made now does not cover medical treatment, so we prepare the appointment of a medical treatment decision maker, who decides for you when you cannot. If you appoint nobody, the law looks to any guardian VCAT has appointed with power over medical treatment, then to a list headed by your spouse or domestic partner. We also draft advance care directives.
- [low] voice: A plan is not a fee. The divorce and property pages, and this site's FAQ answers, say 'our fixed fee for', which is how the firm would say it; the fee note's opening sentence has the same construction.
  Current: An estate plan for one person is a fixed fee of $1,089 including GST.
  Proposed: Our fixed fee for an estate plan for one person is $1,089 including GST.
- [low] consistency: The address leaves out Box Hill and a comma, unlike every other page, so 1 Main Street stands without a suburb.
  Current: We act for individuals, couples and blended families from Suite 10, 1 Main Street, a short walk from Box Hill station with parking at Box Hill Central.
  Proposed: We act for individuals, couples and blended families from our office at Suite 10, 1 Main Street, Box Hill, a short walk from Box Hill station, with parking at Box Hill Central.

## service-probate.html
Scores: voice 7, clarity 7, persuasion 8, answer first 8

Practical and well ordered for an executor: whether a grant is needed, what to do first, what it costs, and frank answers on renouncing and applying without a lawyer. The process steps are fragments with no subject, the time limits section ends on an article link with no prompt to call and no link to the will disputes page, and the fixed fee is phrased as though the application were the fee.

- [medium] voice: All three steps are fragments with no subject, unlike the steps on the other seven pages, and step 2 is ambiguous: a reader cannot tell whether the death certificate and inventory are exhibited to the affidavit or filed beside it. The claims register says the affidavit exhibits all three, which the rewrite makes plain.
  Current: Posted on the Supreme Court's online probate system at least 14 days before applying, unless the court or the Registrar shortens that time.
Filed online with the applicant's affidavit, exhibiting the will if there is one, a certified copy of the death certificate and the inventory of assets and liabilities the Registrar requires. Any original will is then filed with the Probate Office within 28 days.
Made by the Registrar when satisfied, though the court's order is needed while a caveat stands or where it is doubtful whether a grant should be made. The estate can then be collected and, in time, distributed.
  Proposed: We post the notice on the Supreme Court's online probate system at least 14 days before applying, unless the court or the Registrar shortens that time.
We file the application online with the applicant's affidavit, which exhibits the will if there is one, a certified copy of the death certificate and the inventory of assets and liabilities the Registrar requires. Any original will is then filed with the Probate Office within 28 days.
The Registrar makes the grant once satisfied, but a court order is needed while a caveat stands or where it is doubtful whether a grant should be made. The estate can then be collected and, in time, distributed.
- [medium] call-prompt: 'Within them' is clipped and sends the reader back for the noun, and the section that tells an executor when distribution is safe ends on an article link, with no prompt to call and no link to the will disputes page, where the firm's work for executors facing a claim is set out.
  Current: The six months after the grant matter most. A family provision claim must generally be made within them. The court can extend that time, but only on an application made before final distribution, and an extension cannot disturb anything distributed before it was sought. An executor who distributes properly after the six months, with no notice of a claim, is generally protected. Our guide to contesting a will in Victoria explains who can claim.
  Proposed: The six months after the grant matter most, because a family provision claim must generally be made within that time. The court can extend it, but only on an application made before final distribution, and an extension cannot disturb anything distributed before it was sought. An executor who distributes properly after the six months, with no notice of a claim, is generally protected. Our guide to contesting a will in Victoria explains who can claim. If someone has [raised a claim](/service-contesting-a-will), or you are about to distribute, call us before anything is paid out.
- [low] voice: It makes the application the fee; the page's own cost FAQ already says it the way the firm would. The fee note's opening, 'A probate application is a fixed fee of $1,815', takes the same change.
  Current: A straightforward probate application is a fixed fee of $1,815, and letters of administration where there is no will $2,178, each including GST.
  Proposed: Our fixed fee for a straightforward probate application is $1,815, and for letters of administration where there is no will $2,178, each including GST.
- [low] voice: Every other block under 'What we do' opens with what we do; this one opens with what the applicant must prove, so the reader meets a rule before learning who does the work.
  Current: Without a will, the applicant must set out their right to administer, the surviving relatives and their search for a will. We work out who can apply and who inherits, and obtain any consents. Where a will has no executor able to act, we apply for letters of administration with the will annexed.
  Proposed: We work out who can apply and who inherits, and obtain any consents. Without a will, the applicant must set out their right to administer, the surviving relatives and their search for a will, and we prepare that evidence. Where a will has no executor able to act, we apply for letters of administration with the will annexed.
- [low] clarity: 'Before a year from the death' is a clipped legal idiom a grieving reader will stumble on.
  Current: An executor is not bound to distribute before a year from the death, and debts and the costs of administration come ahead of the beneficiaries.
  Proposed: An executor is not bound to distribute the estate until a year has passed since the death, and debts and the costs of administration come ahead of the beneficiaries.

## service-contesting-a-will.html
Scores: voice 7, clarity 7, persuasion 7, answer first 6

Even handed and careful, written for claimants, executors and beneficiaries alike, with a good closing line that asks for the grant date. The lead leaves out the six month limit that decides most enquiries, the time limits section ends without a prompt, three of the five answers open without answering, and two sentences fold a rule of law into a statement about whom we act for.

- [medium] answer-first: The one sentence lead says whom we act for but leaves out the six month limit, the fact a claimant most needs, and the kind of time limit the property settlement lead states for its own claims. At 38 words there is room for it, and the rewrite is 56.
  Current: From our Box Hill office, we act for people contesting a will, whether they were left out, left short or doubt the will is valid, and for executors and beneficiaries defending one, through negotiation, mediation and any hearing.
  Proposed: From our Box Hill office, we act for people contesting a will because they were left out, left short or doubt the will is valid, and for executors and beneficiaries defending one, through negotiation, mediation and any hearing. A family provision claim must generally be made within six months of the grant of probate or administration.
- [medium] clarity: A sentence about whom we act for turns, through a relative clause, into the rule on who bears a provision order, and the closing 'it' has to be traced back to the court.
  Current: We act on either side, and for beneficiaries too, who bear the cost of any provision the court orders for a claimant, in proportion to the value of their interests, unless it orders otherwise.
  Proposed: We act on either side, and for beneficiaries too. Unless the court orders otherwise, the beneficiaries bear the cost of any provision it orders for a claimant, in proportion to the value of their interests.
- [medium] call-prompt: Both deadlines are stated and the section stops; neither paragraph tells a claimant or a caveator to call, although the page's own closing line asks for the grant date.
  Current: Once someone applies for a grant, the caveat lapses 30 days after the caveator is notified, unless grounds of objection are filed and served in that time or a judge orders otherwise.
  Proposed: Once someone applies for a grant, the caveat lapses 30 days after the caveator is notified, unless grounds of objection are filed and served in that time or a judge orders otherwise. If probate has been granted, or you have been told that someone has applied for a grant, call us now.
- [medium] answer-first: 'Only as an eligible person' is circular to a reader who does not know the term; the yes and the no the reader wants arrive in the second and third sentences.
  Current: Only as an eligible person. A grandchild, a member of the deceased's household or a registered caring partner is eligible, but the court can make an order for them only if they were wholly or partly dependent on the deceased. A sibling or a carer who fits none of the Act's categories cannot make a family provision claim.
  Proposed: A grandchild can, and so can a member of the deceased's household or a registered caring partner, but the court can make an order for them only if they were wholly or partly dependent on the deceased. A sibling or a carer who fits none of the Act's categories of eligible person cannot make a family provision claim.
- [low] answer-first: The question is who pays, and the answer opens with an impersonal negative before saying who decides. The two sentences only change places.
  Current: It cannot be assumed that the estate pays everyone's costs. The court generally decides who pays the costs of a proceeding and how much, and it can order a party to pay costs personally, although a settlement can deal with costs by agreement.
  Proposed: The court generally decides who pays the costs of a proceeding and how much, and it can order a party to pay costs personally, although a settlement can deal with costs by agreement. It cannot be assumed that the estate pays everyone's costs.
- [low] answer-first: Two sentences in a question heading, and the answer then opens with an instruction, 'Get advice first', rather than the answer.
  Current: I am the executor and a claim has been threatened. Can I distribute?
  Proposed: Heading: Can I distribute the estate if a claim has been threatened? Answer opening: Not before you have taken advice. The rest of the answer unchanged.
- [low] voice: It says the same thing twice in one sentence, once as 'no fixed fee' and again as 'a written estimate instead'.
  Current: An estate dispute has no fixed fee, and as our fees page explains, a dispute is charged on a written estimate instead.
  Proposed: An estate dispute is charged on a written estimate rather than a fixed fee, as our fees page explains.
- [low] clarity: Three ideas in one sentence, and 'one not properly signed' makes the reader supply 'will'.
  Current: We gather the evidence and lodge caveats, and act to uphold wills too, including one not properly signed, which the Supreme Court can admit to probate if satisfied the person intended it to be their will.
  Proposed: We gather the evidence and lodge caveats. We also act to uphold wills, including a will that was not properly signed, which the Supreme Court can admit to probate if it is satisfied the person intended it to be their will.
- [low] consistency: The address leaves out Box Hill, unlike the other pages, so 1 Main Street stands without a suburb.
  Current: Our office is at Suite 10, 1 Main Street, a short walk from Box Hill station, with parking at Box Hill Central.
  Proposed: Our office is at Suite 10, 1 Main Street, Box Hill, a short walk from Box Hill station, with parking at Box Hill Central.

## service-commercial-leases.html
Scores: voice 7, clarity 6, persuasion 7, answer first 8

The most legally detailed of the eight, and specific about what a review covers and what it costs, with the best closing line for a business reader. The time limits section is dense enough that a tenant has to read it twice and ends without a prompt, the assignment block states only law and never says what we do, and the page does not link the business sale page that covers the same assignment.

- [medium] call-prompt: The section sets out short periods for a tenant who signed without proper disclosure and ends without telling that tenant to call.
  Current: The Retail Leases Act gives no way to extend any of these periods.
  Proposed: The Retail Leases Act gives no way to extend any of these periods, so if you have signed without a disclosure statement, or the one you were given was wrong, call us now.
- [medium] clarity: 'Run to the day' and 'the first of everyone signing, you moving in or you starting to pay rent' both need a second read, at the start of the densest section on the eight pages.
  Current: The 14 days for disclosure run to the day the lease is entered into, which the Act treats as the first of everyone signing, you moving in with the landlord's consent or you starting to pay rent.
  Proposed: The 14 days for disclosure are counted back from the day the lease is entered into, which under the Act is the earliest of the day everyone has signed, the day you move in with the landlord's consent and the day you start paying rent.
- [medium] links: Under 'What we do on a lease', this block states only law and never says what we do, and it covers the same ground as the business sale page without linking it. One sentence is added; the two legal sentences are unchanged.
  Current: Under a retail lease the landlord can refuse consent only on grounds the Act lists, such as a buyer it considers lacks the financial resources or business experience to meet the lease's obligations. Where the business carries on, the seller and its guarantors are not liable for the buyer's obligations if the seller gives the landlord and the buyer an accurate and complete disclosure statement.
  Proposed: We prepare the assignment, the request for the landlord's consent and the seller's disclosure statement, and our [business sale page](/service-buying-or-selling-a-business) explains how we act on the sale itself. Under a retail lease the landlord can refuse consent only on grounds the Act lists, such as a buyer it considers lacks the financial resources or business experience to meet the lease's obligations. Where the business carries on, the seller and its guarantors are not liable for the buyer's obligations if the seller gives the landlord and the buyer an accurate and complete disclosure statement.
- [low] voice: 'Is from $1,089 including GST, for a written summary of the terms that matter and, for a retail lease, the disclosure statement' reads at first as though the review supplies the disclosure statement.
  Current: A lease review for a tenant is from $1,089 including GST, for a written summary of the terms that matter and, for a retail lease, the disclosure statement, with recommended changes and a call to go through it.
  Proposed: Our fee for reviewing a lease for a tenant is from $1,089 including GST. It covers a written summary of the terms that matter, and of the disclosure statement for a retail lease, with our recommended changes and a call to go through it.
- [low] voice: The last sentence repeats the lead word for word.
  Current: People usually call when a lease arrives to sign, an option date is close, a rent review or outgoings bill looks wrong, or a dispute has started over repairs, rent or the end of the lease. The best time to call is before you sign.
  Proposed: People usually call when a lease arrives to sign, an option date is close, a rent review or outgoings bill looks wrong, or a dispute has started over repairs, rent or the end of the lease.
- [low] voice: 'Act' appears twice in one list, and the valuer clause in the middle separates the two halves of the sentence.
  Current: We check the landlord's notices before an option date, act on market rent reviews, where an experienced retail valuer sets the rent if landlord and tenant cannot agree, and act in disputes at the Small Business Commission, at VCAT and in court.
  Proposed: We check the landlord's notices before an option date and act on market rent reviews, where an experienced retail valuer sets the rent if landlord and tenant cannot agree. When a dispute starts, we act for you at the Small Business Commission, at VCAT and in court.
- [low] clarity: 'Its' has to be traced back two sentences to the landlord.
  Current: Its capital costs are excluded too, apart from capital works you agree to carry out yourself and some essential safety measure costs.
  Proposed: The landlord's capital costs are excluded too, apart from capital works you agree to carry out yourself and some essential safety measure costs.

## service-buying-or-selling-a-business.html
Scores: voice 7, clarity 7, persuasion 7, answer first 8

Practical, with a lead that tells the reader exactly when to call and a clear account of the section 52 statement. It states the seller's release from the lease four times, closes the time limits section without a prompt although the section 52 right is lost on taking possession, says vendor once on a page that otherwise says seller, and never links the commercial leases page that covers the lease side in full.

- [medium] voice: The seller's release from the lease is stated in the In brief box, here, in part in process step 2 and again in full in the FAQ, and the commercial leases page states it too. Four times on one page reads as padding, and the two wordings, 'accurate and complete' here and 'prescribed form, with nothing false, misleading or materially incomplete' in the FAQ, invite the reader to wonder whether they differ. Delete this sentence and keep the brief and the FAQ.
  Current: Where the business carries on, giving the landlord and the buyer an accurate and complete disclosure statement means neither you nor your guarantor is liable to perform the lease or pay what the buyer owes under it.
  Proposed: We prepare the contract, the section 52 statement for a small business and, on a retail lease, the disclosure statement the Retail Leases Act asks of an outgoing tenant, and we deal with the buyer's lawyer through to settlement.
- [medium] call-prompt: The section explains the buyer's right to end the contract but not the practical warning that follows from the urgent strip, that the right must be used before taking possession, and it has no prompt to call.
  Current: The three month limit for ending the contract under section 52 applies whether the statement was never given, was not in the prescribed form, left out particulars the regulations require or stated any of them inaccurately.
  Proposed: The three month limit for ending the contract under section 52 applies whether the statement was never given, was not in the prescribed form, left out particulars the regulations require or stated any of them inaccurately. If you have signed and the statement was missing or wrong, call us before you take possession of the business, because the right to end the contract under section 52 must be used before then.
- [low] voice: A 57 word opening sentence, then 'Each is a better conversation', which gives a conversation a comparative it cannot carry.
  Current: We act for buyers and sellers of small and medium businesses, and people usually call at one of three points: a buyer has been handed a contract by the seller's broker, a seller has a buyer and needs a contract and a plan for the lease, or the owners of a family company have agreed to sell. Each is a better conversation before anything is signed, because a signed contract binds you.
  Proposed: We act for buyers and sellers of small and medium businesses. People usually call at one of three points: a buyer has been handed a contract by the seller's broker, a seller has found a buyer and needs a contract and a plan for the lease, or the owners of a family company have agreed to sell. In each case it is better to speak with us before anything is signed, because a signed contract binds you.
- [low] consistency: 'Vendor' appears once on a page that says 'seller' everywhere else, and the semicolon join and the 'is from, for' construction make the scope hard to follow.
  Current: Our review of a contract to buy a business is from $1,210 including GST, for a written report on the main risks in the contract and the vendor's disclosure with a call to go through it; a franchise or a purchase of shares costs more.
  Proposed: Our fee for reviewing a contract to buy a business is from $1,210 including GST. It covers a written report on the main risks in the contract and the seller's disclosure, and a call to go through it. A franchise or a purchase of shares costs more.
- [low] links: 'We run the assignment under the lease and, for a retail lease, the Retail Leases Act' compresses two sets of steps into a phrase that needs a second read, and the page never links the commercial leases page, which covers the lease side in full.
  Current: Transferring the lease usually needs the landlord's consent. For a seller we run the assignment under the lease and, for a retail lease, the Retail Leases Act, and for a buyer we check the rent, outgoings, term left and options to renew.
  Proposed: Transferring the lease usually needs the landlord's consent. For a seller, we run the assignment through the steps the lease and, for a retail lease, the Retail Leases Act require. For a buyer, we check the rent, the outgoings, the term left and any options to renew, and our [commercial leases page](/service-commercial-leases) explains the rest of our lease work.

## Cross page patterns

- The three older articles are the weakest: divorce (23 Mar 2026), binding financial agreements (27 Apr) and de facto (18 May). They predate the answer-first lead, question heading and flowing prose conventions that the newer agreed settlement, child support and enforcement articles follow, and they carry most of the metaphor, slogans and fragment lists. Bringing them up in one pass would be better than doing it piecemeal.
- 'The bottom line' closing sections appear in the BFA, de facto and divorce articles, and outside my set in property-after-separation, property-reforms-2025 and parenting-arrangements. The checker flags the phrase. Each section repeats the lead and ends on a saying, and the BFA one overstates how safe a well-made agreement is. Remove them, with their contents entries, and let How we help and the closing call prompt end each page.
- Call prompts slip into slogans instead of the settled free first call wording 'Your first call is free, and you speak with a lawyer': 'One conversation tells you whether you have a case' (child support), 'One conversation maps your next steps' (divorce), 'Ask us this week, not next year' and 'a confidential first conversation' (de facto). The divorce line 'we make sure ... nothing is lost to a deadline' reads as a guarantee outside the three service promises. Otherwise all six pages respect the plural lawyers voice and the three promises, with no specialist claim and no mention of AI.
- Fee links open /fees or /fees#family-law instead of the fee ids that exist for this purpose: #fee-consent-orders-for-an-agreed-property-settlement, #fee-binding-financial-agreement and #fee-divorce-application-joint. Binding financial agreements and child support have no service page, so they should link the hub blocks #svc-binding-financial-agreements and #svc-child-support-and-spousal-maintenance. All quoted prices match fees.html: $1,089, $1,452, $3,025 and from $3,025 per party.
- Cross-link gaps among these six: no article links insight-enforcing-property-orders.html (only the hub, home, insights and About do), and the agreed settlement article's follow-through paragraph is the natural place. The BFA, divorce and de facto articles never link insight-agreed-property-settlement.html, although each discusses making a settlement final or the property deadline. The de facto article discusses child support without linking that guide, and links grandparents' rights instead.
- Overlap: the choice between consent orders and a BFA is explained three times, in property-after-separation, in full in agreed-property-settlement, and in the BFA article. Keep the full comparison in the agreed settlement article, and have the other two summarise it in two sentences and link it. The time limit paragraph is restated in agreed settlement, de facto, divorce, spousal maintenance and property-after-separation. That is acceptable for standalone pages, but they should use identical figures and wording.
- Number style: the divorce article writes 'twelve' ten times, as do property-after-separation and spousal maintenance, while headlines, titles, service pages and the hub write '12'. The voice rules want figures from 10 upwards. The divorce article and service-divorce.html also disagree on 'one month and one day' versus 'one month'. Section 55(1)(a) says 'at the expiration of a period of 1 month from the making of the order', so one form should be chosen.
- Act citations tacked on as the last sentence of an opening section ('The law in question is the Family Law Act 1975 (Cth), under which these agreements are made.' and 'Divorce is granted under the Family Law Act 1975 (Cth).') read as leftovers from the 26 Sep sources pass. Fold each into the section's first sentence. Clipped link sentences at section ends ('Read our guide to ...', 'More at our family law page.', 'Start at our family law page.') should likewise join the sentence before.
- Gate gap: in scripts/check-publish.py, the check 'every dated currency phrase matches the currency line' uses the case-sensitive pattern \bas at ([A-Z][a-z]+ \d{4}). The child support article's 'As at August 2026' therefore passes against a September 2026 currency line. Today's run reported 104 checks and 0 failed. Its own comment says this check exists because the child support article once carried two months. Make the pattern case-insensitive.
- Checker false positive: check_style.py fails any page whose Sources line names the Federal Circuit and Family Court of Australia (Family Law) Rules 2021, because its statute title exemption only covers bracketed words before 'Act'. Extend the exemption to Rules and Regulations.
- Currency lines: five of the six still say 'as at September 2026'. The de facto and divorce articles were re-verified as correct on 27 Oct and 20 Oct, but under the rule that dates move only when the text changes, a November reader sees a date two months old. Spencer may wish to decide whether a clean verification should move the currency line, dateModified and sitemap lastmod together.
- Implementation notes for whoever applies these changes: mirror any FAQ wording in the FAQPage schema (gate check). Update the contents list and h2 ids with any heading change. Move dateModified, the currency line and lastmod with any change of substance. Keep every lead at 70 words or fewer; the de facto lead is at exactly 70 now. Read the two legal queries (the de facto definition under section 4AA and the child support reforms status) against the authorised legislation and check them a second time before publishing. I also checked section 14(2) of the Wills Act 1997 (Vic). It turns on whether 'it appears that the testator did not want' the gift revoked, so the divorce pages' 'unless it appears you wanted them to stand' is accurate and needs no change.
- Working files, all outside the repository, which is unchanged: extracted main text and article body text for each page, the extraction script, the checked proposed wording (proposed.txt, which passes check_style.py with no notes) and the downloaded legislation, all in /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-spencer-alexander-site/b1d9c322-8bfd-59b9-b3d7-5cacca158acd/scratchpad/audit/content/family-a/
- Style checker: all six pages PASS with no hard failures, setting aside the (Vic) and (Cth) of Act titles and the phone number. The notes that matter are the stock heading 'The bottom line' on four pages and five sentences over 60 words (grandparents 80, spousal maintenance 73 and 72, parenting 65, reforms 64), each a list of legal considerations that reads better split. 'Outsized' is a false positive. The specialist note on intervention orders describes the specialist family violence courts, not the firm. The 13 to 17 bullet counts come mostly from the In this article lists and the rail card.
- 'The bottom line' closes four pages (intervention orders, parenting, property after separation and the 2025 reforms), and each time it restates the article as slogans: 'act in days, not weeks; put evidence before emotion', 'not a coin-flip and not a war', 'the arithmetic of good sense', 'a statutory home'. Replace each with a question heading about the reader's next step and plain advice. Keep the ids so the contents lists and any inbound anchors still work.
- Voice divides by age. The two August articles, grandparents and spousal maintenance, already read close to Spencer's flowing register. The four from March to July use verbless openers, label colons, fragment bullets and curt imperatives: 'Three recurring flashpoints.', 'Mediation is not a hoop.', 'Perspective matters.', 'Unilaterally ignoring it is a charge.', '...use them.' One voice pass over those four would lift this group of pages more than any other single change.
- Two callouts promise more than the three confirmed service promises: 'We can help you today' on parenting, and 'fixed appearances quoted where possible' on intervention orders, which fees.html does not support. Use 'Your first call is free, and you speak with a lawyer', the one business day reply, after hours contact for urgent matters and the written estimate rule.
- Fees and links: parenting and property after separation name the published fees and link the fees page; grandparents, the 2025 reforms and spousal maintenance name none. Spousal maintenance and intervention orders link only the top of the family law hub, not its own sections (#svc-child-support-and-spousal-maintenance and #svc-family-violence-and-intervention-orders). Neither property page links insight-agreed-property-settlement.html, although it links to the property guide. The two 'How we help' sections, on grandparents and spousal maintenance, both end on the generic 'Learn more about our family law practice.'
- Overlap: insight-property-reforms-2025.html restates most of insight-property-after-separation.html, including the four steps, disclosure, wastage, family violence, super, the time limits and the 50/50 myth. Both also use 'for the people living it' and 'even a rough one'. Framing it as an update is allowed under the no rehash rule. But seventeen months after the reforms took effect, the page would serve its reader better if cut back to what changed and whom it affects (pre-2025 advice, which proceedings are covered, family violence evidence), leaving the basics to the main guide by link.
- Time limit wording varies: 'within twelve months of divorce' on property after separation, 'twelve months from divorce' and 'once a divorce takes effect' on the reforms page, and 'from your divorce order taking effect' on spousal maintenance. The precise form used by the property settlement service page and faq.html, 'from the date the divorce order takes effect', should be used everywhere. These pages also write 'twelve months' where the hub and service pages write '12 months'. The variation is a legal precision point, so any change goes through the accuracy check.
- The Sources lines on intervention orders, parenting and spousal maintenance name an Act without sections, although CLAUDE.md asks for the sections or Part relied on; the other three pages already give them. The accuracy routine's notes of 27 Oct and 3 Nov 2026 list the sections read for parenting and intervention orders. Spousal maintenance needs the same reading before its line is filled in.
- Every page's currency line reads 'as at September 2026', although intervention orders, parenting and the 2025 reforms were re-verified in October and November with no change. The line is true as written, but a reader in November sees a stamp two months old on law that was checked last week. Because the gate ties the currency month to dateModified, whether a verification that changes nothing should move both is a decision for Spencer.
- Every FAQ answer change proposed here must be mirrored word for word in that page's FAQPage JSON-LD, which the gate checks, and every heading change in that page's In this article list. Any change to substance moves dateModified, the currency line and the sitemap lastmod in the same commit.
- faq.html's two misidentification answers say the changes start 'no later than 10 November 2026', which is tomorrow in Melbourne. The content backlog already hands them to the Tuesday accuracy routine. Once those answers are updated, link the wrongly named answer from the respondent section of the intervention orders article.
- Repeated stock phrasing across these pages: 'Bring us the picture' on intervention orders and property after separation, 'even a rough one' on property after separation and the reforms page, and 'act in days, not weeks' twice on intervention orders. Apart from the two callouts above, no page in this group mentions AI, describes the firm as a specialist or states a firm fact that conflicts with the rest of the site.
- Working files: the extracted visible text of each <main>, the body-only versions, the checker output and the proposed replacement texts (all of which pass check_style.py) are in /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-spencer-alexander-site/b1d9c322-8bfd-59b9-b3d7-5cacca158acd/scratchpad/audit/content/family-b/. The checker output is in check_style_results.txt and the final proposals in proposed-final.txt. Nothing in the repository was changed.
- Rule 9.01 is unresolved on two of these pages. The statement withdrawn from service-probate.html on 28 Sep 2026 pending Spencer's view, that professional fees for a grant are ordinarily paid from the estate, still appears in insight-probate-victoria.html (body, visible FAQ and FAQPage schema) and insight-trustee-company-or-lawyer-will.html (lead, body and meta description). One decision from Spencer, then one wording across all of them.
- Bare cross-link sentences are tacked onto paragraphs: 'Read our guide to ...', 'See who can contest a will in Victoria.', 'See the time limits in ...', 'See our guides to ...', 'see our note on ...'. There are nine on five pages: guardianship two, valid will three, probate two, super one, testamentary trusts one. Two point at unrelated topics: advance care directives after assets passing outside a will, and fraudulent calumny after caveats. Fold each link into a sentence that says why the reader would go there.
- Published fixed fees are missing where the reader decides. The $495 enduring power of attorney is not named on the guardianship or misuse pages. The super page says fees are 'quoted' when the $1,089 and $1,980 estate plans are published. The testamentary trust cost question is answered only at the end of the page. Fee links go to the top of /fees rather than the #fee- anchors, except on the trustee company page. The trustee company page never links /service-wills-and-estate-planning, and the misuse page links /service-contesting-a-will for a topic it does not cover.
- Lead rules are slipping. Two leads end on a signpost ('Here is what makes a will valid ...', 'Here is what happens, and how we can help.'). The misuse lead omits the six month deadline the body states, against the rule that a lead names any deadline in the body. The testamentary trusts lead is exactly 70 words, so any addition fails the gate.
- Mid-article prompts are on all seven pages and mostly well aimed: probate after the six month limit, super after the challenge section, guardianship after first steps. Two are misplaced: on the misuse page, before the section with the time limit; on the trustee company page, before the section it refers to.
- Headings split by age. The older super and testamentary trust pages, and parts of guardianship and probate, use clever or statement headings ('The tax nobody mentions', 'Who should say no', 'Common complications'), while the newer pages ask the reader's question. Make them plain questions throughout, and use 'Common questions' for every FAQ block. Each heading change needs its H2 id and contents list entry changed too.
- Sayings and metaphors exceed the voice rules, which allow at most one closing saying per article. The super, testamentary trusts, misuse and valid will pages each have three or more: 'Precision is kindness', 'a shield, not a fortress', 'better armour', 'sit in the same room', 'the hardest money to see again', 'Doing nothing is the one option that reliably makes things worse', 'that is where the real work lies'.
- Numbers break the house style, which puts 10 and above and all percentages in figures: 'fifteen per cent' and 'thirty per cent' in the super body, FAQ and schema; 'A twenty-minute health check'; 'thirty years rather than three'; and 'six to twelve months' on the probate page. Two abbreviations are never defined: EPOA on the guardianship page and SMSF on the super page.
- Every FAQ answer on these pages repeats body sentences word for word and must equal its FAQPage schema, which the gate checks. A wording change to a sentence that also sits in a FAQ, such as the probate costs, the super tax rates or the guardianship order duration, has to change the body, the visible FAQ and the schema together. A change to what a page says also moves its dateModified, updated date, currency line and sitemap lastmod in the same commit.
- check_style.py fails the probate and super pages on brackets that belong to the official titles of statutory rules and regulations: Supreme Court (Administration and Probate) Rules 2023, Supreme Court (Fees) Regulations 2018 and Superannuation Industry (Supervision) Regulations 1994. The pages are right. The checker's exemption matches only titles ending in 'Act' and should be widened to Rules and Regulations, which is outside this audit's read-only scope.
- No heavy overlap among the seven. The probate page's executor and first steps sections are short summaries that link the executor duties guide and the first steps checklist. The list of assets that pass outside a will appears on the valid will, super and trustee company pages in consistent terms. The valid will page's executor section should link the trustee company guide, which goes much further on the same choice.
- Firm voice is right throughout: every page speaks as 'we' and of lawyers, the rail and closing call wording follow the three permitted service promises, and nothing describes the firm as specialist or mentions AI. The only firm fact problems are the experience claim made for the firm on the valid will page and the over-promise about one conversation on the probate page.
- Calls written as fragments. Nine of the fourteen call prompts on these pages are verbless questions: 'Believe the will...?', 'Want your medical wishes...?', 'Left out, left short, or defending...?', 'Administering the estate of someone...?', 'Acting as executor and unsure...?', 'Waiting on an executor...?', 'A beneficiary waiting on...?', 'Wondering whether...?' and 'Concerned a will...?'. That advertising register appears nowhere in Spencer's exemplar. Recast each as one plain sentence beginning 'If you...', and name the fixed fee in the call wherever one applies. For example: 'If you are administering the estate of someone who died without a will, call (03) 9125 8355 or send an enquiry. Letters of administration for a straightforward estate where there is no will are a fixed fee of $2,178 including GST.' The enduring power of attorney page's 'Is a parent or partner starting to find decisions harder...?' is a proper question and can stay.
- Rhetoric in the older articles. The June to August pieces close paragraphs on sayings and use clever headings: 'The bottom line' on both the contesting and intestacy pages, 'The one-hour fix', 'Blended families: where the formula bites', 'Supportive attorneys: help without handover', 'A practical package'. They also lean on figures of speech such as 'free swing', 'gives this teeth', 'the court's blessing' and 'the same clock is your ally'. The beneficiary article of 2 Nov 2026 shows the standard: plain question headings, each answered in its first words, with the section of the Act applied to the reader. Bring the older pages up to it.
- Promised outcomes. Several lines promise more than the firm can guarantee: 'Appointing someone yourself removes all of that' on the directives page, 'Every consequence above is avoided by a valid will' and 'an hour of your time' on the intestacy page, 'careful timing avoids' and 'so nothing is missed and nothing unnecessary is paid' on the executor duties page, 'we can advise on a VCAT application quickly' and 'Many clients describe signing one as buying peace of mind' on the attorney page, and 'the letter that usually gets an estate moving again' on the beneficiary page. AGENTS.md says never to promise an outcome, and the site makes only three service promises.
- Estate plan contents. The $1,089 estate plan is a will, an enduring power of attorney and an appointment of a medical treatment decision maker, and the estate planning service page says an advance care directive is estimated in writing. The directives page ('normally as part of a complete estate plan', 'One conversation puts all three in place') and the attorney page ('these documents complete a Victorian estate plan') both imply the directive is included. On the hub, wills-and-estates.html, which was outside this review, the sentence 'Each document is a fixed fee of $495 per person' comes straight after a sentence about the advance care directive, so it reads as though the directive costs $495. Spencer could decide whether to give the directive a fixed price; until then, all three pages should say the same thing.
- Fees and costs. Four pages link to /fees#wills-and-estates. Linking each price to its own anchor would be better: #fee-letters-of-administration, #fee-single-will, #fee-estate-plan-for-one-person, #fee-enduring-power-of-attorney, #fee-appointment-of-a-medical-treatment-decision-maker and #fee-probate-application. The three dispute pages, contesting a will, executor not distributing and fraudulent calumny, never say how the firm charges. The will disputes service page already has the sentence they need: a dispute has no fixed fee, and a written fee estimate is set out in a costs agreement before any substantive work begins. Link /fees#when-a-fixed-fee-does-not-fit with it.
- Headings that answer. The newer pages use question headings that a search result can quote on their own. The older ones use labels ('Medical decisions are separate', 'Safeguards against misuse', 'Keeping it current', 'Defending an estate') or vague pronouns ('How do you make one in Victoria?', 'When should you put one in place?', 'How long do I have?'). Name the subject in every question heading, and update the 'In this article' list in the same edit.
- Common questions sections. Five pages head this section 'Common questions'. The attorney page uses 'Answers to two common questions' and the executor duties page 'Two questions executors ask first', and both put the questions before the closing service section. Standardise on 'Common questions' after 'How we help'. Every FAQ question or answer proposed above must be changed in the FAQPage schema in the same commit, because the gate checks that the schema matches the visible text.
- Currency lines. Five of the seven pages say 'as at September 2026'; the attorney page says October and the beneficiary page November. The ledger re-verified the contesting page on 3 Nov and the executor duties page on 27 Oct without changing them, and by design the gate moves the line only with dateModified. Any edit made from this audit therefore moves the currency line, dateModified, article:modified_time, the updated date and the sitemap lastmod to November 2026 together. Time sensitive statements, such as 'still to come' on the fraudulent calumny page, should be re-checked in the same pass.
- What the checker misses. check_style.py passes six of the seven pages. The only hard failure is the bracketed case citation, and the bullet notes come from the contents lists and the rail card, not from the article prose. Most of the real problems are invisible to it: sayings, staged openings ('What matters is that', 'It is worth being precise', 'The practical rule:'), verbless calls, the slash in a quoted myth, and promised outcomes. Two cheap fixes would help: widen its staged opening pattern, which only catches 'What matters here, now or most is', and add slashes to its checks.
- Rail card. Inside main on every article, the rail card says 'Your situation will have details that change the answer, and a short call sorts them out.' That reads as though the free call will resolve the reader's legal question, which runs up against Spencer's open item on the free call not being free advice. Because the rail card is copied from index.html to every page, any change goes through index.html and the chrome propagation.
- No other breaches. None of the seven pages mentions AI, calls Spencer or the firm a specialist, or states a review count. All of them speak of the firm's lawyers in the plural, and the only service promise added beyond the three is the 'quickly' on the attorney page.
- Working files, read only, nothing in the repository was changed: the extracted main text of each page, a body-only extract, the checker log including runs on every proposed replacement, which all pass, and version 130 of the Administration and Probate Act read for the intestacy election point are in /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-spencer-alexander-site/b1d9c322-8bfd-59b9-b3d7-5cacca158acd/scratchpad/audit/content/wills-a/, with the Act text at /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-spencer-alexander-site/b1d9c322-8bfd-59b9-b3d7-5cacca158acd/scratchpad/audit/content/wills-a/legis/dl/apa130.txt.
- Register: the four older pages, published April to July 2026, use the clipped, aphoristic register that the voice skill lists as machine made. That means verbless fragments, colon reveals such as 'The golden rule:', 'The constraints:' and 'The obligations are real:', 'not X; it is Y' turns, semicolon chains, metaphors such as 'negotiating physics', 'legal chassis', 'toolkit' and 'buying a countdown', and closing sayings such as 'It only ever matters on one day', 'Pressure that produces payment beats process that produces a dividend', 'insurance priced far below the risks it retires' and 'Debts are recovered by systems, not moods'. The October partner disputes article already meets the standard. One rewrite pass over the other four to its register would lift them more than line edits.
- Fee statements that contradict fees.html: business structures says 'Other defined work is quoted as a fixed fee before we start', and contracts says 'fixed fees quoted in writing first' and 'We draft them at fixed fees, quoted first'. fees.html, the commercial hub and the rail card on those same pages say that work the fees page does not price is estimated in writing. The business structures line was recorded for Spencer on 20 Oct 2026 and is still live. insight-shareholder-agreements.html carries a milder 'fixed fee, quoted first' for work that does have a published price.
- Published prices are missing where the reader decides: the shareholders agreement from $2,420 on business structures and contracts; the tenant lease review from $1,089, and the commercial leases service page, in the contracts lease section; and the $495 letter of demand in the debt article's own letter of demand section rather than near the end. All five pages link /fees#commercial-law. The anchors fees.html already carries, #fee-company-set-up, #fee-shareholders-agreement, #fee-terms-of-trade, #fee-review-of-a-contract-to-buy-a-business, #fee-letter-of-demand and #fee-lease-review-for-a-tenant, would take the reader straight to the price.
- Lead closers that signpost instead of answering: 'Here is how they compare, and how we help.', 'Here is the list, and where to start.' and 'We explain each step and its honest economics.' Each spends part of the 70 word lead, which is also the speakable text, on nothing.
- FAQ sections: some answers do not answer their question first. On contracts that is the guarantee answer and 'Do I need a shareholder agreement...?'; on debt, 'Do I wait?'; on structures, 'Trust or company for my family business?', which is itself a fragment. On the buying page, three of four answers repeat the body almost word for word. Every change to a visible question or answer must be made identically in the FAQPage JSON-LD in the head, which the gate requires to match.
- Numbers of 10 and above are written in words, against Spencer's number rule and the same pages' own '21 days': 'ninety-day', 'two hundred days', 'eighteen months', 'fourteen', 'twenty-one', 'ninety cents' and 'one hundred cents'.
- Some legal statements are more complete on sibling pages than in the articles. The commercial hub and faq.html tell a company served with a statutory demand that its application must be supported by an affidavit filed and served within the 21 days, which the debt article omits. The business sale service page gives the section 52 timing as before signing or paying a deposit and notes the liquor licence exclusion, which the buying article omits. Once Spencer settles each point, the article, hub, FAQ and service page should all say the same thing.
- Sources lines omit Acts that the text relies on. Business structures states tax rules but names only the Partnership Act and the Corporations Act. Buying a business relies on the Personal Property Securities Act, the Liquor Control Reform Act and the Fair Work Act without naming them. The ledger's verifications read only the Acts listed, and the gate cannot see the gap.
- The social image alt text describes the wrong photograph on two pages: business structures, 'Two founders working side by side at laptops in a bright office', and buying a business, 'Two people shaking hands across a table in an office'. The in-page alt text on both is right; the og:image:alt in the head was not updated when the photographs changed.
- There are two versions of the commercial rail card. Seven commercial articles carry 'The structures, contracts and deals that build a business, and practical help when a dispute arises.' Partner disputes, safe harbour and terms and conditions carry the generic 'handled with the same care from the first call to the last'. Use the concrete version everywhere.
- Internal links: the 26 Oct 2026 terms and conditions article has no in-body link from any article, although it links to both the contracts and debt articles. The contracts terms of trade section and the debt article's paperwork section are its natural parents. The partner disputes article is not linked from the contracts co-owners section, which is where a reader in that position lands.
- Some closing headings are not questions, unlike every other H2 on these pages: 'Quick answers for founders', 'How we help', 'Common questions', 'Two questions creditors ask' and 'A worked example'. This is lower priority, but question headings keep the answer first structure consistent.
- The author card on every article contains a verbless fragment: 'Admitted as a lawyer in 2018 and a member of the Law Institute of Victoria.' A version with the same facts in Spencer's register: 'Spencer Alexander is the principal of Spencer Alexander Lawyers and has more than ten years of legal experience. He was admitted as a lawyer in 2018, is a member of the Law Institute of Victoria, and holds a Bachelor of Laws with Honours from Monash University and a Master of Laws from the University of Sydney.' It is boilerplate, so changing it moves no page dates.
- Structures, buying, contracts and debt use bold lead-ins on bullets and bold terms inside paragraphs, a pattern the voice skill lists as machine made. The partner disputes article uses none and reads better for it.
- Publishing mechanics for any approved change: a change to what a page says moves dateModified, article:modified_time, the visible updated date, the currency line month and the sitemap lastmod together, which the gate enforces. Changed FAQ text moves the FAQPage schema with it. New or changed legal wording is read in the authorised version first. The 'as at September 2026' currency lines on four pages are not stale in themselves; they will move with the first approved edit.
- Checker and working files: check_style.py passes four of the five pages and fails the buying page only on a false positive, the brackets in an official regulation title. It does not catch the register problems above; the voice skill's supplementary pattern search and a full read did. The main text extracts, the checker inputs and every proposed passage, all style checked clean, are in /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-spencer-alexander-site/b1d9c322-8bfd-59b9-b3d7-5cacca158acd/scratchpad/audit/content/commercial-a/. The Corporations Act sections cited in the findings, 197, 459E, 459G and 467(4), were read today in compilation 149.
- The voice gap follows the publication date. The two oldest pages, shareholder agreements (11 May 2026) and retail leases (1 Jun 2026), are written in an earlier clipped house style. They use colon reveals, bold run-in labels, slogan headings, metaphors and superlatives no one can prove, such as 'the cheapest insurance in commercial law' and 'the single highest-return legal document in small business'. Directors' duties, safe harbour and the terms of trade guide are close to Spencer's voice. The two older pages are better rewritten whole in his voice than patched, keeping word for word every legal sentence the ledger verified (retail leases on 3 Nov 2026, shareholder agreements on 6 Oct 2026).
- The 'is where it begins' saying keeps coming back. Directors' duties uses it twice ('is usually where the exposure begins', 'is where disputes ... tend to start'), retail leases once ('is where surprise invoices live'), terms of trade once ('This is where the gap ... costs the most') and safe harbour once ('is what keeps the options open'). Spencer's voice allows at most one such line in an article. Search for 'is where' and 'is what' before publishing.
- Fee links are inconsistent. Terms of trade links its price to the exact fee row, /fees#fee-terms-of-trade, but retail leases and shareholder agreements link only /fees#commercial-law. Link each quoted price to its own row: /fees#fee-lease-review-for-a-tenant and /fees#fee-shareholders-agreement. Where a reader needs it to know whether a 'from' price applies, state the main assumption behind it. Name the matching service page, /service-commercial-leases, beside the lease review price.
- Two cross references do not deliver what they promise. Directors' duties sends readers to the buying a business guide for personal guarantees, but that guide never mentions them. Retail leases labels a link 'guarantees and debt exposure', but it lands on the creditor's debt recovery guide. Check that each link in a page's body goes to a page that covers what the link's words promise.
- Overlap. Directors' duties and safe harbour define insolvent trading and insolvency in the same operative wording, and both carry the same clause on the director's burden to prove safe harbour with records. Let the directors' page state the rule and its conditions and send readers to the safe harbour guide for the rest. The terms of trade guide overlaps the contracts guide on unfair terms and on when terms are accepted, but it goes well beyond it, which is acceptable. Shareholder agreements and business partner disputes divide cleanly into drafting and dispute.
- Statutory demands. The commercial hub's urgent strip, faq.html and the debt recovery guide all give the 21 day deadline. The safe harbour guide does not, yet a director with a statutory demand is among its most likely readers. Reuse the hub's wording and link /faq#q-i-have-received-a-statutory-demand-what-should-i-do.
- Rail card text differs. Safe harbour, terms of trade and business partner disputes describe Commercial Law as 'Contracts, business sales, disputes and debt recovery, handled with the same care from the first call to the last.' The other seven commercial articles, including directors' duties, retail leases and shareholder agreements, use 'The structures, contracts and deals that build a business, and practical help when a dispute arises.' Use the majority line on all ten; the minority version ends in a generic tail.
- Use one name for one thing. fees.html and the hub's price line say 'shareholders agreement', while the article, its title and the hub's service heading say 'shareholder agreement'.
- Every FAQ answer is mirrored in the page's FAQPage schema. Each FAQ change proposed here, on directors' duties, retail leases and shareholder agreements, must also be made in the schema, or the gate's FAQ check fails.
- Currency lines. Four pages still say 'as at September 2026'. They include retail leases, re-verified on 3 Nov 2026, and shareholder agreements, re-verified on 6 Oct 2026, whose dates were left alone because nothing changed. When the edits recommended here are made, move these together: the currency line, dateModified, the visible updated date, article:modified_time and the sitemap lastmod. That also shows readers a current date.
- Leads are close to the gate's cap of 70 words. The directors' duties and safe harbour leads are at 70 words and the other three at 68, so count every lead rewrite. The proposed leads here count 70 for directors' duties, 70 for retail leases, 68 for shareholder agreements and 70 for terms of trade.
- Number style. 'Sixty hours' on shareholder agreements and 'twelve months' on retail leases should be figures under the house style for 10 and above, matching '12 months' on the commercial leases service page.
- Persuasion should rest on specifics. The How we help sections on directors' duties and safe harbour are generic. The pages that convert best name what the firm does, the price and its scope, and the deadline, as the terms of trade guide does. Every proposal here keeps the firm's plural voice and adds no service promise beyond the three the site allows.
- For the accuracy routine, not a writing fix. The Federal Register lists the Competition and Consumer Amendment (Unfair Trading Practices) Act 2026 as amending the Competition and Consumer Act from 1 July 2027. Re-read the terms of trade guide against it when it commences.
- Read in the authorised versions during this audit, for the legal queries above: Australian Consumer Law sections 23(3) and 64A (Competition and Consumer Act compilation 167, C2026C00405); Corporations Act sections 588FL and 1317J(1) (compilation in force from 19 Sep 2026); Personal Property Securities Act section 62(2) (latest compilation, 14 Oct 2024); Retail Leases Act section 47 (version 027).
- The style checker passes all five pages with no hard failures. Its only notes are 'the bottom line', 'leverage' and a 61 word sentence on retail leases, a 69 word sentence on safe harbour, and 'expert' on shareholder agreements, which refers to a valuer and not the firm. All proposed wording above also passes check_style.py. The extracted texts and checker inputs are in /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-spencer-alexander-site/b1d9c322-8bfd-59b9-b3d7-5cacca158acd/scratchpad/audit/content/commercial-b/ and nothing in the repository was changed.
- Time sensitive: two faq.html answers say that misidentification and the minimum respondent age of 12 'start on a day to be proclaimed and no later than 10 November 2026'. I read section 2(3) and (4) of the as made Act No. 1 of 2026 on legislation.vic.gov.au on 9 Nov 2026, and they confirm 10 November 2026 as the latest commencement date. Both answers, the question heading and the FAQPage schema need the accuracy routine's check and a change of tense on or after 10 Nov 2026.
- Call bands repeat one another instead of using the approved wording. resources.html and insights.html carry the same lead word for word: '... can only take you so far. One call tells you where you actually stand, your options, the time limits, and what the next step will cost.' The FAQ band is a variant of it. Each resource page says 'one call tells you where you stand and what to do next' twice, in the rail card and in the closing band, and makes the same point a third time in 'Not sure where to start?'. None of these bands uses 'Your first call is free, and you speak with a lawyer'. Build them on that line.
- 'Actually', 'actual', 'really', 'genuinely' and 'simply' do no work in at least a dozen places: 'Checklists you can actually use', 'where you actually stand' twice, 'must actually do', 'actually work', 'prenups that actually hold', 'how deals really work', 'as it is actually done', 'genuinely take longer', 'your actual options', 'actually see it again', 'actually needs' and 'It simply means'. Delete them.
- No page in this set links any of the eight service pages, and prices are rarely named or deep linked although fees.html gives every fee its own id, for example fee-probate-application, fee-single-will and fee-consent-orders-for-an-agreed-property-settlement. The executor checklist names $1,815 without a link, the separation checklist names no price, and the FAQ names prices in 2 of its 53 answers.
- Time limits are stated without a call prompt beside them: the FAQ answers on property time limits, family provision claims, adult children and de facto couples, and section 6 of the separation checklist. The statutory demand answer is the model, because it gives a tappable number straight after the 21 days. It is also the only one of the 53 FAQ answers with a tappable number. The number in the enforcement answer is plain text and cannot be tapped.
- Numbers of 10 and above are written in words, against the house rule and the rest of the site. 'Twelve months' appears in the FAQ and the separation checklist, while the home and fees pages say 12 months. The separation checklist says 'thirty days' under an H1 that says 30 days. The FAQ has 'six to twelve months', and it has 'fifty fifty' in one answer and '50/50' in another question on the same page.
- Serial commas before 'and' recur, although Spencer leaves them out: 'what your options are, and what the next step will cost', 'the time limits, and what the next step will cost', 'what needs attention now, and what can wait' and 'likely to outlive you, and willing'.
- Currency lines read months old. The separation checklist says 'as at August 2026', although the accuracy routine verified it unchanged on 13 Oct 2026, and the executor checklist says 'as at September 2026'. Under the current rule the currency line moves only when the substance changes. A decision for Spencer: whether a verification that finds no change should also move the currency month, dateModified and lastmod, so that a reader in November is not shown August.
- Every faq.html wording change must also be made in its FAQPage schema, because the gate compares them. The Last reviewed date, the FAQPage dateModified, the sitemap lastmod and the llms.txt Last updated line must move together.
- Contact preference: thank-you.html and contact.html both say a lawyer replies 'by phone if you left a number, otherwise by email'. That ignores the safe_to_contact answer the form now asks for. Change both together, and only once Spencer confirms that replies follow that answer.
- The truncated insights card excerpt for contesting a will, 'Left out of a will or treated unfairly?', is also in feed.xml and llms.txt, so the fix should go into all three.
- Article headlines shown on insights.html use filler and stock framing: 'prenups that actually hold', 'the document that saves business partnerships', 'what every business owner must know' and 'the complete checklist'. They belong to the article pages, so they are for the article reviewers. Any change touches the article, the feed, llms.txt, the About archive and the hub lists together.
- Both resource pages carry the subscribe line 'One reply unsubscribes you any time. Details are handled per our privacy policy.', which reads clipped and legalistic. 'Reply at any time to unsubscribe, and we handle your details as our privacy policy describes.' keeps the meaning. privacy.html's 'we stop immediately when you do' should be confirmed as the actual practice.
- Optional: the author card on both resources, and on every article, is three fragments ('Admitted as a lawyer in 2018 and a member of the Law Institute of Victoria. Bachelor of Laws with Honours, Monash University, and Master of Laws, University of Sydney.'). Full sentences carrying the same approved facts would read better. It is boilerplate across the site, so check the gate's author card check before changing it.
- Style checker results: the only hard failure in this set is privacy.html's 'expert', a false positive that a small reword clears. The resources '!' mark and the one sentence card excerpts that trigger the paragraph shape notes are structural and need no change. Every proposal above passes check_style.py.
- All eight pages already refer to the firm's lawyers in the plural. No proposal here adds a service promise beyond the free first call, the one business day reply and after hours contact, and none mentions AI. The urgent wording reuses the existing after hours line.
- Prices are missing from the first screen. Each hub's hero line gives one linked price, such as 'Joint divorce application $1,089, fixed fee', but among the service pages only commercial leases states a figure there. The proposed hero lines are each linked to the fee's own anchor on fees.html. Divorce: 'Joint divorce application $1,089, sole $1,452, fixed fees', to /fees#fee-divorce-application-joint. Property settlement: 'Consent orders for an agreed settlement $3,025, fixed fee', to /fees#fee-consent-orders-for-an-agreed-property-settlement. Parenting: 'Consent orders for agreed arrangements $3,025, fixed fee', to /fees#fee-consent-orders-for-agreed-parenting-arrangements. Wills: 'Estate plan for one person $1,089, fixed fee', to /fees#fee-estate-plan-for-one-person. Probate: 'Straightforward probate application $1,815, fixed fee', to /fees#fee-probate-application. Commercial leases: keep the words and link them to /fees#fee-lease-review-for-a-tenant. Business sale: 'Contract review for buyers from $1,210', to /fees#fee-review-of-a-contract-to-buy-a-business. The will disputes page has no fixed fee and keeps its line.
- Every fee note and cost answer links the top of the fees page. fees.html gives each fee its own id, so point each page's fees page link at the same anchors listed above, and the reader lands on the price with its scope and assumptions.
- No service page links another in its body, although each tells the reader they will need a sibling service: divorce to property settlement and wills, property settlement to divorce, probate to will disputes, and commercial leases and business sale to each other. The page findings name the exact words to link, with no words changed.
- Only the divorce page closes its time limits section with a prompt to call. Property settlement, probate, will disputes, commercial leases and business sale state their deadlines and stop, and parenting has neither a time limits section nor an urgent strip. The page findings give a closing line for each in the divorce page's form, ending 'call us now'. CLAUDE.md already requires a call prompt after the time limit section of every article; adding the same rule to playbooks/service-pages.md would keep it on these pages.
- The rail card on all eight pages reads 'Tell us what has happened and we will tell you where you stand', and the closing lines on property settlement, parenting and will disputes use the same phrase. DECISIONS.md of 28 September 2026 left it for Spencer to keep or change against his rule that the free first call is not free advice, and on the wills page it does not fit a client planning ahead, for whom nothing has happened. If he changes it, the commercial leases closing line is the model, because it says what to have ready and what we will explain. For the rail card, that would read 'Tell us what you need, and we will explain how we can help and what it will cost.' The card is copied from each hub, so it changes there and every service page is rebuilt.
- The fee is often phrased as though it were the service: 'An estate plan for one person is a fixed fee of $1,089', 'A straightforward probate application is a fixed fee of $1,815' and 'Consent orders for agreed parenting arrangements are a fixed fee of $3,025', and the hubs use the same form. The divorce and property pages and the probate cost answer already say 'Our fixed fee for' a service 'is', which is how the firm would say it; use that form throughout and keep every figure.
- The what to bring section has five different headings across the eight pages, among them 'What to bring to the first call' and 'What to bring to a first appointment', and on the property settlement page the heading contradicts its own eyebrow, 'Your first call'. The free first contact is a call, so use the commercial leases heading, 'What to have with you when you call', on every page with the eyebrow 'Your first call'; the wills page's eyebrow, 'Your first appointment', changes with it.
- Repetition inside a page is the commonest voice fault. The free first call line appears four times on the parenting page and the seller's release from the lease four times on the business sale page. The commercial leases page says 'The best time to call is before you sign' twice, and the wills page explains the three estate planning documents twice on one screen. Each page's findings remove the duplicate.
- Every page closes with 'This page reflects the law applying in Victoria as at September 2026'. The accuracy ledger shows none of the eight verified since publication on 28 September 2026. The accuracy routine has since confirmed most of the Acts they rely on unchanged: the Family Law Act at compilation 101 and the Powers of Attorney Act at version 007 on 27 October, and the Administration and Probate Act at version 130 and the Retail Leases Act at version 027 on 3 November. Verify the eight next and move each currency line, its dateModified and its sitemap lastmod to the month of verification; the month should not move without a verification behind it.
- Four pages fail the style checker, and only on their Sources lines. The brackets it fails are inside the official titles of two sets of court rules and two sets of fee regulations, the family law rules and fees regulations and the Supreme Court's probate rules and fees regulations, and the checker exempts a bracketed word only where it comes before Act. The voice skill keeps brackets that are part of an official title, so these are not faults in the writing. No page's prose fails, and no page uses a dash, a specialist claim or any mention of AI.
- Two address lines leave out Box Hill, on the wills and will disputes pages, so 1 Main Street stands without a suburb; every other page includes it.
- Every change here goes into the content files in playbooks/service-content and is rebuilt with scripts/build-service-page.py, never hand edited, and each page's FAQ schema then follows the visible text. Two answers proposed for change, the divorce cost answer and the move away answer on the parenting page, are shared with faq.html and should change there in the same commit. The urgent strip proposed for the parenting page and the wills cost question change how those pages look or what they answer, so they go to Spencer as recommendations first.
